This section unfolds the remaining tangled, unsolved fragments of the Moonstone's baffling theft, told through a sequential chain of first-hand witness accounts that each hold a critical piece of the puzzle no prior narrator was positioned to see:
1. Fourth Narrative
2. Fifth Narrative (split across Sections I, II, III, IV, V)
3. Sixth Narrative
4. Seventh Narrative
5. Eighth Narrative
Every lingering loose end of the decades-long mystery is at last drawn to its final resolution in the concluding three-part Epilogue, which reveals the full unvarnished truth of the famous cursed diamond's ultimate fate.

I scribe these lines from a drafty military encampment in India, for my kin back in England. My sole purpose here is to lay bare the reason I have refused to extend the hand of friendship to our cousin, John Herncastle. The quiet distance I have kept between us these months has been misread by members of this family whose good opinion I will not willingly forfeit. I beg you all to hold judgement until you have read every word of this account. On my honor as an officer in His Majesty’s service, every detail I set down here is strictly, unvarnished truth.

This rift between Herncastle and I did not spark from some petty family feud. It was born in the blood and smoke of a public event we both bore witness to: the storming of Seringapatam, under General Baird, on the 4th of May, 1799. To make the full context clear, I must first step back to the days before the assault, and the wild, half-fantastic tales rippling through our camp of the hoard of jewels and gold locked in the Sultan’s palace vaults.

---

One of the most outlandish of those stories centered on a Yellow Diamond, a gem famous enough to have carved its own place in native Indian lore. The oldest surviving traditions say the stone was set in the forehead of the four-handed Hindu god that embodies the Moon. Partly for its unique pale golden hue, partly for the long-held superstition that it drank power from the deity it adorned, waxing and waning in brightness in lockstep with the lunar cycle, it earned the name that sticks to it across India to this day: *the Moonstone*. I have heard a similar old folk belief cropped up in ancient Greece and Rome, though it attached to a lesser, translucent lunar stone, not a sacred diamond dedicated to a god.

The recorded adventures of the Moonstone stretch back to the 11th century. That was when the Mohammedan conqueror Mahmoud of Ghizni swept across India, sacked the holy pilgrimage city of Somnauth, and stripped the centuries-old temple there of every treasure the priests could not hide. Of all the temple’s statues, only the moon-god escaped the raiders’ fury. Three Brahmin priests spirited the unviolated icon, its forehead still set with the Moonstone, away under cover of night, and carried it hundreds of miles to Benares, India’s second sacred city.

They housed the god in a new shrine: a hall inlaid with rare gems, beneath a roof propped up by solid gold pillars. The night the shrine was formally consecrated, Vishnu the Preserver appeared to the three Brahmins in a dream. He breathed divine power directly onto the diamond embedded in the god’s forehead, and the priests fell to their knees, covering their faces in their robes. Vishnu gave them a command: from that hour forward, three priests would take turns watching the Moonstone night and day, for every generation that followed. He also spoke a prophecy of ruin: any mortal fool enough to lay violent hands on the sacred gem, and every heir who received it after him, would meet with unmitigated disaster. The Brahmins ordered that warning carved in gold lettering across the shrine’s main gates.

Centuries passed. Generation after generation, the successors of those three original Brahmins kept their unbroken vigil over the Moonstone. Then, in the early 1700s, the Mughal Emperor Aurungzebe ordered his armies to raze every Hindu temple in the land. The moon-god’s shrine was defiled, its sacred animals slaughtered, its statues smashed to splinters, and a high-ranking officer in Aurungzebe’s army seized the Moonstone for himself.

Too weak to recover their sacred gem by force, the three guardian priests followed the thief in disguise. The generations turned over: the officer who had committed the sacrilege died a horrific, miserable death, the Moonstone passed from one lawless, greedy Mohammedan hand to the next, but the line of guardian priests never let it slip out of their sight, waiting for the day Vishnu’s will would return the gem to its rightful resting place.

By the end of the 18th century, the diamond had come into the possession of Tippoo, Sultan of Seringapatam. He ordered it set as the pommel of his favorite personal dagger, and locked it away with his most prized treasures in the palace armoury. Even there, hidden in the Sultan’s own private quarters, the three guardian priests kept their secret watch. Three unknown officers in Tippoo’s household, men who had earned his full trust by converting (or pretending to convert) to the Muslim faith, were widely rumored to be the disguised Brahmin guardians.

---

That was the strange, fanciful tale of the Moonstone that circulated through our camp in the weeks before the attack. None of us treated it as anything more than a campfire story—none, that is, except my cousin Herncastle, whose obsession with all things supernatural made him swallow the legend whole. The night before the assault, he flew into a absurd, unprovoked rage at me and the rest of our mess for dismissing the story as fiction. A petty argument blew up, and Herncastle’s terrible temper got the better of him. He blustered, loud enough for half the tent to hear, that the second the English army took Seringapatam, that diamond would be on his finger. The whole mess dissolved in roars of laughter, and we all assumed the matter was done for good.

I now skip forward to the day of the attack. Herncastle and I were separated within minutes of the first cannon fire. I did not see him when we waded through the Cauvery river, when we planted the Union Jack in the first breach in the walls, when we fought inch by inch across the ditch beyond and spilled into the town. It was not until dusk, after the fort was fully ours, and General Baird himself had found Tippoo’s corpse buried under a heap of slain soldiers, that we crossed paths again.

We had both been assigned to parties dispatched by the General to stamp out the looting and chaos that followed the conquest. The camp followers had committed unforgivable atrocities, and worse, groups of soldiers had found an unguarded side door to the palace treasury, and were shoving handfuls of gold and jewels into their uniform pockets. It was in the courtyard just outside that treasury that Herncastle and I met, both struggling to rein our own men back into line. I could see plain as day the slaughter he had waded through had driven his fiery temper to the edge of frenzy. In my judgement, he was in no fit state to be given disciplinary duty.

Riot reigned in the treasury, but I saw no outright acts of violence—at first. The men disgraced themselves with rough, giddy jokes, passing barbs back and forth, until the old story of the Moonstone popped up again as a running taunt. “Who’s got the Moonstone?” was the rallying cry that sent groups of looters darting off to hunt the gem the second we had herded them out of one corner of the palace.

I was still fruitlessly trying to restore order when I heard a guttural, terrified scream ring out from the far side of the courtyard. I ran straight toward the noise, certain a new, far deadlier riot had broken loose. I rounded the corner to find two Indians, clearly high-ranking palace officers from their fine silk uniforms, lying dead across the threshold of an open armoury door. A cry from deep inside the room sent me rushing forward. Inside, a third Indian, mortally wounded, was sinking to the floor at the feet of a man whose back was turned to me. The moment I stepped over the sill, the man spun around: it was John Herncastle, a flaming torch in one hand, a dagger dripping warm blood in the other. The large yellow stone set in the dagger’s hilt flared in the torchlight like a spark of living fire.

The dying Indian crumpled to his knees, pointed straight at the dagger in Herncastle’s hand, and gasped out a few words in his native tongue: “The Moonstone will have its vengeance yet on you and yours.” He fell forward, dead, before the last syllable left his mouth.

Before I could speak a single word, the group of soldiers who had followed me across the courtyard crowded into the doorway. Herncastle charged at them like a madman, swinging his torch and the bloody dagger. “Clear the room!” he roared at me. “Post a guard on this door, no one comes in!” The men scattered back out of his path. I set two sentries from my own company, men I knew I could trust, to stand watch over the door. I did not see Herncastle again for the rest of the night.

At dawn the next day, with looting still spreading across the palace, General Baird issued a public proclamation by beat of drum: any man caught red-handed stealing would be hanged immediately, no matter his rank. The provost-marshal stood right at the drum’s side, to make it clear the General’s threat was no empty one. It was in the crowd gathered to hear the proclamation that Herncastle and I met again. He held out his hand to me as if nothing at all had happened. “Good morning,” he said, casual as ever.

I did not take his hand. “Tell me first,” I said, “how that dying Indian in the armoury met his death, and what those last words of his meant, when he pointed at that dagger in your hand.”

“The man died of his wound, I suppose,” Herncastle said, blank-faced. “I have no more idea what his ravings meant than you do.”

I studied him closely. The wild frenzy of the night before had burned completely away, leaving him cold and calm. I gave him one last chance to tell the truth. “Is that all you have to say for yourself?” I asked.

“That is all,” he said.

I turned my back on him, and we have not exchanged a single word since.

---

I want it clearly understood: this account of my cousin’s actions, unless some dire circumstance forces me to make it public, is for the eyes of our family only. Herncastle has given me no excuse to report this incident to our commanding officer. His fellow officers have taunted him more than once about the diamond, referencing his loud brag the night before the assault, but the memory of what I saw him do in that armoury has been more than enough to keep him from ever mentioning the gem again. Word says he intends to exchange into a different regiment, openly to put distance between himself and me.

Whether that rumor is true or not, I cannot bring myself to formally accuse him. I have no hard evidence, only moral certainty. I cannot prove he killed the two men lying dead across the armoury threshold. I cannot even swear my own two eyes saw him strike the fatal blow to the third man, inside. The dying Indian’s curse could be dismissed as the raving of a man delirious with blood loss, and I have no way to contradict that. I leave it to all of our relatives, on both sides of the family, to read this account and judge for themselves if the cold aversion I now hold for John Herncastle is justified, or not.

I do not put any stock in the old, fantastic Hindu legend of the gem’s supernatural curse. But I will admit, before I close these pages, that I hold a superstition of my own. I am convinced unpunished crime carries its own fatal weight. I do not only believe Herncastle is guilty of that triple murder in the armoury. I am even fanciful enough to write here that if he keeps that diamond, he will live to regret the day he ever laid hands on it. And if he ever gives it away, every single person who takes that stolen gem from him will live to regret it too.

---

If you flip to page 129 of the first volume of *Robinson Crusoe*, you will find the line written plain as day: “Now I saw, though too late, the Folly of beginning a Work before we count the Cost, and before we judge rightly of our own Strength to go through with it.”
Only yesterday, I chanced to open my own tattered, well-loved copy of Crusoe right on that exact page. And here I sit, May 21st, 1850, barely twelve hours later, fresh out of a short chat with my lady’s nephew, Mr. Franklin Blake, that has landed me in exactly the predicament that line warns against. The conversation went as follows:
“Betteredge,” says Mr. Franklin, just back from calling on the family lawyer Mr. Bruff, “I’ve been going over old family matters with him, and we got to talking about that missing Indian Diamond—the Moonstone, the one that vanished from my aunt’s Yorkshire house two years back. Both of us are agreed the whole tangled story ought to be set down in writing, in the interests of the truth, and the sooner the better.”
I did not see his drift at first, but I’ve long learned it’s always wisest to side with the lawyer if you want a quiet, peaceful household, so I nodded and said I thought that made perfect sense.
Mr. Franklin went on: “You know as well as I do that innocent folks have already fallen under unfair suspicion over that cursed stone. If we don’t get the facts down on paper now, future generations might go on smearing good names for no reason, with no official record to appeal to. There’s no question this strange, tangled family story of ours needs to be told. And Bruff and I think we’ve hit on exactly the right way to do it.”
I had no quarrel with that, of course—still, I could not for the life of me see what business any of that had to do with me, a humble house steward knocking on seventy years old.
“We’ve got two sets of facts to lay out,” he explained. “First, we need to show how the Moonstone first fell into my uncle Herncastle’s hands back when he was serving in India, fifty years ago. I already have that preface sorted, you see: an old eyewitness account from the family papers, so no extra work is needed there. The next part, the one no one else alive knows half as well as you, is how that diamond turned up at my aunt’s Yorkshire house two years ago, and how it vanished right under our noses in barely twelve hours. You were there the whole time. You saw every little thing that happened. So you’re the one who has to pick up the pen, and start the story off for us.”
That’s how I learned I’d been roped into the job. If you’re curious what I did next, I’ll be plain: I did exactly what you would do in my shoes. I made a big show of protesting that I was far too uneducated and unskilled to tackle such a weighty task—even while, deep down, I was certain I was clever enough to pull it off, if I just gave my wits a fair shot.
Mr. Franklin must’ve read that private little thought right off my face. He would not hear a word of my false modesty, refused to let me weasel out, and insisted I give those supposed wits the chance to prove themselves.
It has been two whole hours since he walked out my door. No sooner had his boots stopped clacking down the drive than I hauled out my writing desk, sharpened a pen, and settled in to start the tale. And I have sat here, useless as a wet match, ever since—staring at my open copy of Crusoe, seeing that line about counting the cost before you start your work stare right back at me.
You will mark that I stumbled on that exact line by pure accident the day before I rashly agreed to take this job on. If that is not a quiet, homemade kind of prophecy, I do not know what is.
Mind you, I am no superstitious fool. I have read my share of books in my time, a self-made scholar in my own humble way. At seventy, my memory is still sharp as a tack, and my legs keep up with it too. Do not go thinking I am some ignorant old man when I say there has never been a book written that holds a candle to *Robinson Crusoe*, and there never will be, not as long as I live. I have tested that book for decades, usually paired with a good pipe of tobacco, and it has never let me down, no matter what mess life throws at me. When my spirits are low? Crusoe. When I need a bit of plain common-sense advice? Crusoe. Back when my dear late wife was nagging my ears off? Crusoe. These days, when I have had a drop too much ale? Crusoe. I have worn out six stout, well-thumbed copies of the book over the years. Last year, for my lady’s birthday, she gave me my seventh—nice blue binding, price four shillings and sixpence, with a full-page illustration of Crusoe on his island for good measure. I celebrated by having a little extra ale, and sure enough, a few pages of Crusoe was all it took to set me right again.
Wait a minute, though—this is all just me rambling on about my favourite book, and I have not written a single word about the missing Moonstone, have I? I am wandering off chasing Lord knows what, Lord knows where. Let us set this rambling sheet aside, grab a fresh new page of paper, tip my cap to you, dear reader, and start the story properly, from the top.

A page or two back, I made mention of my lady. It stands to reason the cursed Moonstone could never have ended up in our house to be lost if it hadn’t been gifted to my lady’s daughter, Miss Rachel—and Miss Rachel never would’ve drawn breath to receive that accursed present if my lady herself hadn’t laboured and brought her into the world. So starting our story with my lady means we’ve gone back far enough to not miss any loose threads, and let me tell you, when you’ve got a messy, tangled job like this mystery on your hands, that’s a real comfort right at the start.

If you know anything of London’s fashionable set, you’ve surely heard tell of the three beautiful Miss Herncastles: Adelaide, Caroline, and Julia—the youngest, and by far the best of the three, in my humble opinion, and I’ve got 50 odd years of acquaintance to back that judgement up. I entered the service of their old father, Lord Herncastle, when I was fifteen, hired on as pageboy to wait on the three young ladies. Thank the Good Lord we’ve no business with that old hothead in the Moonstone affair—he had the sharpest tongue and shortest temper of any man I ever met, high or low. I stayed on in that household till the day Julia married the late Sir John Verinder.

Sir John was an excellent, soft-hearted man who only needed someone sensible to steer him, and between us, he found exactly that in my lady. He thrived under her care, grew plump, happy, and died peacefully, every step from the day she walked down the aisle with him to the day she closed his eyes for the last. Of course I went with the new bride down to Sir John’s country estate. “Sir John,” she told him straight, “I can’t do without Gabriel Betteredge.” “My lady,” he laughed back, “neither can I.” That was how he was with her, and that’s how I came to be settled at the Verinder Park for good. I didn’t care where I went so long as my mistress and I were in the same household.

Seeing how my lady took a great interest in the estate farms and outdoor work, I did too—all the more so because I was the seventh son of a small farmer myself. She got me apprenticed to the head bailiff, I worked hard, pleased everyone, and climbed the ranks fast. Some years later, one Monday my lady said, “Sir John, your bailiff is a dull old fool. Pension him generously, and let Gabriel take his post.” Come Tuesday, Sir John came and found me to say the deed was done. You hear endless tales of miserable, bickering married couples, but these two were the perfect opposite. Let that be a warning to the quarrelsome, and encouragement to anyone thinking of tying the knot.

Well, you’d say I was living in clover then, wouldn’t you? A position of trust and respect, my own little cottage to live in, rounds of the estate to keep me busy in the mornings, accounts to sort through in the afternoons, and my pipe and my tattered, dog-eared copy of *Robinson Crusoe* for the evenings. What more could a man want? Remember what Adam pined for when he was alone in Eden, and if you don’t blame him for it, don’t blame me either.

The woman I set my sights on was Selina Goby, the housekeeper I’d hired to tend my cottage. I agree entirely with the late William Cobbett’s rule for picking a wife: make sure she chews her food slow and plants her feet firm on the ground when she walks, and you’ve no troubles ahead. Selina checked both those boxes, which was one reason to marry her. I had another, entirely my own discovery: as a single housekeeper, she charged me a weekly wage for her board and services. As my wife, she couldn’t charge me a penny. Pure economy, with a little dash of fondness thrown in. I told my mistress the whole plan exactly as I’d thought it out. “I’ve turned Selina Goby over in my mind,” I said, “and it’ll work out far cheaper to marry her than keep her on as hired help.”

My lady burst out laughing so hard she had to sit down, saying she didn’t know whether to be more shocked at my blunt language or my unromantic principles. I didn’t understand the fine joke of it, only that she didn’t forbid the match, so I went straight to Selina and proposed. What did she say? Lord, you don’t know women at all if you even need to ask. Of course she said yes.

A week before the wedding, once people started talking about making me a new suit for the ceremony, the jitters hit me. I’ve talked to plenty of other men in my position, and every last one of them admitted that a week out from the wedding, they secretly wished they could wriggle out of it. I went further: I actually tried to get out of it. Not that I was unfair! The laws of England say a man has to compensate a woman for breaking a marriage bargain, and I followed them to the letter. I offered Selina a brand new feather bed and fifty shillings to call the whole thing off. You’ll hardly believe it, but the silly fool turned me down flat.

After that, there was no getting out. I bought the new suit as cheaply as I could, and got through the rest of the ceremony and married life as frugally as possible. We weren’t a happy couple, and we weren’t a miserable couple. We were six of one and half a dozen of the other. We kept getting in each other’s way, no matter how hard we tried not to: if I wanted to go up the stairs, Selina was coming down, and if she wanted to go down, I was tramping up. That’s married life, as far as my experience goes. After five years of constant stairwell run-ins, a merciful Providence took Selina from me, leaving me with our little girl Penelope and no other children. Not long after that, Sir John died too, leaving my lady with her little daughter Rachel, and no other children. If you know anything of my lady, you won’t be surprised to hear Penelope was raised right under her own eye, sent to school, taught all sorts of useful skills, and promoted to be Miss Rachel’s own lady’s maid the second she was old enough.

I kept on as head bailiff, tramping the estate every day, right up to Christmas 1847, when everything changed. My lady turned up at my cottage one evening to take tea with me alone. She pointed out that counting from the day I started as a 15 year old pageboy for old Lord Herncastle, I’d served her family for more than fifty years, and pressed a beautiful hand-knit wool waistcoat into my hands, stitched all by herself, to keep me warm through the harsh winter. I was half choked up with gratitude, till I realized the waistcoat wasn’t an honour at all—it was a bribe. She’d noticed I was growing old before I’d noticed it myself, and had come to wheedle me into giving up the hard outdoor tramping of the bailiff’s job, to take the soft indoor post of house steward, and take it easy for the rest of my days.

I fought back as hard as I could against the indignity of being “retired” while I still thought I was perfectly fit for the farm rounds. But my lady knew my soft spot: she framed it not as a favour to myself, but as a favour to her. After that, I was done for. I wiped my streaming eyes on that brand new wool waistcoat like a daft old man, and said I’d think on it.

Once she was gone, my mind was all in a tangle, so I turned to the remedy I’ve never known fail me in any emergency: I lit my pipe, and flipped open *Robinson Crusoe*. Before I’d read five pages, I landed on the most comforting line I could have asked for, on page 158: “Today we love, what tomorrow we hate.” The path cleared right up for me. Today, I hated the very thought of giving up my bailiff rounds. Tomorrow, on Crusoe’s authority, I’d be all for the soft steward’s post. I’d just grab tomorrow’s frame of mind when it came, and the whole thing would be settled easy. That night I fell asleep the Verinder’s farm bailiff, and woke up the next morning their new house steward. No fuss, no regrets—all thanks to *Robinson Crusoe*.

Just now, my daughter Penelope peeked over my shoulder to read what I’ve written so far. She says it’s all beautifully told, every word true. But she’s raised a very valid objection: I was asked to tell the story of the Moonstone, and so far I’ve told nothing but the story of myself. Curious, isn’t it? I can’t account for it. I wonder if those fancy gentlemen who make their living writing books ever find their own silly selves getting in the way of their grand important subjects, the way I just did? If they do, I feel for them.

Well, that’s another false start, and a whole lot of good writing paper wasted. There’s nothing for it, far as I can see, except for you lot to keep your tempers, and for me to start all over again for the third time. This time, I swear, I’ll get to the Diamond.

I’ve spent two whole days puzzling over how to kick this story off proper. First I tried scratching my head till my scalp stung — got nothing out of that but dandruff. Then I fetched my daughter Penelope in to give me a hand, and that’s where we hit on a plan.

Penelope swears the trick to a true story is writing it day by day, pinned hard to exact dates, starting the very day we got word Mr. Franklin Blake was coming to stay. Once you force your memory to tie itself to a calendar, it drags up more odds and ends than a retriever rooting through a bramble bush. The only trouble is digging up those dates in the first place — which Penelope says she’ll handle, thanks to the diary she’s kept ever since her schoolmistress made the whole class write one, decades back.

When I suggested, brilliant as you like, that she just tell the whole story out of her diary herself, she turned bright red, looked at me like I’d suggested she run naked through the village, and snapped that not a living soul ever gets to read a line of her private journal. “Fiddlesticks!” she huffed, when I asked what on earth she could possibly be hiding in there. I said, “Ah — sweethearts, is it?”

So we’re sticking to the plan, and I open on Wednesday morning, the 24th of May, 1848, when Lady Verinder called me into her private sitting room.

“Gabriel,” she said, “I’ve news that’ll surprise you. Franklin Blake is back from abroad. He’s been staying with his father in London, and he’ll be here tomorrow to visit, right up till next month — for Rachel’s birthday.”

If I’d been holding a hat in my hand, nothing but 30 years of loyal service would’ve stopped me flinging it clean up to the ceiling. I hadn’t seen Mr. Franklin since he was a scruffy little boy running wild in this very house. Far as I remembered, he was the nicest lad that ever spun a top till it hummed, or broke a drawing room window chasing a stray cricket ball. Miss Rachel, who was leaning against the window sill while I said that, sniffed loud enough to rattle the teacups. *She* remembered him as the worst tyrant that ever lived, who tortured her best doll by pulling all its hair out, and dragged her around the garden till her little legs gave out harnessed to a string like a cart pony. “I still burn with indignation and ache with fatigue just thinking about it,” she declared.

You’ll be wondering, of course, how it came to pass that Mr. Franklin spent all the years between boyhood and manhood roving abroad, never coming home. The short of it is this: his father, Lady Verinder’s eldest sister’s husband, was the famous Mr. Blake — equally famous for his mountains of money, and his lifelong obsessive lawsuit to prove he was the rightful heir to a dukedom he’d never seen. For decades he badgered every court in the land, lined a hundred lawyers’ pockets thick as your thumb, and set half the gentry of England at each other’s throats arguing whether his claim was valid or not. His wife died, two of his three children died, before the courts finally told him to get out and stop wasting their time. When it was all over, and the sitting duke kept his title, Mr. Blake decided the only way to get even with his country was to deny it the honour of raising his only surviving son. “Why would I trust my nation’s institutions,” he raged, “after the way they’ve treated me?” Add to that the fact that he hated all boys, his own included, and you won’t be surprised that the end result was nine-year-old Franklin being shipped off to boarding schools in Germany, while his father stayed nice and warm in England, making speeches in Parliament and writing an unfinished pamphlet railing against the duke that’s still gathering dust somewhere to this day.

Thank God that bit’s done with. We don’t need to waste another thought on old Mr. Blake and his dukedom. Leave him to it. Our business is the diamond — and the diamond leads us straight back to Mr. Franklin, the innocent soul who brought that accursed gem through our front door.

He never forgot us after he left, that lad. He sent letters every now and then, sometimes to Lady Verinder, sometimes to Miss Rachel, sometimes to me. We had an arrangement before he sailed, you see: he borrowed a ball of twine from me, my four-blade pocket knife, and seven shillings and sixpence in cash. I never saw that money again, and I never expected to. His letters to me were mostly cheerful little notes asking to borrow more. Lady Verinder kept me up to date on how he grew: after Germany, he studied in France, then Italy, and the three countries together turned him into a sort of universal jack-of-all-trades. He wrote a little, painted a little, sang and played the piano and composed a little — no doubt borrowing supplies and money for all of it, same as he borrowed from me. When he turned 21, his mother’s fortune of seven hundred a year fell into his hands, and ran through it like water through a sieve. There was a hole in that man’s pocket no tailor on earth could’ve sewn shut. But everyone loved him, wherever he went, for his easy, bright way of talking. He called his address “Post Office, Europe — left till called for.” Twice he booked passage back to England to visit us, and twice some unmentionable woman distracted him and stopped him coming. His third attempt worked, as you already heard. On Thursday the 25th of May, we’d finally get to see what that scruffy little boy had grown into. He was 25 years old, by our count, good blood in his veins, and full of fire. Now you know as much about Mr. Franklin Blake as I did, the day before he turned up at the house.

That Thursday was the finest summer day you could hope for: blue sky, warm breeze, not a drop of rain in sight. Lady Verinder and Miss Rachel, not expecting Mr. Franklin till dinner, drove out to lunch with friends three miles off. Once they were gone, I double checked the guest room we’d got ready for him, made sure the sheets were aired and the fire was laid just right. Then, as butler and steward (I took both jobs, mind, because I’d never trust anyone else to hold the key to old Sir John’s legendary cellar), I fetched up a bottle of our finest vintage Latour claret and set it out in the sun to lose its cellar chill before dinner. Figuring what’s good for old French wine is good for an old butler, too, I grabbed my wicker beehive chair to go sit in the sunny back court — when I heard a soft, steady drum-tap coming from the front terrace.

I rounded the corner, and found three deep mahogany-skinned Indian men, dressed all in white linen frocks and trousers, staring up at the house. Each had a tiny hand drum slung over his chest. A little pale, delicate English boy with fair hair stood behind them, holding a canvas bag full of their props. I pegged them straight away as strolling conjurors, the kind that travel the countryside doing tricks for coin. The man at the front, who spoke perfect English and had manners finer than most lords I’ve waited on, confirmed it, and asked permission to perform his tricks for Lady Verinder.

I’m no sour old man, and I don’t distrust anyone just because their skin’s a darker shade than mine. But even the best of us have weaknesses, and mine is this: whenever I know the family silver basket is sitting out on the pantry table, any stranger with manners nicer than mine sets every alarm bell ringing in my head. So I told the chief Indian the lady of the house was out, and politely but firmly showed all four of them off the grounds. He bowed so low his turban almost touched the paving stones, and they left.

I trundled back to my chair, sat down in the sun, and was just about to nod off into a very pleasant doze, when Penelope came running at me like the whole west wing was on fire.

“Get the constables!” she gasped. “Those Indian jugglers mean to do Mr. Franklin Blake harm!”

That name jolted me wide awake. I made her slow down and explain.

It turned out she’d been gossiping with the lodge keeper’s daughter, and the two of them had seen the Indians leave the grounds, then stop on the other side of the hedge that lines our boundary. They’d got it into their heads the little English boy was being abused by the foreigners (for no reason I could fathom other than that he was small and fair and looked soft), so they crept along the inside of the hedge to spy. What they saw was no ordinary juggler’s rehearsal.

First the three Indians checked the road up and down, made sure not a soul was in sight. Then they all turned and stared hard in the direction of the house, jabbering away fast in their own language, looking confused. Then they turned to the little boy, like he was the only one who could help them. The chief Indian, the one who spoke English, said: “Hold out your hand.”

Penelope said her heart nearly jumped right out of her chest right then. I privately thought it was more likely her stays, but all I said out loud was “You make my flesh creep.” (A little compliment like that never goes amiss with a woman, mark my words.)

The boy shrank back, shook his head, said he didn’t want to. The Indian didn’t get angry, though. He just asked him if he’d rather be sent back to London, left in the market where they’d found him, asleep in an empty wicker basket, hungry and ragged and no one to care for him. That was enough. The little boy held out his palm, trembling.

The chief pulled a small glass bottle out of his tunic, poured a pool of thick black ink, dark as ink, right into the hollow of the boy’s hand. He traced strange signs in the air above the boy’s head, touched his hair, and said: “Look.”

The boy went stiff as a statue, his eyes fixed dead on the ink pooled in his palm.

Up to that point, it just looked like silly hocus pocus to me, a waste of good ink, and I was already nodding off again — till Penelope’s next words jolted me upright.

The Indians checked the road again. The chief spoke to the boy: “See the English gentleman who comes from foreign parts?”

The boy said, very quiet: “I see him.”

“Is he travelling on no road but the road to this house, today?” the Indian asked.

The boy said: “He travels on no road but the road to this house.”

The Indian paused, and asked the next question, slow and clear. “Does the English gentleman carry *It* on him?”

The boy paused too, then said: “Yes.”

One last question. “Will he arrive here, as he promised, at the end of the day?”

“I can’t tell,” the boy said. “The mist rises in my head now. I can see no more.”

The chief said something sharp to the other two Indians, pointed at the boy, then pointed at the inn in the nearby town where we later learned they were staying. He traced more signs over the boy’s forehead, blew on his face, and the boy woke up gasping, like he’d been pulled out of a dream. Then the four of them set off for the town, and the two girls saw no more of them.

Most things have a moral, if you look for it. My sensible, sober take on the whole business was this: first, the jugglers had overheard the outdoor servants gossiping about Mr. Franklin’s upcoming visit, and cooked up a magic trick to perform for Lady Verinder when she got home, to make a few extra shillings. Second, they were just rehearsing their lines out by the hedge, same as actors before a play. Third, I’d be wise to keep an extra eye on the silver basket that evening. Fourth, Penelope ought to calm down and let her old dad get back to his sun nap.

But Penelope would have none of it. She was deadly serious. “Oh father,” she said, clutching my arm, “don’t you dare joke about this. What is *It*? What were they asking if he had on him?”

“We’ll ask Mr. Franklin when he gets here, my dear,” I said, winking to show I was teasing. But she took it dead seriously. “Ask him!” she said. “And see if he thinks it’s a laughing matter, too.” Then off she stormed.

After she left, I decided I really would ask Mr. Franklin, just to set her mind at rest. But before we go any further, I’d best warn you: you won’t find a single joke in that conversation later that day. To my absolute shock, Mr. Franklin took the whole business far more seriously than Penelope ever did.

You’ll understand how seriously, when I tell you — he said the “It” those Indian conjurors were asking after, was none other than the Moonstone.

I know full well I risk boring you to tears rambling on about me and my rickety beehive armchair. A drowsy old man dozing in a sun-warmed back yard is hardly thrilling company, I grant you. But facts must fall in the order they actually happened, so bear with me just a little longer – we’re edging close to Mr. Franklin Blake’s long-awaited arrival later that same day.

My daughter Penelope had barely stepped out of sight before I was jolted out of my pre-nap haze by a racket of clattering plates and cutlery from the servants’ hall: dinner was served. I take all my meals in my own little parlour, so I had no stake in the staff’s dinner but to wish every last one of them a good appetite, before settling back to doze in my chair. I was just stretching my stiff old legs when a woman came barrelling straight into my path. It wasn’t Penelope this time – it was Nancy, the kitchen maid, face all set in a sullen pout. As head of the household staff, I never let a sulky face slip past unchallenged as a matter of principle, so I stopped her dead. She tried to push past without answering my questions, so I gave her ear a gentle tweak – my usual, friendly way of showing I approve of a plump, hardworking lass.

That’s how I learned Rosanna Spearman, our second housemaid, was late to dinner again. Nancy was sent to drag her back, and she was spitting mad about it, complaining all the heavy work fell to her shoulders while Rosanna lazed off. I felt a sharp twinge of pity for Rosanna, and I could see plain as day Nancy would march back to the sands yelling at the poor girl far harsher than she deserved. Since I had nothing better to do, I told Nancy to go back to her roast – I’d go fetch Rosanna myself, and give her the little warning about punctuality I knew she’d take kindly coming from me. Nancy, who has a famous appetite, lit up so fast I chucked her under the chin out of habit, grabbed my walking stick, and set off for the shore.

Wait – don’t let me rush ahead. I’d be doing you a disservice if I didn’t explain two things first, and both are tied tight to the great Moonstone mystery. You need to know Rosanna’s story, and you need to know the story of the strange stretch of sand she was so fond of. I promise we’ll be neck-deep in oddities and secrets soon enough.

To start with Rosanna: she was the only new hire in the house. Four months before this day, my lady visited a London reformatory, a home for women just released from prison who were trying to outrun their old bad choices. The matron, seeing how kind Lady Verinder was, pointed out Rosanna Spearman – a quiet girl who’d once stolen a small sum from a household, no grand city schemes to rob thousands, just the bad luck of being a nobody the law threw its full weight at. The matron swore she was one in a thousand, all she needed was one fair chance to prove she was worthy of trust. Lady Verinder, the most Christian and generous soul who ever walked this earth, gave her that chance. No one on the staff but me and Miss Rachel knew her past. She got full wages, all the same privileges as the other maids, quiet private words of encouragement from my lady when no one else was around, and she repaid that kindness ten times over. Even with her frequent faint spells, she did every task modestly, carefully, without a single complaint.

But she never made friends with the rest of the servants, save for my daughter Penelope who was kind but never close. She wasn’t pretty enough to make anyone jealous, anyway – the plainest woman in the whole house, with one shoulder lopsided and bigger than the other. The other maids resented her silence, her solitary ways. When they gossiped in their free time, she read or mended clothes. On her days off, she almost always slipped out alone. She never argued, never took offence, just kept a quiet, stubborn, polite distance from all of them. The other maids swore she was putting on airs, acting like she was too good for them – nothing but rank injustice, of course, no matter that there was some soft, quiet edge to her voice that didn’t sound quite like a common housemaid.

Now the sands. Our house perches high on the Yorkshire coast, almost right on the sea. Every path for miles around is pretty, scenic, well-trodden – except one. That quarter-mile trail winds through a sad, shadowy plantation of fir trees, and spits you out between two low, craggy cliffs onto the loneliest, ugliest little bay on the whole coast. Two jagged rocky spits, the North and the South, jutt out from the sand dunes into the water, and between them lies the worst patch of quicksand on the entire Yorkshire shore. At the turn of the tide, something deep in the unknown mud below stirs, and sets the whole surface of the sand heaving, rippling, shivering all over – that’s why the locals call it the Shivering Sand. A great sandbank half a mile out breaks the full force of the open ocean, so when the tide rolls in, it creeps over the quicksand dead silent, no waves, no roar, just smooth, rising water. No fishing boat dares sail into that bay. No kids from the nearby Cobb’s Hole village come here to play. Even the seagulls, it seems to me, fly wide of the spot.

And that horrid, lonesome bay was Rosanna Spearman’s favourite place on earth. She’d sit there alone, reading or stitching, hour after hour, when all the other maids would take the lovely paths into town together. That’s exactly where I was heading to fetch her to dinner.

I saw no sign of her in the fir plantation. When I climbed over the dunes onto the beach, there she was – in her little straw bonnet, that plain grey cloak she always wore to hide her deformed shoulder as best she could, all alone staring out at the quicksand and the open sea. She jumped when I came up behind her, and turned her face away so I couldn’t see her. As head of staff, I never let a maid refuse to meet my eye without asking what’s wrong, so I turned her back around, and saw she’d been crying. I pulled out one of my lady’s six fine bandanna handkerchiefs, told her to sit with me on the slope of the sandbank, I’d dry her eyes and hear her out. By the time I’d settled my old bones down, she’d already wiped her face on her own cheap, thin cambric handkerchief. She looked quiet, and wretched, but sat down right next to me like a good girl.

When I asked her what she was crying over, she said it was the old years of her past, the life she was running from, coming back to haunt her. I told her that part of her life was wiped clean, why couldn’t she forget it? She plucked at the lappet of my coat, the very spot she’d scrubbed a grease stain out of the day before with a new cleaning mixture. The grease was gone, but the nap of the cloth was still dull and marked right where the stain had been. “The stain is taken off, Mr. Betteredge,” she said, shaking her head. “But the place shows. The place still shows.”

That left me speechless. There was no easy answer to that, not a single one. Her soft brown eyes were fixed on me, full of a quiet respect for my happy, blameless old age – a life she knew she could never have, no matter how hard she tried, and my heart felt heavy for her. I couldn’t think of a single thing to say to comfort her, so I helped pull myself up, and told her it was time to go in to dinner. She refused to help me up, instead she slipped her hand into mine and squeezed it soft. She said she didn’t want dinner, begged me to let her stay a little longer. When I asked her what drew her to this horrid place, she drew little patterns in the sand with her finger, and said it felt like something was pulling her here, that sometimes she thought her own grave was waiting for her in that bay.

I was indignant, naturally, telling her at my time of life I wasn’t going to stand for a five-and-twenty year old talking about her grave when there was roast mutton and suet pudding waiting for her in the house. She didn’t even seem to hear me. She put her hand on my shoulder to hold me sitting next to her, said it was lonelier for her in the house, surrounded by all the honest maids who had no idea what she’d done, than it was here all alone. The tide was turning right then, and the whole brown surface of the quicksand was rippling, shivering, heaving. She pointed at it, wide-eyed, said it looked to her like hundreds of suffocating people were trapped under it, fighting to get to the surface, sinking lower and lower. She begged me to throw a stone in, watch the sand suck it down.

I had a sharp rebuke on my tongue, to knock that unwholesome fancy right out of her head, when a voice from the sand dunes yelled my name at the top of its lungs: “Betteredge!”

I shouted back before I even thought who it was. Rosanna jumped to her feet, staring towards the voice, and her face flushed a bright, beautiful red I’d never seen on her plain cheeks before. She looked stunned, breathless, like she couldn’t believe her ears. “Who is it?” I asked her. She whispered the same question right back, soft, like she was talking only to herself.

I twisted around on the sand, and there he was – coming out of the dunes, bright-eyed, dressed in a fine fawn-coloured suit, gloves and hat to match, a rose stuck in his buttonhole, a grin on his face bright enough to make the Shivering Sand itself want to smile back. Before I could scramble to my feet, he plopped down right next to me, threw his arms around my neck in that over-familiar foreign fashion he picked up on his travels across the Continent, and squeezed the breath clean out of me. “Dear old Betteredge!” he laughed. “I still owe you seven-and-sixpence. Now do you know who I am?”

Lord bless us – it was Mr. Franklin Blake, three full hours earlier than we’d ever expected him.

I didn’t even get a word out before he glanced past me at Rosanna, looking a little surprised at how flustered she was. I turned to look, and she was blushing even harder, bright as a cherry, when she caught his eye. She didn’t curtsy, didn’t say a single word to either of us, just turned and ran off, vanishing over the dunes, the hem of her grey cloak fluttering out of sight in seconds. That was so unlike her – she was always the most polite, well-mannered girl on the whole staff.

“Odd girl,” Franklin said, frowning a little confused. “I wonder what she sees in me to shock her so?”

I teased him a little, joking that it must be all that fancy foreign polish on him, the varnish he picked up on his travels, that spooked her. I write that silly exchange down here to comfort every plain, slow-witted soul reading this: even the sharpest, most well-educated young gentleman with all the Continental experience in the world, and me, an old man with decades of household sense, hadn’t the faintest ghost of an idea what Rosanna Spearman’s strange, flustered behaviour really meant. She was almost gone from our thoughts before her cloak vanished completely. But don’t you forget her. Read on, and you’ll end up feeling as sorry for that poor, lost girl as I did, when the whole truth of the Moonstone finally unspools.

The last of the summer stragglers had drifted out of sight along the coast, and the second the two of us were left alone on the gritty edge of the Shivering Sand, I made my third clumsy attempt to heave myself up off my seat on the shore. Mr. Franklin’s hand came down light but firm on my shoulder to stop me.
“One blessing at least of this godforsaken place,” he said, “we’ve got the whole stretch of it to ourselves. Stay right where you are, Betteredge. I’ve got something weighty to say to you.”
While he spoke I was squinting at him, trying to dig up a trace of the scruffy rosy-cheeked boy I’d known running wild about the estate years before. The man standing in front of me baffled me at first look. No sign of that round boyish face, no trace of his old trim little play jacket. His complexion had paled to a faint, nervous clear white, and the lower half of his face was covered in a thatch of curly brown beard and moustache that took me completely by surprise. He had that quick, jumpy, charming touch-and-go way about him, I’ll grant you, but nothing that matched the free-and-easy rascal manners of the lad I remembered. Worse still, he’d promised to grow well over middle height, and he’d failed to keep that promise: neat, slim, well-made, but a good inch or two short of what we’d all expected. For a minute I thought the years had rubbed every last scrap of his old self away — until I met his eyes. That bright, straightforward, unflinching glint was exactly the same. There was my boy again, and I stopped poking at my memories of him right there.
“Welcome back to the old place, Mr. Franklin,” I said. “All the warmer welcome, sir, seeing as you’ve turned up hours before we were all expecting you.”
“I’ve a very good reason for beating the timetable,” he answered. “Betteredge, I’ve been tailed and watched in London for the last three or four days. I swapped the afternoon train for this morning’s one expressly to shake off a certain dark, scowling stranger who’s been dogging my every step.”
Those words didn’t just surprise me. They jolted me straight back to the three wandering Indian jugglers we’d had hanging around the gate that very morning, and Penelope’s silly little notion that they meant no good to Mr. Franklin Blake.
“Who’s the man watching you, sir? And what on earth for?” I asked.
Instead of answering, he turned the question back on me. “Tell me about those three Indians you had at the house today. It’s just possible that my mysterious stranger and your three jugglers are pieces of the very same puzzle.”
“How do you know about the jugglers already, sir?” I blurted out, piling one bad-mannered question on top of the other — but you don’t expect perfect politeness from an old servant like me, do you?
“I ran into Penelope at the front door the second I arrived,” he said, grinning. “Your daughter promised to grow into a pretty girl, Betteredge, and she kept that promise. Small ear, small foot — your late wife Mrs. Betteredge had those little charms too, didn’t she?”
“Late Mrs. Betteredge had her share of faults, sir,” I told him. “One of them, begging your pardon for saying so, was that she could never stick to a topic to save her life. She was more like a fly than a woman, darting from one thing to the next and never settling anywhere long.”
“She’d have been my perfect companion,” Mr. Franklin laughed. “I never stick to anything either. Penelope told me you’d be the one to give me all the juggler details, by the way. ‘Father’s a wonderful man for his age,’ she said, blushing bright pink, ‘and he expresses himself beautifully.’ Don’t look cross — I knew her when she was seven and running around the garden barefoot, so you can’t begrudge me a quick kiss on the cheek. Now let’s be serious. What did those jugglers do?”
I was a little huffy at Penelope for spilling that silly story of hers second-hand, but there was no use dragging my feet now, so I laid the whole incident out for him. All the laughter drained right off his face as I talked. He sat frowning hard, twisting at the end of his beard between his fingers, and when I finished he repeated the two questions the head juggler had asked the small boy he’d stopped, like he was carving them into his memory.
“‘Is it on the road to this house, and on no other, that the English gentleman will travel today?’ ‘Has the English gentleman got It about him?’” He fished a small, sealed paper parcel out of his coat pocket, his face deadly serious now. “I suspect ‘It’ means this. And this, Betteredge, is my uncle Herncastle’s famous Moonstone Diamond.”
“Good Lord, sir!” I burst out. “How in the world did you end up in charge of that wicked Colonel’s cursed rock?”
“The old sinner’s will left that diamond as a birthday present to my cousin Rachel,” he said. “My father, as the Colonel’s executor, handed it straight over to me to bring down here safe and sound.”
I doubt I could have been more shocked if the tide creeping smooth over the Shivering Sand had suddenly turned to dry land right under my backside. “The Colonel’s diamond left to Miss Rachel? And your father the Colonel’s executor? Why, I’d have laid any wager you cared to name, Mr. Franklin, that your father wouldn’t have touched that man with a pair of tongs!”
“Strong words, Betteredge! What was there against the Colonel, anyway? He belonged to your generation, not mine. Tell me everything you know about him, and I’ll tell you how my father ended up as his executor, and the rest of the ugly secrets I dug up about him and that diamond in London. You called him the wicked Colonel just now. Dig into that old memory of yours, and tell me why.”
I saw he was dead earnest, so I leaned in and told him. Let me pause here a second to beg you to pay full attention. Clear your head of your dinner, your new bonnet, your horse bets, your club grievances, all the nonsense that makes your eyes glaze over. Forget politics, forget City share prices, forget whatever triviality is prancing through your mind right now. If you skip this part, you’ll be totally lost the moment we go deeper into the story. Don’t take that as me being rude — haven’t I seen you readers drift off even when you’re holding a great author’s book in your hands, the second the page stops being a person talking right to you?
I told him about the old lord, my lady’s father, short-tempered and sharp of tongue, who had five children total. First two sons, then three little girls in a rush years later, my mistress the youngest and kindest of the lot. The oldest son Arthur inherited the title and all the estates. The second, the Honourable John Herncastle, got a huge fortune left to him by a distant relative and joined the army.
They say it’s an ill bird that fouls its own nest, and the Herncastles are my nest, so I won’t dwell on every last rotten thing about the Honourable John. But I will say he was one of the biggest blackguards that ever drew breath. He joined the Guards first, and got kicked out before he was twenty-two for reasons no decent man would name. He shipped off to India to see if they were laxer out there, and get himself some active service. For all his faults, he was brave — a cross between a bulldog, a gamecock, and a dash of the wildest savage you ever saw. He was at the sacking of Seringapatam, then swapped regiments twice more, until he made Lieutenant Colonel, got a brutal sunstroke, and came home to England.
He came back with a reputation so black every single door in his own family slammed shut in his face. My lady, newly married by then, led the charge, declaring with my lord’s full approval that her brother would never set foot in her house. There were half a dozen unspeakable slurs attached to his name, but the stain of the Moonstone is the only one that matters here. Everyone said he’d stolen that huge yellow gem from a Hindu temple during the Seringapatam looting, by means so vile he never dared admit to them. He never tried to sell it — he had no need for money, and never cared about profit. He never gave it away, never showed it to a single living soul. Some said he was afraid it would get him in trouble with the military authorities. Others, fools who didn’t know the man at all, said he was afraid showing it would get him killed.
There was a grain of truth in that last rumour. He wasn’t afraid, not him. But his life had been threatened twice in India, and everyone knew the Moonstone was the reason for it. When he came home and found every last person shunning him, they said the Moonstone was the reason for that too. The mystery of that diamond turned him into an outlaw among his own people. Men wouldn’t let him into their clubs. The women he tried to marry all turned him down. Friends and relations pretended they were too short-sighted to recognise him on the street.
Any other man would have tried to clear his name. But the Honourable John never gave an inch, even when he was wrong, even when all society was against him. He’d kept that diamond in India, defying every assassin that came for him. He kept that diamond in England, defying every bit of public opinion that said he shouldn’t. That was the man: brute, stubborn, daring everything, his handsome face twisted with that look like the devil himself was sitting behind his eyes.
He lived a vicious, solitary, underground life. Rumours drifted to us now and then: he was smoking opium and collecting old rare books, he was messing around with strange dangerous chemistry, he was carousing in the worst slums of London with the dregs of society. Only once, two years before this very day, a year and a half before he died, did I ever see him face to face.
It was the night of Miss Rachel’s birthday, the 21st of June, and we had a big party going on in my lady’s London house in her honour. A footman came to fetch me, saying a gentleman was waiting in the hall to see me. I walked out, and there stood the Colonel, haggard, shabby, old and worn, wild and wicked as he’d ever been.
“Go up to my sister,” he said, “and tell her I’ve come to wish my niece many happy returns of the day.”
He’d sent letters before trying to reconcile with my lady, though I’m convinced he only did it to annoy her — but this was the first time he’d ever turned up at the door in person. I almost told him my lady had a full party tonight, but that devilish glint in his eye daunted me, so I trundled upstairs with his message, and left him waiting in the hall like he asked. All the servants were clustered at the far end of the corridor staring at him, like he was a walking loaded cannon that could go off and blow them all to bits at any second.
My lady had a sharp streak of the family temper, same as the rest of them. “Tell Colonel Herncastle,” she said, when I gave her the message, “that Miss Verinder is engaged, and I decline to see him.” I tried to beg her for a softer answer, knowing the Colonel didn’t play by the rules that govern normal gentlemen — but it was no use. The temper flared straight at me. “When I want your advice,” she snapped, “you know I ask for it. I don’t ask for it now.”
I trundled back downstairs, and took the liberty of softening her message to something more polite: “My lady and Miss Rachel regret that they are both engaged, Colonel, and beg to be excused from having the honour of seeing you.”
I expected him to fly into a rage. To my total shock, he didn’t. He was unnaturally quiet, those glittering pale grey eyes fixed on me for a long minute, and he laughed — not a normal laugh out loud, but a soft, quiet, chuckling, horribly mischievous little laugh he kept right inside his chest. “Thank you, Betteredge,” he said. “I shall remember my niece’s birthday.” And with that, he turned on his heel and walked right out the front door.
The next birthday rolled around, and we heard he was bedridden, ill. Six months after that, half a year before today, we got a letter from a very respectable clergyman saying two miraculous things: first, that the Colonel had forgiven his sister on his deathbed, and second, that he’d forgiven everyone else and died a most pious, edifying death. I’ve got all the respect in the world for the Church, but I know for a fact the devil kept full possession of John Herncastle right up to his last breath, and that his final abominable trick was fooling that poor clergyman completely.
That was all I had to tell Mr. Franklin. He leaned in closer the longer I talked, and the part about the Colonel being turned away on Rachel’s own birthday seemed to hit him like a bullet right on target. He didn’t say it, but I could see plain as day he was made uneasy by it.
“You’ve told your side, Betteredge,” he said. “Now it’s my turn. But before I tell you what I dug up in London, and how I got tangled up in this diamond mess, I want to know one thing. You look like you haven’t the faintest clue why we’re sitting here on the sand going over all this. Are your eyes lying?”
“No sir,” I said. “They’re telling the plain truth.”
“Then let me lay out my point of view for you, before we go a step further.” He leaned back, that bright boyish little flash of pride at how clever he was coming back for a second, just like when he was a lad. “There are three very serious questions tied up in the Colonel’s birthday gift to Rachel. Count them off on your fingers if it helps you follow. First: was the Colonel’s diamond the centre of a lifelong conspiracy among the Hindu priests in India who swore to get it back to their temple, no matter what it took? Second: did that conspiracy follow the diamond all the way to England? Third: did the Colonel know exactly they were following it, and did he deliberately leave that cursed rock as a legacy of trouble and danger, to get his revenge on his sister, using her own innocent daughter as the tool? That’s what I’m driving at, Betteredge. Don’t let me frighten you.”
Too late. He’d already frightened me. If he was right, our quiet little English country house had just been invaded by a devilish Indian diamond, dragging after it a gang of ruthless living thieves, set loose on us all by the petty vengeance of a dead man. Who ever heard the like of it? In the nineteenth century, mind you, the age of progress, in a country that boasts of the blessings of British law and the constitution? No one ever did — so no one will believe it, I don’t doubt. But I’ll tell the story all the same.
When you get a shock like that, it always hits you right in the stomach. My gut was twisting itself into knots, I was fidgeting on the sand, my mind wandering all over the place. Mr. Franklin noticed me squirming, stopped himself mid-sentence about to launch into his own story, and snapped: “What do you want?”
I didn’t tell him, but I’ll tell you in confidence. What I wanted more than anything in the whole world was to get back to the house, light my old clay pipe, and lose myself in the pages of my tattered, dog-eared copy of Robinson Crusoe.

I was still reeling in a fog of tangled thoughts, desperate for five quiet minutes to sort my wits back into order, when my daughter Penelope stepped square in my path—exactly the way her late mother always used to block my way on the stairs when she was set on prying a story out of me. She instantly demanded a full accounting of every word that had passed between Mr. Franklin Blake and me at our meeting by the Shivering Sand.

There was only one sensible move under the circumstances: snuff out Penelope’s curiosity before it could spark any further awkward questions. I told her Mr. Franklin and I had rambled on about foreign politics until we could not stand the sound of each other’s voices any longer, then both drifted fast asleep in the midday sun. Try that trick the next time your wife or daughter corners you for an answer you cannot give, and trust in the natural sweetness of women to kiss and make up for the slight no more than an hour later.

The afternoon dragged on, and Lady Verinder and Miss Rachel returned home. It goes without saying how stunned they were to learn Mr. Franklin had arrived on the early train and then immediately galloped off again on horseback. It goes equally without saying that they pounced with sharp, direct questions right away—and that my “foreign politics nap” lie did not hold water for a single second with them. Out of all ready excuses, I shrugged and told them Franklin’s sudden arrival was just one of his famous, unaccountable whims. When they pressed to know if his mad dash out on horseback was a second whim, I said yes it was, and slipped out of the bind in what I considered a very clever bit of improvisation.

I thought I had cleared all my hurdles with the family, but more waited for me back in my own quarters. Penelope turned up, true to that natural sweetness I’d counted on, to kiss and make up for my earlier fib—while armed with that equally unshakable natural female curiosity, to ask me what on earth was wrong with our second housemaid, Rosanna Spearman.

It seemed Rosanna had returned to the house in a most unaccountable state right after leaving Mr. Franklin and me by the Shivering Sand. Per Penelope, her face had flushed every shade of the rainbow: giddy and laughing for no reason one minute, sunk into unprovoked tears the next. She fired off a hundred rapid questions about Mr. Franklin Blake in one breath, then snapped angrily at Penelope for daring to imply a strange visiting gentleman could possibly be of any interest to her. Penelope caught her grinning as she scribbled Franklin’s name in the back of her workbox, then found her ten minutes later sobbing into the mirror, staring at her hunched, deformed shoulder. Could she have known Franklin before that day? Impossible. Could she have heard word of him from anyone else? Impossible again. I could personally vouch for how genuinely shocked Franklin had been when he saw the girl stare at him, and Penelope swore Rosanna’s endless curious questions were entirely unfeigned.

The whole tiresome back-and-forth dragged on until my daughter dropped a bombshell that struck me as the most absurd supposition I had ever heard in my life. “Father!” she said, dead serious. “There is only one explanation. Rosanna has fallen in love with Mr. Franklin Blake at first sight.”

You have read tales of beautiful young ladies falling head over heels at first sight, and you think that is natural enough. But a plain ex-reformatory housemaid with a crooked shoulder, falling instantly in love with a gentleman visiting her mistress’s estate? You will not find a more ridiculous premise in any storybook in all of Christendom. I laughed until the tears rolled down my cheeks.

Penelope took my merriment poorly, and in a very quiet, hurt voice said, “I never knew you could be cruel before, father,” and walked out.

Her words hit me like a dash of cold water straight to the face. I was furious at myself for the way I’d teased her—and for the sharp, unplaceable unease that settled in my bones the second she spoke. We will not speak of this any further, if you please. I regret drifting onto the topic, and you will understand why soon enough, as our story unfolds.

The dinner dressing bell rang before Mr. Franklin rode back from Frizinghall. I brought his hot water up to his room myself, fully braced to hear some disaster had struck during that long delay. To my great disappointment, and no doubt yours too, nothing untoward had happened at all. He had not spotted a single sign of the wandering Indian jugglers on his ride there or back. He had dropped the Moonstone off at the bank, described only as a high-value item of great price, and tucked the official receipt safe in his pocket. I trundled back downstairs feeling deflated, as if all the high tension around the diamond that morning had fizzled out to a very flat ending.

I never got to see how Franklin’s first meeting with his aunt and Miss Rachel went, of course. In my position as head butler, waiting at dinner (save for the highest of family festivals) would have undermined my authority in the eyes of the other staff—something Lady Verinder already teased me for doing far too easily, without me going out of my way to invite it. All the news I got from the dining room that night came from Penelope and our footman Samuel. Penelope said Miss Rachel had spent twice as long tending to her hair that evening as she ever had before, and she had never looked half so bright, half so pretty as when she glided down to the drawing room to meet Franklin. Samuel reported that keeping a respectful, blank composure before his betters while waiting on Mr. Franklin Blake was the hardest test of his entire training as a servant: the man was so obviously smitten he could barely string a coherent sentence together.

Later that evening, we could hear them singing duets through the open windows leading to the terrace: Franklin’s tenor trilling high, Miss Rachel’s lighter soprano sailing even higher over the top, and Lady Verinder at the piano shepherding them both over every tricky note, as if she was leading them safely across hedges and ditches all the way through the song. When I brought Franklin his brandy and soda in the smoking room much later, I found Miss Rachel had driven all thought of the Moonstone clean out of his head. “She is the most charming girl I have met since I came back to England!” was all I could drag out of him, no matter how gently I steered the conversation toward more serious business.

Towards midnight, I did my usual rounds to lock up the house, Samuel trailing after me as my second in command. When every last door was bolted save for the side door opening onto the terrace, I sent Samuel off to bed, and stepped outside to take a breath of fresh air before I turned in for the night. The night was still and close, the full moon hanging bright and round in the sky. It was so quiet out of doors that I could hear the faint, low murmur of the sea swell rolling in over the sandbar at the mouth of our little cove. The terrace side of the house lay cast in deep shadow, but the broad moonlight lit the full length of the gravel walk running along the house’s other adjacent side. When I lowered my gaze after staring up at the moon, I spotted the long shadow of a person stretched across the moonlit gravel, thrown forward from behind the far corner of the house.

Old and experienced as I was, I did not call out and give my presence away. But I was also old and heavy, and my boots crunched too loud on the gravel, betraying my approach. Before I could slip silently around the corner to catch the trespasser off guard, I heard light footsteps—more than one pair, I swore—scurrying away as fast as they could. By the time I rounded the corner, whoever the intruders were had already vanished into the thick shrubbery at the far end of the walk, hidden from sight behind the tangled trees and undergrowth. From there, they could easily climb our estate fence and slip out onto the public road. If I had been forty years younger, I might have stood a chance of catching them before they cleared the grounds. As it was, I doubled back to rouse Samuel, no alarms, no rousing the sleeping household, and the two of us took a pair of shotguns to comb every inch of the property, the shrubbery, all around the house, until we were certain no one was left lurking anywhere.

On our walk back, I spotted a tiny glint of light on the clean gravel, under the moon: a small glass vial, holding a thick, sweet-smelling liquor as black as ink. I said nothing of it to Samuel. But I remembered exactly what Penelope had told me earlier that day, about the Indian jugglers, and the little pool of black ink they had poured into the palm of the boy’s hand for their fortune trick. I knew instantly who I had disturbed skulking behind the house that night. The three Indians were there, creeping through the dark, bent on their heathen tricks to find out exactly where the Moonstone was hidden that very night.

For a moment, I must hit pause on this story.

Digging up my own old memories, and calling on Penelope to supplement them from her little daily journal, I realize we can skip lightly over the stretch between Mr. Franklin Blake’s arrival and Miss Rachel’s twenty-first of June birthday. Most of those weeks drifted by without a single thing worth marking down. With your kind permission, I’ll only note the key dates that matter, and reserve my day-by-day, blow-by-blow telling for when the Moonstone stops being a secret between a few of us and becomes the whole household’s urgent, all-consuming business.

That settled, we move right back to where we left off: the bottle of sweet-smelling magic ink I found abandoned on the gravel path late that night.

The next morning, the twenty-sixth of the month, I showed Mr. Franklin the ink bottle and told him the whole story of the wandering Indian jugglers. His take was sharper than I expected: he was not only certain the Indians had been hanging around the house hunting the Diamond, but convinced they were foolish enough to fully believe their own hocus-pocus — the sort that lets them draw signs on a little boy’s head, pour ink into his palm, and claim he can see people and places no normal human eye could reach. “We’ve got the exact same trick over here too,” he told me, “just without the ink. They call it clairvoyance, French for ‘clear sight.’ Depend on it, the Indians assumed we’d keep the Diamond in this house, and they brought their little boy seer to show them exactly where it was hidden if they snuck inside.”

“Do you think they’ll try breaking in again, sir?” I asked him.

“Depends entirely on what the boy can actually do,” he said. “If he can see the Diamond right through the iron walls of the Frizinghall bank safe, we won’t see hide nor hair of those Indians for a good while. If he can’t? We’ll catch them skulking in the shrubbery again before three more nights are out.”

I waited, sure enough, for that second chance to catch the rogues. But strange as it sounds, it never came.

Maybe the jugglers heard rumors in town that Mr. Franklin had been spotted heading to the bank with the Diamond, and drew their own conclusions. Maybe the boy actually did see the gem in that vault (which I, for one, flat refuse to believe). Maybe it was just pure dumb luck. But the plain fact is: not a single one of those Indians came within a mile of the house for all the weeks leading up to Miss Rachel’s birthday. They stayed in town peddling their little magic shows, Mr. Franklin and I kept quiet about our suspicions to avoid tipping them off, and that’s the end of the Indian business — for now.

On the twenty-ninth, Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin stumbled on a new hobby that ate up all their spare time, and it’s important I tell you about it, because it ties directly to the trouble that’s coming later.

Gentlefolk face a very nasty hidden reef in their lives, you see: the reef of their own total idleness. They spend every day casting around for something to do, and far too often — especially the ones who call themselves “intellectual” — they drift blindfolded straight into some messy, cruel pastime. Nine times out of ten, they spend their days torturing or ruining something, and they pat themselves on the back for “improving their minds” when all they’re really doing is making a right mess of the house. I’ve seen ladies and gentlemen trudge out every morning with empty pillboxes, catch newts, beetles, spiders, frogs, stick pins through the poor wretches, and cut them to bits without a flicker of remorse, all in the name of “natural history.” I’ve seen them poke at perfectly pretty flowers with sharp little scalpels just to see what’s inside, as if the color or the scent gets any sweeter once you rip the thing apart. You end up staining your fingers with photography chemicals, chipping bits of stone off the walls to put in your rock collection, dripping grit into every dinner dish, or keeping tadpoles in dirty glass jars that make the whole house want to throw up. Hard work to pay for your bread and roof is nothing next to that empty, restless idleness. Thank your lucky stars your head has something it has to think about, and your hands have something they have to do.

Thankfully, Mr. Franklin and Miss Rachel never hurt a living thing. All they ruined was the wood panel of a door.

Mr. Franklin, who dabbled in every art under the sun, had lately got into what he called “decorative painting.” He’d invented a new custom mixture to thin his paints that he named his “vehicle.” I don’t know what was in it, but I can tell you what it did in two words: it stank so bad the dogs sneezed the second they stepped into the room. Miss Rachel was wild to try it, so he sent all the way to London for the supplies, strapped an apron and a bib over her nice gown, and set her to work decorating the little private sitting-room everyone called her boudoir. They started on the inside of the door. Mr. Franklin scraped all the nice shiny varnish off with pumice stone to make a rough surface to paint on, and under his direction, Miss Rachel covered it all with griffins, songbirds, flowers, and chubby little cupids copied from designs by that famous old Italian painter — the one who painted half the Virgin Marys in the world and had a baker’s daughter for a sweetheart. It was slow, messy work, but they never seemed to tire of it. Every spare minute they were glued together over that door, chattering and painting away, as busy as bees.

The next date worth marking is Sunday, the fourth of June. That night, we all sat in the servants’ hall debating a question that, like the door decoration, will turn out to matter a great deal later. Seeing how much Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin liked being around each other, and what a handsome, well-matched pair they made, most of the staff bet we’d have a wedding in the house before summer was over. I was the one who poured cold water on that idea — I thought Miss Rachel might very well marry, but I very much doubted her groom would be Mr. Franklin Blake.

Nobody could deny Mr. Franklin was head over heels for her. The hard part was figuring out what was going on in Miss Rachel’s head. Let me paint her portrait for you proper, and you can try to fathom her for yourself, if you can.

Her eighteenth birthday was coming up on the twenty-first of June. If you like dark, slim women (which I’m told are out of fashion in London society lately), you’d think she was the prettiest girl you ever laid eyes on. Every line of her was perfectly proportioned, from the top of her glossy jet-black hair down to her feet, and the way she sat, stood, and walked made it obvious her grace came from her body, not her fancy clothes. Her eyes were just as dark as her hair. Her nose was a tiny bit too small, I’ll admit, but her mouth and chin?
