Mr. Franklin swore they were “morsels for the gods,” and her warm, sun-kissed complexion was far more reliable than the actual sun, always looking soft and lovely. She carried her head as straight as a dart, sharp and spirited and full of fire, had a clear, silver voice, and a smile that lit up her eyes before it even reached her lips. That’s Miss Rachel, exactly as she was.

She wasn’t perfect, of course. She had just as many faults as you or I. To be blunt, she was nothing like most girls her age: she had ideas all her own, and she was stubborn enough to defy every fashion and every social rule if they didn’t suit her. In small, trivial things that independence was charming, but in matters that counted, Lady Verinder and I both agreed it went too far. She judged everything for herself, never asked for anyone’s advice, never told anyone what she was planning to do beforehand, never came running with secrets and confidences even to her own mother. She loved hard and hated hard, and went her own way through joy and sorrow, never leaning on anyone else. Over and over I heard Lady Verinder say, “Rachel’s best friend and Rachel’s worst enemy are one and the same: Rachel herself.”

And for all that stubborn secrecy? There wasn’t a flicker of falseness in her. I never once saw her break a promise. I never saw her say no and mean yes. I remember more than one occasion when she was a little girl, she took the blame and the punishment for something a playfellow did, and even when they called her out on it, she never confessed who really did it — and she never lied about it, either. She’d look you straight in the face, toss her little head, and say flat out, “I won’t tell you.” Even when they locked her up on bread and water for being naughty, she never snitched. Devilishly self-willed, yes — but the finest, truest creature that ever walked this earth. If that sounds like a contradiction? Spend twenty-four hours watching your own wife. If she doesn’t show you some sort of contradiction in that time? Heaven help you — you married a monster.

Now that you know Miss Rachel, we’re face to face with that question of who she intended to marry. On the twelfth of June, Lady Verinder sent an invitation to a gentleman in London to come celebrate her daughter’s birthday. That was the man I was convinced she had set her heart on: another cousin of hers, Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite.

Lady Verinder’s second sister had a terrible heartbreak young, then married a rich Frizinghall banker named Ablewhite against the whole family’s wishes, a so-called “misalliance” that caused all sorts of drama back in the day. But time softened everyone up, and the Ablewhites became well-respected local gentry living in a big fine house just outside Frizinghall. We won’t be seeing much of the rest of the family, but Mr. Godfrey, their second son, matters very much to this story.

As far as I was concerned, Mr. Franklin didn’t stand a chance against him.

For one thing, Mr. Godfrey was over six feet tall, broad-shouldered, with a rosy fair complexion, a smooth clean-shaven round face, and a head of thick flaxen hair that fell lazily over the back of his neck. You probably already know him if you’ve ever given money to a ladies’ charity in London: he was a barrister by trade, a favorite of every lady in the city, and a full-time professional philanthropist. He was vice-president, manager, and referee for every society you could name: societies for poor new mothers, societies for rescuing fallen women, societies for pushing women into jobs that used to be done by men. Wherever there was a committee of nice ladies sitting around a table debating charity work, there was Mr. Godfrey at the end of the table, all smiles and hat in hand, making sure no one lost their temper and guiding them through all the boring business. I saw him speak once at Exeter Hall once, and he could make an audience of a thousand people cry so hard they emptied their purses for his cause before they even knew what happened. He was famous, kind, sweet-tempered, loved by everyone. What chance did a regular man like Franklin have against him?

On the fourteenth, his reply arrived. He accepted the invitation, said he’d come from Wednesday (the birthday) to Friday evening, when his charity duties would force him back to London. He even enclosed a poem he’d written for his cousin’s “natal day.” Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin laughed at the silly verses all through dinner, and Penelope, who was solidly on Franklin’s side, turned to me all smug and asked what I thought of my theory about Godfrey now. “My dear girl,” I told her, “Miss Rachel is leading you up a false scent. My nose isn’t that easy to fool. Wait till Mr. Ablewhite shows up in person.”

Penelope argued Mr. Franklin could win her over long before Godfrey got here. And I had to admit, Franklin was pulling out all the stops to win her favor. He was one of the heaviest smokers I ever met, but he gave up his cigars cold turkey just because Rachel said she hated the stale smoke smell on his clothes. He slept horribly for days without his tobacco, came down every morning haggard and dark-eyed, until even Rachel begged him to start smoking again. No, he said, he wouldn’t do a single thing to annoy her, and he’d fight the craving until he learned to sleep without cigars, no matter how long it took. The whole downstairs staff agreed that level of devotion was sure to win Rachel over, especially with all the hours they spent together decorating that door. But I knew something they didn’t: Penelope had spotted a framed photograph of Godfrey on Rachel’s bedroom dressing table, showing him speaking at a public meeting, his hair blown back by his own passionate speech, his soft eyes smiling out at everyone. Every morning while Rachel was getting her hair combed, that photo was staring right at her. He’d be there in person soon enough, I was sure of it.

On the sixteenth of June, something happened that made Franklin’s chances look even slimmer. A stranger with a thick foreign accent showed up at the door asking to speak to Mr. Franklin Blake privately on business. It couldn’t have had anything to do with the Moonstone, because Franklin never said a word about it to me — he went straight to Lady Verinder the second the man left. She must have mentioned it to Rachel, because that evening at the piano, Rachel snapped at Franklin, saying some very sharp things about the disreputable people he’d run around with and the bad habits he’d picked up living abroad on the Continent. The next day, neither of them went near the door to paint a single stroke. I guessed some old trouble — a debt, or a woman — that Franklin had got himself tangled up in overseas had followed him all the way back to England, but no one was telling the servants anything.

By the seventeenth, the little cloud had blown over. They were back at the door, painting and laughing together, as friendly as they ever were. Penelope swore Franklin had popped the question to Rachel when they made up, and that she’d neither said yes nor no — she’d teased him that he wasn’t serious, and then Penelope caught her looking all sorry afterwards. But I knew Rachel’s stubborn, private nature far too well to believe she’d ever let her maid read her that easily. Penelope wasn’t reporting what she’d actually seen, she was reporting what she wanted to be true.

On the nineteenth, we sent for the doctor. He came to see our second housemaid, Rosanna Spearman — the same girl who’d already puzzled me so much down at the Shivering Sand. The lull before the birthday had made her behavior even more strange. Penelope and I had both caught her sneaking around, deliberately putting herself in Mr. Franklin’s path as often as she could. He didn’t notice her any more than he noticed the house cat, never once wasted a look on her plain quiet face. Her appetite got worse, her eyes were red and puffy every morning from crying half the night. One day Penelope made a terrible discovery: she walked in on Rosanna at Mr. Franklin’s dressing table, carefully plucking the rose Miss Rachel had put in his buttonhole earlier that day, and tucking a different rose, one she’d picked herself, in its place. After that, she got snappish with me when I tried to give her gentle hints to behave, and she even grew short with Miss Rachel on the rare occasions Rachel spoke to her.

Lady Verinder noticed the change, and asked me what I thought was wrong. I tried to protect the poor girl, told her it was just nervous exhaustion, and when the doctor agreed, Lady Verinder offered to send her to one of our quiet inland farms for a change of air. Rosanna begged and sobbed on her knees to be allowed to stay, and in an unforgivable moment of soft-heartedness, I advised Lady Verinder to let her stay a little longer. If I could have seen even a little way into the future, I would have dragged Rosanna Spearman out of this house myself, that very day.

The 21st of June, Miss Rachel’s birthday, dawned cloudy and restless, but by midday the sky cleared bright and fair. Down in the servants’ hall, we marked the happy anniversary as we always did: laying out our small handpicked gifts for Miss Rachel, with me, as the senior servant, delivering the annual formal address. I follow the exact same custom the Queen uses when she opens Parliament — I say nearly identical words, year after year, no surprises. Before I speak, the whole household waits for that speech as if it’s the most thrilling novelty they’ve ever heard. When I’m done, and it turns out to be the same familiar lines from last year, they grumble a little, then brighten up and look forward to a “newer version” 12 months down the line. Easy folk to govern, both in Parliament and the kitchen, that’s the long and short of it.

After breakfast, Mr. Franklin pulled me aside for a private talk about the Moonstone. The time had finally come to retrieve it from the Frizinghall bank and place it directly in Miss Rachel’s hands, as the late Colonel’s will demanded. I couldn’t say what had put our young gentleman out of sorts that morning — whether he’d tried to court his cousin again and got a sharp rebuff, or if his weeks of broken, restless sleep were making his usual contradictory, erratic nature twice as bad. Whatever the cause, he couldn’t settle on a single plan for the diamond, flipping through twenty different conflicting decisions in as many minutes.
Me, I stuck strictly to plain, unshakable facts. Nothing out of the ordinary had happened to give us reason to alarm my lady about the jewel, and no amount of overthinking could erase the legal obligation Mr. Franklin owed to his late uncle to hand the gem over to his cousin. That was my unshifting position, and twist and turn as he might, he was forced to land on the same conclusion in the end. We settled he would ride over to Frizinghall after lunch to collect the diamond, with Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite and his two sisters almost certainly keeping him company on the ride home.
Once that was sorted, Mr. Franklin went back to join Miss Rachel. They spent the rest of the morning and a good chunk of the afternoon on that never-ending project of theirs, decorating the front door. My Penelope stood by to mix their paints as directed, while my lady, as lunch drew near, darted in and out of the room holding a handkerchief pressed tight to her nose — they’d used a shocking amount of Mr. Franklin’s linseed oil that day — trying in vain to drag the two stubborn artists away from their work. It was three o’clock before they peeled off their paint-stained aprons, released Penelope (who was half giddy and lightheaded from the fumes), and scrubbed the paint smudges off their hands and faces.
But they’d finished what they set out to do: the door was fully decorated on Miss Rachel’s very birthday, and they were absurdly proud of it. The griffins, cupids, and flourishes all tangled up in painted flowers were, I’ll admit, very pretty to look at, but there were so many of them, all jumbled and twisted into topsy-turvy poses, that you’d leave with a throbbing headache for hours after you’d finished admiring the handiwork. If I add that Penelope ended that morning’s work by being sick in the back kitchen, I mean no disrespect to the linseed oil. It stops stinking once it dries, after all — and if Art demands those sorts of sacrifices, even if the girl in question is my own daughter, I say Art can have them.
Mr. Franklin snatched a few quick bites from the luncheon table and rode off for Frizinghall. He told my lady he was going to pick up his cousins, of course. Only he and I knew the real errand was to fetch the Moonstone.
As one of the house’s high-ranking ceremonial occasions, I was posted at the sideboard that night to oversee all the serving staff for the birthday dinner, so I had more than enough to keep me busy while Mr. Franklin was gone. I checked every decanter of wine, ran a quick inspection of the footmen and maids assigned to serve the table, then slipped off to rest a minute before the first guests arrived. A puff of my favorite tobacco and a few pages of that well-thumbed book I’ve mentioned before calmed me down completely, body and mind. I was roused from what I’d swear was not a nap, just a pleasant daydream, by the clatter of hooves outside the front door. When I stepped out to greet them, I found a whole little cavalcade: Mr. Franklin, the three Ablewhite cousins, and one old Mr. Ablewhite’s grooms, riding behind them.
Strangely enough, Mr. Godfrey struck me as being just as off his game that morning as Mr. Franklin was. He shook my hand warmly as usual, and gushed politely about how glad he was to see his old friend Betteredge looking so well — but there was a faint, uncharacteristic cloud over his face I couldn’t make heads or tails of. When I asked after his father’s health, he answered very shortly, “Much as usual.”
Luckily, the two Miss Ablewhites were cheerful enough to make up for twenty gloomy moods. They were nearly as tall and broad as their brother: strapping, yellow-haired, rosy-cheeked lasses bursting at the seams with so much health and high spirits the poor horses carrying them were visibly trembling under their weight. When they jumped down from their saddles without waiting for anyone to help them, I swear they bounced on the gravel like they were made of india-rubber. Every word out of their mouths started with a loud, open-mouthed “O!”; every action they took was loud and brash, and they giggled and screamed over the tiniest provocation, whether it was appropriate or not. Bouncers, that’s what I call them.
Under cover of all the noise the two girls were making, I got a chance to mutter a private word to Mr. Franklin in the hall.
“You’ve got the Diamond safe, sir?”
He nodded, and tapped the breast pocket of his coat.
“Seen hide nor hair of those Indian jugglers?”
“Not a glimpse.”
With that, he asked for my lady, and headed straight for the small drawing room. Barely a minute later, the bell rang, summoning Penelope to tell Miss Rachel Mr. Franklin Blake wanted a word with her.
Half an hour later, I was crossing the hall when a sudden burst of shrieks from the small drawing room made me stop dead. I wasn’t even slightly alarmed — I recognized that loud, wide “O!” shriek as the signature noise of the two Bouncers. Still, I stepped in under the pretense of asking for my lady’s instructions about the dinner menu, just to make sure nothing serious had gone wrong.
There was Miss Rachel, standing frozen by the table like she was in a trance, holding that accursed Colonel’s Diamond in her hand. The two Bouncers were kneeling on either side of her, gawking at the gem so hard their eyes were nearly popping out of their heads, screaming every time it caught the light and flashed a new color. There at the far end of the table, Mr. Godfrey was clapping his hands like a small excited child, murmuring soft little exclamations of “Exquisite! Exquisite!” Mr. Franklin was slouched in a chair by the bookcase, tugging hard at his beard, staring anxiously out the window. And there, right by that very window, was the object of his gaze: my lady, holding the official extract of the Colonel’s will in her hand, her back turned to the whole noisy party.
She turned to face me the second I walked in, and I could see that tight Verinder family frown settling over her eyes, that sharp Verinder family temper twitching at the corners of her mouth.
“Come to my room in half an hour,” she said. “I shall have something to discuss with you then.”
With that, she walked straight out. It was plain as day she was stuck on the exact same dilemma Mr. Franklin and I had debated back by the Shivering Sand: was the Moonstone legacy proof she’d treated her cruel late brother unfairly all those years, or was it proof he was far more malicious than she’d ever even imagined? Those were heavy questions for my lady to untangle, while her daughter, who knew nothing at all of the Colonel’s vicious character, stood there holding the strange, cursed birthday gift he’d left her.
Before I could leave the room myself, Miss Rachel — always kind to the old servant who’d been in this house since the day she was born — stopped me.
“Look, Gabriel!” she said, and flashed the diamond right in a thick beam of sunlight streaming through the window.
Lord bless my soul, it was a diamond, sure enough! Nearly as big as a plover’s egg. The light pouring off of it was as soft and bright as the glow of a full harvest moon. When you stared down into the heart of the stone, that deep rich yellow pulled at your eyes until you couldn’t see anything else at all. This tiny little jewel you could hold between your thumb and forefinger felt as vast and unfathomable as the sky itself. We set it down in the full sunlight, then drew all the curtains to block out the light — and there it was, glowing eerily all on its own in the dark, casting that same pale moon-like gleam without a single flame to light it. No wonder Miss Rachel was transfixed. No wonder her cousins were screaming. The diamond had such a hold on me that I blurted out a loud, wide “O!” just as big as any of the Bouncers’ shrieks.
The only one of us who kept his wits about him was Mr. Godfrey. He slipped an arm around each of his sisters’ waists, and looked at the diamond, then back at me, with that pitying, learned expression of his.
“Carbon, Betteredge! Mere carbon, my good friend, after all!”
I suppose he meant to educate a simple old servant. All he did was jog my memory about the dinner I still had to oversee. I hobbled off to my little army of waiting staff downstairs. As I left the room, Mr. Godfrey cooed, “Dear old Betteredge, I have the truest regard for him!” — all while he was still embracing his sisters and casting little adoring sidelong glances at Miss Rachel. That man had a bottomless stock of affection to hand out to everyone, it seemed. Mr. Franklin looked like a grumpy, quiet savage next to him.
At the end of the half hour, I reported to my lady’s room exactly as ordered. The conversation she and I had was mostly the same talk Mr. Franklin and I had shared back by the Shivering Sand — with one difference: I kept all my thoughts about the three Indian jugglers to myself, since nothing had happened to give me good reason to alarm her unnecessarily on that front. When she dismissed me, I could see she’d landed on the darkest possible interpretation of the Colonel’s motives, and was dead set on getting the Moonstone out of her daughter’s hands at the very first opportunity.
On my way back to the servants’ wing, I ran straight into Mr. Franklin. He asked if I’d seen any sign of Miss Rachel. I told him I hadn’t. Then he asked if I could tell him where Mr. Godfrey had gone. I said I didn’t know for certain — but I started to suspect that Mr. Godfrey wasn’t very far away from Miss Rachel at all. Mr. Franklin’s own suspicions had clearly landed on the same idea. He tugged so hard at his beard I thought he’d yank a chunk out, then stormed off to the library and slammed the door so hard the whole hallway rattled, that bang carrying every ounce of his frustrated feelings right along with it.
I wasn’t disturbed again while I prepped for the dinner, right up until it was time to smarten myself up in my formal livery. I’d just pulled on my starched white waistcoat when Penelope popped into my room, pretending to brush the few wispy strands of hair I still had left and straighten my white cravat. My girl was bubbling over with excitement, and I could tell she had some secret news she was dying to share. She kissed the top of my bald head, and whispered right in my ear, “I’ve got news for you, father! Miss Rachel turned him down.”
“Who’s ‘him’?” I asked.
“The ladies’ committee man, father,” Penelope huffed. “That sneaky, sly thing! I hate him for trying to shove Mr. Franklin out of the way and win her for himself!”
If I’d had enough breath in my lungs I’d have certainly scolded her for speaking so disrespectfully of such a well-known, upstanding philanthropic gentleman. But my daughter happened to be yanking at my cravat at that exact moment, her whole body thrumming with irritation, and I was half strangled before I could get a single word out.
“I saw him drag her away alone into the rose garden,” Penelope went on, still yanking at my hairbrush so hard my scalp was stinging. “I hid behind the holly bush to watch how they came back. They walked in arm-in-arm, both laughing like there was nothing in the world to worry about. They came out walking completely separate, both as grave as grave can be, staring straight away from each other — there was no mistaking what had just happened. I’ve never been so happy in my life, father! There’s at least one woman in the whole world who can resist Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, and if I was a lady I’d be the second one!”
Just as I was opening my mouth to protest that nonsense, the crunch of carriage wheels on the gravel outside hit my ears. The very first of the dinner guests had arrived. Penelope ran off like a shot. I slipped my coat on, checked myself in the mirror: my head was bright red as a boiled lobster, but apart from that I was every bit as neat and properly dressed for the evening’s ceremonies as any butler could hope to be. I stepped into the hall just in time to throw open the front doors and announce our first two guests. You needn’t care much about them, after all. They were only the philanthropist’s father and mother — Mr. and Mrs. Ablewhite.

One after another, the rest of the party followed the Ablewhite clan through the doors, until we had all twenty-four guests settled in their places around the long dinner table. It was a fine sight, when the Rector of Frizinghall—blessed with a smooth, resonant delivery—stood up to say grace.

I won’t waste your time running through the full guest list. You’ll not cross paths with any of them again for the rest of the story, save for two men who sat flanking Miss Rachel at the head of the table. As the birthday girl, queen of the occasion, she was the natural centre of every eye that night—made all the more conspicuous by the fact that she’d worn the fateful birthday gift her mother had tried to persuade her to leave locked away: the Moonstone Diamond, fixed as a gleaming brooch pinned to the bodice of her white dress, rigged up for her by that universal handy man Mr. Franklin Blake with nothing but a bit of thin silver wire and his neat, clever fingers.

Every guest oohed and aahed over the diamond’s impossible size and colour, as you’d expect. But only the two men sat right beside her said anything that stuck in my memory long after the plates were cleared.

The guest on her left was Mr. Candy, our local Frizinghall doctor. A cheerful, companionable little fellow, he had one stubborn flaw: he could never resist a bad joke, and would plunge headfirst into conversation with total strangers without stopping to test the waters first, often upsetting people by accident before he even realised he’d said something wrong. Only in his actual medical practice did he show good sense—his enemies claimed he picked his discretion by instinct, and he often got results where far more careful, qualified doctors fumbled and failed. His line about the diamond was, true to form, a silly mystifying prank. He leaned in as solemn as a judge, and begged her, “in the name of science, let me take it home and burn it. We’ll heat it to exactly the right temperature, blow a steady stream of air over it, and little by little—puff!—it will evaporate into nothing, and you’ll never have to lie awake worrying about keeping a priceless stone safe ever again.” I saw Lady Verinder, listening from the head of the table, wear such a careworn little look I swear she half-wished the doctor was in earnest, and that Rachel was half as eager to sacrifice her birthday gift for the cause of science.

The man on Rachel’s right was far more notable: the celebrated Indian traveller Mr. Murthwaite, the same man who’d risked his life travelling in disguise through parts of the subcontinent no white European had ever dared set foot before. Tall, lean, wiry, sunbrowned and almost completely silent, he had a permanent look of weary restlessness and a pair of steady, unblinking eyes that missed nothing. Rumour in our neighbourhood ran that he was sick to death of our quiet, humdrum village life, and ached to head back out wandering the wild, ungoverned corners of the East. Over the whole of the three-hour dinner, I doubt he spoke six full words, or drank more than a single glass of wine—all save for what he said to Rachel about her diamond. The Moonstone was the only thing in the whole room that interested him, it seemed. He’d heard tales of it even in those remote, dangerous Indian places he’d trekked through. He stared at it in silence for so long Rachel grew flustered and fidgeted under his gaze, until he spoke in his cool, flat, unshakable tone: “If you ever travel to India, Miss Verinder, leave your uncle’s birthday present behind. A Hindu diamond is sometimes far more than a pretty trinket—it is part of a Hindu’s religion. I know of a certain city, and a certain temple inside that city, where dressed as you are now, your life would not be worth five minutes’ purchase.” Rachel, safe and warm in her English country home, thought the idea of being in such danger perfectly thrilling. The two Bouncer girls, our neighbourhood’s most giddy young misses, were even more delighted: they dropped their knives and forks with a clatter and shrieked in unison, “O! How interesting!” Lady Verinder fidgeted in her chair at once, and steered the conversation hard onto a new topic.

As the dinner dragged on, I slowly realised this feast was not going half as well as all the birthday feasts we’d hosted in the past. Looking back now, with all that happened later fresh in my mind, I’m half convinced that cursed diamond had cast a blight over the entire company. I poured their wine as generously as I could, and used my privilege as the old, trusted family servant to follow even the unpopular dishes around the table, whispering confidentially, “Do try a bite, ma’am/sir, I promise it’ll do you good.” Nine times out of ten they humoured me and took a portion, out of fond old Gabriel Betteredge regard—but it made not a scrap of difference. Long, awkward silences kept gaping open across the table that made me shift uncomfortably at the sideboard. When people did speak, they managed to stumble into the most unfortunate, ill-timed conversations you could imagine. Mr. Candy, for one, said more unlucky, foot-in-mouth things that night than I’d ever heard him say in all his years of practice. Take just one sample, and you’ll understand the torture I was standing through, trying my hardest to keep the party’s spirits up.

One of the ladies at dinner was good Mrs. Threadgall, widow of the late Professor Threadgall. She talked about her late husband constantly, but it never occurred to her to mention to strangers that he was dead—she seemed to think every adult in England would already know that. In one of those long silences, someone brought up the dry, unpleasant topic of human anatomy, and Mrs. Threadgall immediately started gushing about how anatomy was her husband’s favourite hobby in his spare time. As bad luck would have it, Mr. Candy, sitting across the table and having no idea the Professor had been in his grave for ten years, jumped in to be helpful.

“The College of Surgeons got in some remarkably fine new skeletons recently,” he called across the table in a loud, cheerful voice. “I strongly recommend the Professor pay them a visit, ma’am, the next time he has an hour to spare.”

You could have heard a pin drop. The whole table went dead silent out of respect for the Professor’s memory. I was standing right behind Mrs. Threadgall, pouring her a glass of hock, when she dropped her head and mumbled very quietly, “My beloved husband is no more.”

But Mr. Candy, far from suspecting the truth, carried on louder and more polite than ever.

“The Professor may not know,” he went on, “that a member of the College’s card will admit him any day except Sunday, between ten and four.”

Mrs. Threadgall tucked her face right down into her neckerchief, and repeated, even quieter, “My beloved husband is no more.”

I winked so hard at Mr. Candy across the table I almost strained my eye. Rachel tapped him on the arm. Lady Verinder shot him a look that could have curdled cream. It was no use. He kept going, all hearty, unstopping cordiality: “I would be delighted to send the Professor my personal pass, ma’am, if you would just be so kind as to pass along his current address.”

“His current address, sir, is the grave!” Mrs. Threadgall burst out, suddenly flying into a rage so fierce her shout made the wine glasses rattle on the table. “The Professor has been dead these ten years.”

“Oh good Heavens!” Mr. Candy gasped. Save for the two Bouncers, who burst into hysterical giggles, the silence that fell after that was so heavy you’d have thought every last person at the table was already on their way to join the Professor in the grave.

That was Mr. Candy’s contribution to the awkwardness, but the rest of the party were nearly as bad in their own ways. They either clammed up when they should have been chatting, or babbled so hard they talked clean past each other. Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite, that famously eloquent public speaker, wouldn’t exert himself to entertain a soul in private. Whether he was sulking or still embarrassed after that rejection he’d got in the rose garden earlier that day, I can’t say. He stuck strictly to talking to the woman sat right next to him, one of his charity committee members: a pious, spiritual lady who had a fine set of collarbones and an excellent taste in dry champagne. Standing close behind them at the sideboard, I heard every word they exchanged while I pulled corks and carved the roast mutton. They’d long since finished discussing their plans for rescuing fallen women and running charity workshops, and were deep in some high-falutin spiritual debate. “Religion,” I heard Mr. Godfrey say between the pops of corks, “is just love. And love is just religion. The earth is nothing but heaven, a little worn out and scuffed around the edges. And heaven is just the same earth, all polished up to look brand new. There are some very unpleasant people down here on earth, to be sure—but up in heaven, every single woman will be a member of one enormous, perfectly harmonious committee that never argues, and all the men will wait on them hand and foot as ministering angels.” Beautiful words, no doubt—but why on earth was he hoarding all that charming talk for just one lady, and not sharing it with the rest of the party?

Surely, you’ll say, Mr. Franklin Blake would have stepped in and livened things up? Not a bit of it. He was in fine, high spirits—Penelope had no doubt told him all about Godfrey’s humiliating rejection in the rose garden—but no matter how hard he tried to spark conversation, he always landed on the wrong topic, or spoke to the wrong person, managing to puzzle half the table and offend the rest. That mixed foreign training of his, those French, German and Italian quirks I mentioned before, was on full, bewildering display at Lady Verinder’s dinner table.

What do you make of him debating at great length, in his witty, frank French style, exactly how far a married woman could let her admiration for a man who was not her husband go—all to the vicar of Frizinghall’s elderly spinster aunt? What do you say to him, shifting into his German philosophical mode, telling our local lord of the manor, a world famous expert on cattle breeding who was in the middle of ranting about how to raise prize bulls, that all real world experience was worthless, and the only correct way to breed a perfect bull was to sit and meditate until you pulled the ideal idea of a perfect bull out of your own mind? And then, when our county MP got red in the face after the cheese and salad course, sputtering that if we lost our ancient traditional safeguards from the spread of democracy, we’d have nothing left at all—what do you say to Mr. Franklin, leaning over with that cool Italian smile of his, replying, “You are wrong, sir—we would still have three things left: Love, Music, and Salad.” He terrified half the table with those sorts of absurd outbursts, and when his plain English side finally came out, he dropped all his foreign smoothness and started making such scathing jokes ridiculing the entire medical profession that he actually made good-tempered little Mr. Candy fly into a proper rage.

The argument started when Franklin admitted, offhand, that he’d been sleeping very badly lately. Mr. Candy immediately told him his nerves were completely out of order, and he needed a full course of medication right away. Franklin laughed and replied that in his opinion, a course of blind guesswork medicine and stumbling around in the dark were exactly the same thing. Candy snapped back that Franklin was already stumbling around in the dark chasing sleep, and only medicine could show him the path to find it. Franklin shot back that he’d only ever heard the phrase “the blind leading the blind” as an old proverb, and now he was seeing it play out in real life. They went back and forth, trading sharp jabs, until both of them were red in the face—Candy so incensed defending his profession that Lady Verinder was forced to step in and order them to stop arguing at once. That final, public show of authority snuffed out the last remaining flicker of fun the party had left. Conversation sputtered up here and there for a minute or two at a time, but there was no life left in it. The Devil, or that cursed Moonstone, had possessed that entire dinner party—and every last person there was relieved when Lady Verinder rose from her seat, signalling the ladies could leave the gentlemen to their post-dinner wine.

I had just lined all the decanters up in a neat row before old Mr. Ablewhite, who was standing in for the master of the house, when a sound drifted from the terrace that made me drop my serving cloth on the spot. Mr. Franklin and I stared at each other across the table. It was the low thud of an Indian drum. As sure as I live, those wandering jugglers had come back the very second the Moonstone returned to the house!

I hobbled out to the terrace ready to chase them off, but the two Bouncer girls beat me to it. They whizzed out the doors like a pair of fireworks, wild to see the Indians perform their tricks, and the rest of the ladies and gentlemen trotted right after them. Before you could say “good gracious”, the three Indian jugglers were bowing and salaaming to the party, and the Bouncers were fussing over their little boy performer and kissing his cheek. Mr. Franklin moved to one side of Rachel, and I positioned myself right behind her. If our suspicions were correct, she was standing there completely innocent of any danger, showing the Indians the Moonstone glittering on the bodice of her dress for all to see.

I was so flustered by the whole wretched evening and the audacity of those men showing up right when the diamond was out on display that I can barely remember what tricks they performed. The next clear thing I noticed was Mr. Murthwaite, the Indian traveller, gliding around the half-circle of guests to step up quietly behind the jugglers. Without warning, he spoke to them in a low, guttural language none of the rest of us could understand.

If he had driven a bayonet into each of their sides, I doubt the Indians could have spun around to face him with more tiger-quick speed. The very next second, they were bowing and salaaming to him in their most obsequious, snaky polite way, their faces schooled to show nothing. After a minute of quiet back and forth in that unknown tongue, Mr. Murthwaite drifted away as silently as he’d arrived. The lead Indian, who acted as their interpreter, turned back to the party, and I noticed his dark coffee-coloured skin had gone a sickly pale grey from the moment Murthwaite had spoken. He bowed to Lady Verinder, and announced the performance was over. The Bouncer girls were so unutterably disappointed they let out a unified loud, wailing “O!” directed straight at Mr. Murthwaite for cutting their fun short. The little Indian boy went around passing his hat for donations, the ladies trundled back inside to the drawing room, and all the gentlemen save Franklin and Murthwaite headed back in for their wine. I went with one of the footmen to escort the Indians safely off the property.

Heading back to the house through the shrubbery, I caught the sharp smell of tobacco, and found Mr. Franklin and Mr. Murthwaite pacing slowly under the trees, the traveller puffing on a cheroot. Franklin waved me over to join them.

“This is Gabriel Betteredge,” Franklin said, presenting me to Murthwaite. “The old family servant and friend I told you about a little while ago. Tell him, if you please, what you just told me.”

Mr. Murthwaite took his cheroot out of his mouth, leaned wearily against a tree trunk, and spoke. “Mr. Betteredge, those three men out there are no more street jugglers than you or I are.”

That was a shock, of course. I asked the traveller if he’d met these particular men before.

“Never,” he said. “But I know what real Indian street juggling looks like, and what you saw tonight was a clumsy, badly done imitation. Unless decades of wandering the subcontinent has left me completely mistaken, those men are high-caste Brahmins. I accused them of being disguised, and you saw how it shook them—even the most gifted Hindu actors find it almost impossible to hide that kind of shock. They have committed two unforgivable sins against their caste already: first, they crossed the sea, which is strictly forbidden to high caste Brahmins, and second, they disguised themselves as lowly common jugglers. To make that double sacrifice in their home country is unthinkable, unless their motive is extraordinarily serious, and they already have a plan to earn their way back into the good graces of their caste when they return. There is no other reason to do such a thing.”

I was struck completely speechless. Franklin, after fidgeting for a moment, clearly weighing up whether he ought to share the secret family business with a total outsider, broke the silence. “I am loath to share private family matters that are none of your concern, Mr. Murthwaite,” he said, “but after what you just told us, I owe it to Lady Verinder and Rachel to explain the full context, in strict confidence.” He then told the traveller the whole story: the colonel, the diamond stolen from the Hindu temple, the deathbed wish to send it to Rachel for her birthday, the whole mess of it. Even that unflappable Mr. Murthwaite was so fascinated by the story his cheroot went out in his fingers.

“Now,” Franklin said, when he’d finished. “What does your experience tell us we ought to do?”

“My experience,” Murthwaite replied, “tells me you have narrowly escaped death more times over the last few weeks, Mr. Blake, than I have in all my years wandering the most dangerous parts of India—and that is saying something.”

Franklin stared at him, shocked. “Is it really that serious?”

“Deadly serious,” Murthwaite said. “There is no other explanation. Their sacrifice of caste was entirely to recover the Moonstone, so it can be put back on the forehead of their sacred Hindu idol. Those men will wait for their chance with the patience of cats, and strike with the ferocity of tigers the second they see it. I cannot for the life of me work out how you are still alive, after carrying that priceless sacred diamond back and forth between London and this house. Let’s see why you got so lucky. You took the

The whole of that Thursday night dragged by without a single unexpected event to ruffle the house. When Friday dawned, though, two pieces of news broke that settled soft and unsettling over all of us.

First off, the baker’s delivery lad swore blind he’d run into Rosanna Spearman the previous afternoon, wrapped in a thick veil, tramping the moor footpath toward Frizinghall. Now, you’d think no soul in the county could mix Rosanna up with anyone else, not with that twisted, hunched shoulder marking her plain as day, poor girl. But the lad had to be wrong—Rosanna, as we all knew, had been laid up ill in her bedroom the entire Thursday afternoon, never once putting a foot downstairs.

The second bit of news came by way of the postman. That cheery little Mr. Candy, our local doctor, had joked on the birthday night as he drove off through the lashing rain that a doctor’s skin was naturally waterproof. Turns out his skin wasn’t half as waterproof as he claimed. He’d caught a fierce chill that night, and now he was laid low with a raging fever, light-headed in delirium, chattering nonsense as easily as he used to spout silly quips when he was in his right mind. We all felt for the little man, but Franklin Blake seemed far more upset on Rachel’s account than anything else. Over breakfast, right there in front of me while I waited on the table, he told Lady Verinder that if the mystery of the Moonstone didn’t get sorted soon, Rachel was going to fray so bad she’d need the very best medical attention we could get our hands on.

Barely half an hour after breakfast cleared away, a telegram arrived from old Mr. Blake in reply to his son’s earlier note. He told us he’d pulled strings through his friend the Metropolitan Police Commissioner, and tracked down exactly the right man for the job: Sergeant Cuff. He’d be arriving on the morning train from London, due before noon.

Franklin jumped clean out of his chair when he read the Sergeant’s name. Turns out he’d heard wild, hair-raising anecdotes about Cuff from his father’s lawyer back when he was staying in London.
“I’d dare say we’re near the end of all this mess already,” he said, grinning for the first time since the Diamond went missing. “If half the stories are true, there isn’t a man alive in all England better at untangling a messy mystery than Sergeant Cuff.”

Every last one of us was buzzing, impatient to lay eyes on this legendary detective. Superintendent Seegrave, rolling back up to the house right on schedule as he was told, heard the news about Cuff and immediately locked himself in a private room with pen, ink, and paper, scribbling a long, detailed report so he wouldn’t look like a fool in front of the London expert. I’d have happily trundled down to the station myself to meet the Sergeant, but Lady Verinder’s best carriage and horses were far too fancy to waste on a police officer, even the great Cuff, and the small pony chaise was reserved for Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite later that day. Godfrey was gutted he had to leave his aunt mid-crisis, so he’d kindly pushed his departure back to catch the very last train that night, just long enough to hear what the clever London copper made of the case. He had a committee of struggling ladies’ charities waiting to consult him first thing Saturday morning, so he couldn’t put off leaving any longer.

When the train was due, I tramped down to the front gate to keep watch.
A hired fly pulled up the drive just as I reached the lodge, and out stepped a grizzled, elderly man so painfully thin he looked like he didn’t carry a single spare ounce of flesh anywhere on his bones. He was dressed head to toe in sober black, a plain white cravat knotted tight around his neck. His face was sharp as a hatchet, his skin yellow, dry, and withered like an autumn oak leaf. His pale steely-grey eyes had a very disconcerting habit: when they locked onto yours, he looked like he was digging out a secret you didn’t even know you were hiding from yourself. His footsteps were soft as a cat’s, his voice low and mournful, his long lanky fingers hooked like a bird’s claws. He could have passed for a country parson, or an undertaker—any respectable man you could name, except what he really was. You’d hunt the whole country over and never find a man more different from blustery, red-faced Superintendent Seegrave, or less comforting to look at when your whole household is tangled up in a terrible theft.

“Lady Verinder’s house, is this?” he asked.
“Yes, sir.”
“I’m Sergeant Cuff.”
“This way, if you please, sir.”

On the walk up to the manor, I told him my name and my position as the household steward, to let him know he could speak freely about the case with me. Not a single question about the case did he ask, though. He just admired the landscaped grounds, and commented that the sea air off the moors was brisk and refreshing. Privately, I found myself wondering how on earth this quiet, odd fellow had ever earned his famous reputation. We walked up to the house like two strange hounds chained together for the very first time, neither one quite trusting the other.

We asked for Lady Verinder, and found out she was in the back conservatory, so we cut around the side of the house to the gardens, and sent a servant to fetch her. While we waited, Sergeant Cuff peered through the arch of evergreen shrubs on our left, spotted our rose garden, and tramped straight into it, showing the very first spark of genuine interest I’d seen from him yet. To the gardener’s total shock, and my absolute disgust, this famous detective turned out to be a walking encyclopedia of rose-growing trivia.

“Ah, you’ve got the perfect south and south-west exposure here,” the Sergeant said, nodding his grizzled head, a faint little flicker of pleasure warming his mournful voice. “This is exactly the right layout for a rosery—nothing beats a circular bed set inside a square, with wide paths between every bed. But these gravel paths won’t do, my man. Grass walks, gardener—grass between the roses. Gravel’s far too harsh for them. That bed there, mixing white roses and blush pink roses? That’s perfectly lovely, they always complement each other. And here’s the old English white musk rose, Mr. Betteredge—holding its own right alongside all the fanciest new imported varieties. Precious thing,” he said, stroking the musk rose gently with his long hooked fingers, talking to it like it was a small child.

A fine sort of man to get back Rachel’s Moonstone and track down the thief who stole it, I thought to myself.

“You’re very fond of roses, Sergeant?” I asked.
“I haven’t got much spare time to be fond of anything,” he said. “But when I do get a moment of spare affection to dole out, nine times out of ten, it goes to the roses. I grew up running around my father’s nursery garden, and please God, I’ll end my days there too. One of these days, I’ll hang up my thief-catching boots, and spend the rest of my life growing roses. Grass walks, gardener, between every single bed,” he added, still clearly sore about our gravel paths.

“Seems an odd hobby for a man in your line of work, sir,” I dared to say.
“If you bothered to look around you, which most folks won’t,” he said, “you’d see a man’s favourite pastime is almost always the exact opposite of his day job. Show me two things more opposite than a rose and a thief, and I’ll rethink my tastes—though it’s probably too late for me to change them at my age. The damask rose makes a fine sturdy stock for the more tender varieties, right, gardener? I thought so. There’s a lady coming now. That’s Lady Verinder, isn’t it?”

He spotted her before either me or the gardener did, even though we knew exactly which direction to look for her, and he didn’t. That’s when I started to suspect he was a good deal sharper than he’d seemed at first glance.

Either the sight of him, or the news of his errand, put Lady Verinder in a rare fluster. For the first time in all the years I’d known her, she was lost for words meeting a stranger. Sergeant Cuff put her at ease in two seconds flat. He asked if anyone else had been called in to work on the robbery before him, and when he found out Superintendent Seegrave was still on the premises, he asked permission to speak to the Superintendent first before doing anything else at all.

Lady Verinder led the way back to the house. Before he followed her, Cuff paused to get one last word in with the gardener about the paths. “Get her ladyship to let you put down grass,” he said, glowering at the gravel. “No gravel! No gravel at all.”

I can’t explain why Superintendent Seegrave looked two sizes smaller than life the second he was introduced to Sergeant Cuff. It’s just a fact of the day, that’s all. The two of them shut themselves in a room together for what felt like hours, no intruders allowed. When they finally came out, Superintendent Seegrave was bright-eyed and over-excited, and Sergeant Cuff was yawning like he was bored out of his mind.

“The Sergeant wishes to see Miss Verinder’s sitting room,” Seegrave announced to me, puffing out his chest like he was a general addressing a private. “He may have questions to ask. You are to attend him, if you please!”

While he was ordering me around like that, I glanced over at the great Sergeant Cuff. He was staring back at Seegrave with that same quiet, waiting look he’d given everyone else so far. I won’t say I knew for sure he was just sitting there waiting for Seegrave to make a total fool of himself—I’ll only admit I strongly suspected it.

I led the way upstairs. The Sergeant moved softly, checking every inch of the Indian cabinet and the whole of Rachel’s little decorated boudoir, firing off questions—hardly any for Seegrave, almost all for me—that neither of us could make head nor tail of. Before long, he reached the door, and came face to face with that famous decorative paintwork we all knew so well. He laid one lean, inquisitive finger on the tiny smear just below the lock—the very same mark Superintendent Seegrave had noticed when he’d yelled at all the maids for crowding into the room.

“That’s a shame,” Cuff said. “How did this happen?”

He asked me, not Seegrave. I told him the maids had crowded into the boudoir the previous morning, and some of their long petticoats must have brushed the paint and left the mark. “And Superintendent Seegrave kicked them all out before they could do any more damage, sir,” I added.

“Right!” Seegrave barked in his gruff military tone. “I ordered them out. Petticoats did it, Sergeant—plain and simple, the petticoats did it.”

“Did you notice which petticoat made the mark?” Cuff asked me again, still ignoring the Superintendent.

“No, sir.”

He turned to Seegrave then. “And you noticed, I suppose?”

Seegrave looked completely caught off guard, though he tried to recover fast. “I can’t recall it off the top of my head, Sergeant. It’s a mere trifle, after all—just a tiny trifle.”

Sergeant Cuff stared at Seegrave the exact same way he’d stared at the gravel paths in the rosery, and spoke in that same soft mournful voice, giving us the first taste of just how sharp he really was.
“I was working a private inquiry last week, Mr. Superintendent,” he said. “At one end of the case, there was a murder. At the other end, there was a random spot of ink on a tablecloth no one could explain. In all my years working the darkest, messiest corners of this world, I have never once come across such a thing as a trifle. Before we take one more step in this investigation, we are going to find the exact petticoat that made that smear, and we are going to prove for certain exactly when that paint was wet.”

Seegrave took the dressing-down pretty sulkily, and asked if he should round up all the maids for questioning. Sergeant Cuff thought for a full minute, then sighed and shook his head.
“No,” he said. “We’ll sort out the paint timeline first. That’s a simple yes-or-no question, quick work. Questioning all the maids about their petticoats will drag on for hours. What time was it when the servants crowded into this room yesterday morning? Eleven o’clock, was it? Is there anyone in this house who knows for certain whether that paint was wet or dry at eleven o’clock yesterday morning?”

“Mr. Franklin Blake, her ladyship’s nephew, knows,” I said.

“Is the gentleman in the house?”

Franklin was hovering right nearby, waiting impatiently for his chance to meet the famous Sergeant. He was in the room half a second later, and laid out his evidence right away:
“That door, Sergeant,” he said, “Miss Verinder painted it herself, under my eye, with my help, using a special varnish formula I mixed myself. Whatever colours you mix with that varnish dry completely, to the touch, in exactly twelve hours.”

“Do you remember exactly when the smeared patch under the lock was finished, sir?” Cuff asked.
“Perfectly,” Franklin said. “That was the very last little bit of the whole door left to paint. We were determined to finish it Wednesday, and I myself put the final touches to it around three o’clock that afternoon, or not long after.”

“Today is Friday,” Cuff said, turning back to Seegrave. “Let’s do the sums, sir. At three o’clock Wednesday afternoon, that patch of paint was finished. The varnish dries it completely in twelve hours—so that paint was fully dry by three o’clock Thursday morning. At eleven o’clock Thursday morning, you held your little inquiry in this room. Eleven minus three is eight. That paint had been dry for eight whole hours, Mr. Superintendent, when you decided the maids’ petticoats had smeared it.”

That was the first total knockout blow for poor blundering Seegrave. If he hadn’t singled out my daughter Penelope as a suspect earlier, I might even have felt sorry for him.

Once that paint timeline was sorted, Sergeant Cuff clearly wrote Seegrave off as a lost cause completely, and started talking only to Franklin, who was far more use to him.
“You’ve very likely just handed us the whole clue to this mess, sir,” he said.

The second the words left his mouth, the bedroom door flew open, and Rachel Verinder stepped out among all of us without warning.
She stared straight at the Sergeant, acting like she didn’t even notice he was a total stranger.
“Did you say,” she asked, pointing straight at Franklin, “that he had put the clue into your hands?”

“This is Miss Verinder,” I whispered, right behind the Sergeant’s shoulder.

“That gentleman, miss,” Cuff said, those steely grey eyes of his not leaving her face for a single second, “may very well have put the clue into our hands.”

Rachel turned for half a second like she was going to look at Franklin, but she snapped her gaze away again before their eyes could even meet. There was something wild and unsettled running through her. She flushed bright red, then went deathly pale. When the colour drained from her face, there was a new, sharp look in her eyes that made my blood run cold.

“Thank you for answering my question, miss,” Cuff said. “Now I’d like to ask you one in turn. There’s a strange smear on the paintwork of your door, right there. Do you know when it was made? Or who made it?”

Instead of answering him at all, Rachel just kept right on talking, like he hadn’t spoken, like she hadn’t even heard him.
“Are you another police officer, then?” she asked him.
“I’m Sergeant Cuff, miss, of the Detective Police.”
“Do you think a young lady’s advice is worth listening to?”
“I’d be very glad to hear what you have to say, miss.”
“Do your duty all on your own—and don’t take a single bit of help from Mr. Franklin Blake!”

She said those words so spitefully, so viciously, with such a burst of raw, unprovoked anger directed at Franklin ringing in her voice, that even though I’d known her since she was a tiny baby, even though I loved and respected her next to Lady Verinder herself, I was ashamed of her for the very first time in my whole life.

Sergeant Cuff’s unblinking eyes never moved an inch from her face. “Thank you, miss,” he said. “Do you know anything about that smear on the door? Could you have made it by accident yourself?”
“I know nothing at all about the smear.”

With that, she turned on her heel, slammed the bedroom door shut, and locked herself inside. This time, I heard what Penelope had heard the day before: the second she was alone, she burst out sobbing uncontrollably.

I couldn’t bear to look at the Sergeant, so I stared at Franklin, who was standing nearest to me. He looked far more broken and distressed by Rachel’s outburst than I was.
“I told you I was worried about her,” he said. “Now you see why.”

“Miss Verinder’s just a little out of sorts about losing her valuable diamond,” Cuff remarked, calm as anything. “It’s a very expensive jewel. It’s only natural! Perfectly natural!”

That was the exact same excuse I’d made for Rachel, back when she’d snapped at Superintendent Seegrave the day before—and now a total stranger, a man with no personal stake in her reputation, was making the very same excuse for her. A cold, unaccountable shudder ran right through me. I realise now that was the exact moment I felt the first flicker of a terrible, unspoken new suspicion bloom in Sergeant Cuff’s mind—all from what he’d seen Rachel do, and heard Rachel say, in that first strange, hostile meeting.

“A young lady’s tongue is a privileged thing, sir,” Cuff said to Franklin. “Let’s forget that outburst ever happened, and get straight back to business. Thanks to you, we know exactly when that paint went completely dry. The next thing we need to find out is the last time anyone saw that patch of paint without the

I found Lady Verinder in her private sitting room, half-aside with her embroidery, and she started so hard the needle pricked her finger when I told her Sergeant Cuff had asked to speak to her. “Must I see him?” she asked, sharply, like the words pained her. “Can’t you stand in for me, Gabriel?”

I stared, befuddled. I’d never seen her so put out by a guest, much less a man she’d invited into her own house. She softened, and sighed. “My nerves are more frayed than I care to admit. There’s something about that London police officer that makes my skin crawl—I can’t name what it is. I’ve got a fool’s presentiment he’s brought nothing but trouble and misery through our front door with him. It’s not like me, I know. But that’s how it is.”

I had no reply to that. The longer I spent in Sergeant Cuff’s company, the fonder I grew of him, so her sudden, unaccountable dread made no sense to me at all. She rallied quickly, being the high-courage woman I’d served for thirty years. “If I have to see him, I have to. But I won’t face him alone. Bring him in, Gabriel, and don’t leave my side for a single minute he’s here.”

That was the first fit of the megrims I’d seen grip my mistress since she was a girl. I headed back to the boudoir to fetch the Sergeant, and on the way spotted Franklin Blake drifting out into the garden to join Godfrey Ablewhite, whose carriage to the station was due to pull up any minute.

The second Lady Verinder set eyes on Sergeant Cuff, she paled a whole shade lighter. But she held herself steady, and asked if he objected to my presence. She added, quick, that I was no mere old servant, but her trusted adviser on all household matters, the man most useful to consult on anything related to the staff. Sergeant Cuff bowed politely, and said he welcomed my company—he had matters to discuss about the household at large, and my prior experience on the staff had already proven far more helpful than Superintendent Seegrave’s bumbling inquiries.

We pulled up two chairs by the fire, and got straight down to business.
“I’ve already formed a clear opinion on this case,” Sergeant Cuff said, “and I beg your ladyship’s leave to keep it to myself for the time being. All I will lay out now is what I found in Miss Verinder’s sitting room earlier today, and what I propose we do next, with your permission.”

He walked through the story of the wet paint smudge on the boudoir door’s panel, and laid out every conclusion he’d drawn from it, phrased far more respectfully than he’d ever spoken to Seegrave.
“Two things are certain,” he said, to finish. “First: the Moonstone is no longer in the cabinet drawer it was locked in. Second: that smudge of white paint must have rubbed off onto some item of clothing belonging to someone in this house, the night the diamond vanished. We cannot take another single step in this investigation until we find that stained garment.”

“Finding that garment,” my mistress said, sharp, “means finding the thief, I presume?”

“I beg your ladyship’s pardon— I do not say the diamond was stolen. Not yet. I only say it is missing. The stained clothing may well be the lead that takes us straight to where it is, no more.”

Lady Verinder glanced over at me. “Do you follow that, Gabriel?”
“Sergeant Cuff follows it, my lady,” I said.

She turned back to him. “How do you propose to search for this stained item? Superintendent Seegrave already ransacked the boxes and bedrooms of every one of my long-serving, loyal servants. I will not, under any circumstances, have them insulted and suspected a second time.” (There was my mistress! A woman in ten thousand, every inch of her.)

“That is exactly the obstacle I came to you to resolve,” the Sergeant said. “Seegrave has done untold damage to this inquiry already, by letting every servant see he thought them guilty. If I make them feel singled out again, there is no telling what barriers they will throw up in my path—the maids most of all. But we *have* to search all their wardrobes a second time. Seegrave was only looking for the diamond. We are looking for a tiny smudge of paint no one who was wearing the garment that night would even have noticed. I agree completely that their feelings deserve respect. But the search has to happen.”

It looked like a deadlock, plain and simple.

“Luckily, I have a plan to meet that deadlock,” Sergeant Cuff said. “I will tell the entire household that we are searching the wardrobe of every single person who slept under this roof on Wednesday night. From your ladyship, down to the youngest scullery maid. No exceptions.”

I cut in. “The maids will immediately think we are trying to trick them, and suspect them all the more.”
“Not if we frame it as a universal formality, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “The servants will see it as perfectly fair, that their masters and mistresses are submitting to the exact same check as they are. Instead of hiding things, they’ll make a point of honour to help the search along.”

I saw the sense of it at once. So did my lady, after a moment’s thought. “You are certain this is the fastest way to get to the truth?” she asked.
“It is the shortest path I can see, my lady.”

She stood up to ring for her maid, to fetch the keys to her own wardrobe. “You will have full access. Speak to the staff when you are ready, with my own wardrobe keys in your hand.”

Sergeant Cuff stopped her, with a very unexpected question. “Had we not better confirm first that the other ladies and gentlemen staying in the house will consent to the same terms?”

“The only other lady in residence is Miss Verinder,” my lady said, surprised. “The only gentlemen are my nephews, Mr. Blake and Mr. Ablewhite. None of the three will raise the slightest objection.”

I remembered then that Mr. Godfrey was due to leave. Just as the words left my mouth, there was a knock at the door, and Godfrey stepped in to say his goodbyes, with Franklin trailing behind him, ready to walk him to the station. Lady Verinder laid out the plan, and Godfrey agreed at once, calling through the window to his man Samuel to haul his portmanteau back upstairs. He pressed the key to his luggage right into Sergeant Cuff’s palm. “My bags can follow me to London the second the inquiry is done,” he said.

Franklin laughed after him, and told the Sergeant he didn’t even own a locked wardrobe—every stitch of clothing he had was free to look through, no questions asked.

That left only Miss Rachel to give her consent, before we could gather the staff and begin the search.

But before my lady headed upstairs to get Rachel’s keys, Sergeant Cuff made one more request. “If it is convenient, I would like to see the household washing-book first. The stained garment could easily be a piece of linen. If our wardrobe search turns up nothing, I want to cross-reference every item of laundry sent out this week, to see if one piece is unaccounted for. That missing piece will almost certainly be the garment the paint got on, tossed away or destroyed by its owner before it could be sent to the wash. Superintendent Seegrave made another mistake when he herded all the maids into Miss Verinder’s sitting room on Thursday morning, and pointed that paint smudge out to every one of them. That gave whoever was wearing that stained garment more than enough time to get rid of it.”

My lady told me to ring for the washing-book. It was brought in by Rosanna Spearman, our second housemaid. The poor girl looked haggard to the bone, pale as a sheet, after her sudden illness the day before, but she was back to her duties that morning. Sergeant Cuff watched her very closely—studied her face when she stepped through the door, and glanced at her crooked shoulder as she slipped back out.

“Has that girl been in your service as long as the rest of the staff?” he asked.
“Why do you ask?” my lady said.
“The last time I saw her,” Sergeant Cuff said, quiet as anything, “she was in prison, serving a sentence for theft.”

My lady wasted no time telling him the full story, how she’d hired Rosanna out of a reformatory, that the matron had spoken highest of her, that she’d been a model servant the whole time she’d worked in the house. “You do not suspect her, I hope?” she asked, earnest as anything.

“I already told your ladyship,” Cuff said, “that I do not suspect anyone of theft, as of yet.”

My lady wasted no time then, heading upstairs to get Rachel’s keys. Cuff beat me to opening the door for her, and gave her a deep, formal bow. She shuddered as she walked past him, like he’d passed a cold hand over her shoulder.

We waited. And waited. No keys came. Sergeant Cuff said nothing at all. He turned his long, lean frame to the window, stuffed his lanky hands in his pockets, and whistled “The Last Rose of Summer” soft and low, that melancholy little tune he was so fond of.

Finally Samuel stepped in, not with a set of keys, but a crumpled scrap of pencil note for me. I fumbled my spectacles on, and read it. My lady had written that Rachel had flatly, unconditionally refused to let anyone examine her wardrobe. They’d pressed her for reasons, and she’d burst into tears. Pressed a second time, she’d only said: “I won’t, because I won’t. You can use force on me if you like, but I will consent to nothing less.”

I could not blame my lady for not wanting to face Cuff with that ridiculous, impossible answer. I’d have been red as a beet to stand in front of him with that sort of nonsense, myself.

“Any sign of Miss Verinder’s keys?” the Sergeant asked.
“Miss Rachel refuses to let anyone look through her things,” I said.

“Ah,” he said. The word was soft, but there was no surprise in it at all. It was the voice of a man who’d been waiting to hear exactly that.

“Does that mean we have to give up the search entirely?” I asked.
“It has to be given up,” he said. “We cannot search half the house and leave Miss Verinder’s things untouched. It is all, or nothing. Send Mr. Ablewhite’s portmanteau to London on the next train, and return the washing-book to the young woman who brought it, with my compliments and thanks.”

He laid the washing-book on the table, pulled out his penknife, and started trimming his nails, calm as anything.
“You don’t look even a little bit disappointed,” I said.
“I am not,” he said.

I tried to press him for answers. “Why on earth would Miss Rachel put obstacles in your way? Getting that diamond back is her own best interest, is it not?”

“Wait a little, Mr. Betteredge,” he said, closing his penknife with a sharp click. “Wait a little.”

I was too loyal to Miss Rachel to guess what he was driving at, too blind to the ugly lines of the case that were starting to creep into view. Lady Verinder’s strange dread of him, I realized later, had been her own half-glimpse of that truth, dim as a shape seen through a fogged window. I had no clue what he was thinking.

“What do we do next?” I asked.

Sergeant Cuff stood up, and nodded towards the garden door.
“Come outside with me,” he said. “Let’s go have a look at the roses.”

The fastest route from Lady Verinder’s sitting room out to the gardens cuts straight through that familiar shrubbery path—Mr. Franklin Blake’s favourite walk, the spot we always found him loitering if he’d vanished from the rest of the grounds. Now, I’m an obstinate old man, I’ll own it: the more Sergeant Cuff clamped his thoughts shut tight against me, the harder I pried to peek inside his head. We’d barely turned onto the path before I tried another trick to draw him out.
“At this point,” I said, “if I was in your boots, I’d be completely at my wits’ end.”
“If you were in my boots,” the Sergeant corrected, not even pausing in his steady trudge, “you’d already have settled on your theory—and every last lingering doubt about those conclusions would be long gone. Don’t pester me for details just yet, Mr. Betteredge. I didn’t drag you out here to be badgered like a cornered badger. I brought you out for information. We could’ve talked inside, sure, but doors and curious listeners have a nasty habit of pairing up. In my line of work, we develop a very healthy taste for fresh open air.”
Who could outmanoeuvre a man like that? I surrendered, and waited as patiently as I could for whatever he’d say next.
“We won’t waste time unpacking Miss Verinder’s strange reasons for refusing to help this investigation,” he went on. “All that matters is that her refusal makes this whole job twice as hard as it needs to be. We’ve got to solve the mystery of that paint smear on the door—and take my word for it, solving that smear means solving the Moonstone mystery right along with it—another way. I’ve decided interviewing the servants, digging into their recent actions and moods, will serve me better than rummaging through all their wardrobes. Before I start, though, I’ve got a few questions. You’re a sharp observer, did you notice anything odd about any of the staff, allowing for all the natural panic and shock after the Diamond went missing? Any fights between them? Anyone far off their usual temperament—suddenly snappish, or suddenly taken ill out of nowhere?”
I’d just barely registered that he was describing Rosanna Spearman’s sudden, mysterious illness at dinner the night before, and was about to say so, when Sergeant Cuff’s head snapped sideways toward the bushes. He muttered a soft, under-his-breath “Hullo!”
“What’s wrong?” I asked.
“Twinge of rheumatism in my back,” he replied, loud enough that anyone hidden nearby would clearly hear. “We’re due for a change in the weather soon, mark my words.”
A few more steps brought us to the corner of the house. We turned sharp right onto the terrace, descended the central steps, and stepped out onto the open lawn, where we could see every inch of the surrounding grounds clear as day. Cuff stopped there.
“About that young maid, Rosanna Spearman,” he said. “She’s not what you’d call a pretty girl, so it’s not very likely she’s got a sweetheart hanging around. But for the girl’s own sake, I’ve got to ask you straight: has she set her heart on any boy around the place, like all the other servants do eventually?”
I stared at him, completely bewildered. What on earth made him ask that kind of question at a time like this?
“I saw Rosanna hiding in the shrubbery as we walked past,” he explained.
“When you said ‘Hullo’?”
“That’s right. If she was there lying in wait for a sweetheart, her hiding’s no harm done. But if there’s no sweetheart in the picture? With all the weird business going on in this house? That hiding is a deeply suspicious circumstance. It’d be my painful duty to act on it accordingly.”
I was trapped. I knew perfectly well this shrubbery was Mr. Franklin’s favourite walk. I knew he’d most likely head straight there when he got back from the station. I knew my daughter Penelope had caught Rosanna lingering around that path a dozen times, swearing up and down the quiet little maid was foolishly trying to catch Mr. Franklin’s eye. If Penelope was right, Rosanna had just been lying in wait for him when the Sergeant spotted her. I could either repeat Penelope’s silly sounding gossip and clear her name, or leave the poor unfortunate girl to face the full weight of Sergeant Cuff’s suspicion. Out of pure, unadulterated pity for her soul, I told him the whole truth: Rosanna Spearman had been mad enough to fall hopelessly in love with Mr. Franklin Blake.
Sergeant Cuff never laughed. The only sign he was amused, on the rare occasions something caught his fancy, was a tiny little curl at the corner of his mouth. He curled that corner now.
“Might as well call it what it is,” he said dryly. “Ugly, low-ranking servant girl pining for a gentleman. Falling for a man as charming and handsome as Mr. Franklin Blake isn’t the mad part of her behaviour, far from it. I’m glad we got that sorted out, anyway. It’s a relief to clear up a loose end. And I’ll keep your secret, Mr. Betteredge. I’m always tender to human foolishness—though I don’t get many chances to exercise that kind of charity in my line of work. You’re sure Mr. Franklin hasn’t the faintest clue the girl’s got a crush on him? Ah. He would’ve figured it out fast enough if she’d been pretty. Ugly women get a terrible deal in this life. Let’s hope they get rewarded for it in the next. Nice garden you’ve got here, perfectly kept lawn. Look how much better the roses look growing in grass instead of gravel. No, thank you, I won’t pick one. Hurting a flower that’s perfectly happy on its stem pains me. Just like it pains you to see something going very wrong in the servants’ hall, right? You didn’t notice anything you couldn’t explain in any of the servants the moment they first found out the Diamond was gone?”
Up until that second, I’d got on splendidly with Sergeant Cuff. But that sly, seamless way he slipped that question right back in made my hackles go up. I did not relish the thought of helping him sneak around prying into my fellow staff’s private business like a snake in the grass.
“I saw nothing,” I said, keeping my face as steady as I could. “We all lost our heads that night, me included.”
“Oh,” he said, his deadpan eyes pinning me right to the spot. “That’s all you’ve got to tell me, is it?”
“That’s all.”
“Mr. Betteredge,” he said, holding out his hand. “Would you oblige me by shaking hands? I’ve taken an extraordinary liking to you.”
I can’t for the life of me explain why he chose the exact second I was lying through my teeth to give me that mark of approval. I was downright proud of myself! I really thought I’d finally outwitted the famous Sergeant Cuff.
We headed back to the house. The Sergeant asked me to set aside a private room for him, then send all the indoor servants in to see him one by one, ordered by their rank, from highest to lowest. I showed him straight into my own quarters, then called the whole staff to assemble in the hall. Rosanna was there, looking as calm and ordinary as could be. I suspected she’d overheard him talking to me about interviewing the staff right before he spotted her hiding in the bushes, because she acted like she’d never set foot in that shrubbery in her whole life.
I sent them in one after another, just as he’d asked.
The cook went first, stayed barely five minutes, and came out reporting that Sergeant Cuff was a gloomy sort, but a perfect gentleman.
Lady Verinder’s own maid went next, stayed far longer, and stormed out huffing that if the Sergeant didn’t believe a respectable woman, he could at least keep his rude opinions to himself.
Penelope popped in after her, stayed only a moment, and whispered to me on her way out that the poor man must have been cruelly jilted in his youth, no other explanation for that permanent sour face.
The first housemaid went in after Penelope, stayed almost as long as Lady Verinder’s maid, and came out fuming that she hadn’t taken a job in this household to be openly doubted by some lowly police officer.
Rosanna Spearman went in next. She stayed longer than all the rest put together. When she walked back out, she didn’t say a single word. Her lips were pale as ash.
Samuel the footman followed her, stayed a minute or two, and snarked that whoever polished Sergeant Cuff’s boots ought to be ashamed of themselves for doing such a bad job.
Little Nancy the kitchen maid went last, stayed just a couple minutes, and came out grinning that Sergeant Cuff had a heart, unlike everyone else in the house, he never made cruel jokes at poor overworked girls’ expense.
When all the interviews were done, I walked into my room to see if he had any further orders for me, and found Sergeant Cuff at his old familiar trick: staring out the window, whistling *The Last Rose of Summer* under his breath.
“Any discoveries yet, sir?” I asked.
“If Rosanna Spearman asks permission to go out for a walk,” he said, “let the poor girl go. But come tell me first, before you say yes.”
I might as well have kept my mouth shut about Rosanna and Mr. Franklin! It was obvious the unfortunate girl was already deep under Sergeant Cuff’s suspicion, no matter how hard I’d tried to protect her.
“I hope you don’t believe Rosanna had any hand in stealing the Moonstone?” I ventured to say.
The corners of his sad, droopy mouth curled up again, and he stared me dead in the face the same way he had in the garden.
“I think I’d better not tell you, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “You might lose your head, you know, for the second time.”
Suddenly I wasn’t nearly so sure I’d outsmarted the great Sergeant Cuff after all. A knock at the door interrupted us. It was the cook’s messenger, come to say Rosanna had just asked to go out, complaining of a headache and saying she needed fresh air. I told her yes, following Cuff’s nod.
“Which way is the staff exit out of the house?” he asked once the messenger had left. I showed him. “Lock the door to your room,” he ordered. “And if anyone asks where I am, tell them I’m inside meditating to calm my mind.” He gave that tiny, secret curl of a smile again, and slipped out the door.
Left alone, my curiosity was eating me alive. It was obvious the Sergeant’s suspicions about Rosanna had come from something he’d heard during those interviews.
