The only two people besides Rosanna who’d stayed in that room longer than a few minutes were Lady Verinder’s maid and the first housemaid—the two spiteful women who’d made Rosanna’s life a misery ever since she arrived at the house. I strolled casually into the servants’ hall where the pair were sitting down to tea, and invited myself to join them. Everyone knows a good cup of tea to a woman’s tongue is what a drop of oil is to a sputtering lamp. It didn’t fail me. In half an hour, I knew everything Sergeant Cuff knew.
The two maids never for a second bought Rosanna’s story about being sick in her room all afternoon the day the Diamond vanished. They’d snuck up to her room at intervals, found her door locked, knocked and got no answer, pressed their ears to the wood and heard no sound. When she’d come down later for tea, still claiming she was ill, and gone back up to bed, they’d checked her door again. It was still locked. The keyhole was stuffed full of paper. They’d seen a light glinting under her door at midnight, and heard a fire crackling in her room at four o’clock in the morning—for heaven’s sake, in the middle of June, when no soul lights a bedroom fire! They’d spilled the whole story to Sergeant Cuff, who’d stared at them both with such sour, distrustful looks that they were absolutely convinced he didn’t believe a word of it. That’s why they’d all come out of the interviews so angry and offended, and why their tongues were loose enough to spill every last detail to me over teacups. The Sergeant had pretended to disbelieve them on purpose, of course, to keep their overinflated egos from running their mouths and tipping Rosanna off that they’d ratted on her.
I wandered out into the soft summer afternoon, miserable for the poor girl, and deeply uneasy about where this whole mess was heading. A little later, drifting toward the shrubbery, I ran straight into Mr. Franklin. He’d just got back from dropping Rachel off at the station, and had spent a long closed-door talk with Lady Verinder. She’d told him all about Rachel’s inexplicable refusal to let anyone search her wardrobe, and he was so low-spirited he could barely bring himself to talk about it. For the first time in all the months I’d known him, that brooding, stubborn family temper was written all over his face.
“Well, Betteredge,” he said, glum. “How do you like living surrounded by this whole atmosphere of mystery and suspicion? Remember the morning I first turned up here with the Moonstone? I wish to God we’d hurled it straight into the Frizinghall quicksand the second we set eyes on it.”
He didn’t speak again for a minute or two after that, until he’d calmed himself down. We walked side by side in silence for a little while, then he asked where Sergeant Cuff had got to. I couldn’t very well lie and tell him the Sergeant was locked in my room meditating. I told him exactly what had happened, every detail, including the two maids’ story about Rosanna’s locked room and the midnight fire.
Mr. Franklin’s sharp mind clicked straight to the answer in a blink.
“Didn’t you tell me this morning,” he said, “that one of the local tradesmen swore he’d met Rosanna on the path to Frizinghall that same day, when we all thought she was sick in bed?”
“Yes, sir.”
“If those two maids are telling the truth, then the tradesman definitely saw her. That illness of hers was just a ruse to sneak out of the house. She had a guilty reason to go to town secretly. The paint-stained nightgown the Sergeant is hunting for is hers. That fire they heard crackling at four a.m. was her burning it to get rid of the evidence. Rosanna Spearman stole the Moonstone. I’m going straight in to tell my aunt what we’ve worked out.”
“Not just yet, if you please, sir,” a sad quiet voice said right behind us.
We both spun around. Sergeant Cuff was standing there, looking as calm and unflappable as ever.
“Why not just yet?” Mr. Franklin demanded, his temper flaring instantly.
“Because if you tell Lady Verinder,” Cuff said evenly, “Lady Verinder will tell Miss Verinder. And that’s the last thing we can afford to have happen right now.”
Mr. Franklin stepped close to the Sergeant, face blazing with anger. “Suppose she does tell her. What then?”
“Do you think it’s wise, sir,” Cuff said, not moving an inch, “to ask me that kind of question at a time like this?”
There was a long, heavy silence between them. They stared each other straight in the eye. Mr. Franklin dropped his voice all at once, suddenly sharp and careful.
“I suppose you know, Mr. Cuff,” he said, “that you are treading on very delicate ground here?”
“Wouldn’t be the first time, not by many hundreds,” Cuff replied, as unshakable as a stone. “You need to understand this: if you tell Lady Verinder, or anyone else, what we’ve worked out before I give you permission, I throw up the case immediately and walk off the job.”
There was no arguing with that. Mr. Franklin had no choice but to submit. He turned on his heel, stormed off in a fury, and left the two of us standing there. I was trembling all over, completely confused, no idea who to suspect or what to believe next. But two things were perfectly clear to me: first, that Miss Rachel was somehow, in some unaccountable way, at the very root of that sharp, unspoken standoff between them, and second, that they’d understood each other perfectly without saying a single word of extra explanation out loud.
“Mr. Betteredge,” Cuff said, taking me by the arm, “you’ve done a very foolish thing while I was gone. You’ve been doing a little amateur detective work of your own. From now on, be so kind as to do your detective work right alongside me.”
I shook his arm off and stopped short. Thief or no thief, I pitied Rosanna Spearman, and I wasn’t about to help him set traps for her. “What do you want from me?” I asked.
“Just a little information about the local countryside,” he said. I couldn’t very well refuse to give him directions. “Is there a footpath from this house that leads straight down to the sea beach?” He pointed toward the fir plantation that ran straight to the Shivering Sand.
“Yes,” I said. “There’s a path.”
“Show it to me.”
Side by side, as the grey of the summer evening crept over the grounds, Sergeant Cuff and I set off toward the Shivering Sand.

We found Lady Verinder sat in a room lit by nothing but a single reading lamp, its shade twisted low to cast her whole face in deep shadow. Unlike her usual frank, direct way of greeting us, she huddled close to the table, her eyes fixed stubbornly to the pages of an open book.
“Officer,” she said, “does it matter to the investigation you are running, to know in advance if anyone in this house plans to leave?”
“Most important, my lady,” Sergeant Cuff replied.
“Then I must tell you: Miss Verinder means to go and stay with her aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite, in Frizinghall. She has arranged to leave first thing tomorrow morning.”
Sergeant Cuff turned his gaze straight to me. I took a step forward to speak for my mistress, and then—ashamed as I am to own it—my heart failed me entirely, and I stepped back again, not a single word passing my lips.
“Might I ask your ladyship when Miss Verinder told you of this plan to visit her aunt?” the Sergeant inquired.
“About an hour ago,” Lady Verinder answered.
Cuff looked at me a second time. They say old men’s hearts don’t stir easy, but mine was hammering so hard I swear it would have burst if I was still five and twenty!
“I have no right, my lady,” said Cuff, “to control Miss Verinder’s actions. All I ask is that you put off her departure, if you can, till later in the day. I must travel to Frizinghall myself tomorrow morning, and I will be back no later than two o’clock. If Miss Verinder can be kept here until then, I would like two unexpected words with her before she goes.”
Lady Verinder ordered me to tell the coachman not to bring the carriage round for Miss Rachel until two in the afternoon. “Have you anything more to say?” she asked the Sergeant once the instruction was given.
“Only one thing, your ladyship. If Miss Verinder is surprised by the change in plans, do not tell her I am the reason her trip is delayed.”
My mistress’s head snapped up off her book, as if she was about to burst out with something unguarded—then she forced herself to clamp down hard on the words, fixed her eyes back on the open page, and waved us both off with a sharp flick of her hand.
“That is a remarkable woman,” Cuff said once we were out in the hall. “If not for her iron self-control, the whole mystery that has stumped you, Mr. Betteredge, would have been solved tonight.”
At those words, the truth finally crashed through my thick old head. I think I must have gone clean mad for a minute: I seized Cuff by the collar of his coat and pinned him hard against the wall. “Damn you!” I shouted. “There is something wrong with Miss Rachel—and you have been hiding it from me this whole time!”
Sergeant Cuff stared up at me, flat to the wall, not moving a single muscle of his melancholy face, not lifting a hand to push me off. “Ah,” he said, quiet as ever. “You guessed it at last.”
My hands fell away from his collar, and my head sank to my chest. Bear with my outburst, won’t you? I served this family for fifty years. Miss Rachel used to climb right up on my knees and yank at my whiskers when she was tiny. For all her sharp little ways, she was the dearest, prettiest, best young mistress an old servant could ever hope to love. I apologized to Sergeant Cuff, though I’m afraid my eyes were streaming, and I cut a very unseemly figure doing it.
“Do not distress yourself, Mr. Betteredge,” Cuff said, kinder than I had any right to expect. “In my line of work, if we took offence easy, we would not be worth the salt on our porridge. If it helps you work off your temper, grab my collar again. You do not do it half right, but I will overlook the clumsiness for the sake of your feelings.” The corners of his mouth twitched up, his own dreary version of a joke he was very proud of.
I led him to my small sitting room and shut the door. “Tell me the truth, Sergeant,” I said. “What do you suspect? There is no kindness in hiding it from me now.”
“I do not suspect,” Cuff said. “I know.”
My bad temper flared up again before I could stop it. “Do you mean to tell me plainly,” I snapped, “that Miss Rachel stole her own Diamond?”
“Yes,” he said. “That is exactly what I mean. Miss Verinder has kept the Moonstone in her secret possession from the very first, and she drew Rosanna Spearman into her confidence, counting on us to suspect the poor maid of the theft. That is the whole case, wrapped up tight. Go on, grab my collar again, if it makes you feel better.”
God help me, that was not the sort of comfort I needed. “Give me your reasons,” was all I could choke out.
“You will hear them tomorrow,” Cuff said. “If Miss Verinder refuses to put off her trip to her aunt—and you will find that she will refuse— I will have no choice but to lay the full case before your mistress. I do not know what will come of that, so I want you there, present, to hear every word that is said from both sides. Leave the matter rest tonight. You will not get another single word about the Moonstone out of me. Now look, your supper is laid out on that table. I am a man who treats that particular human frailty very tenderly. Ring the bell, and I will say grace. ‘For what we are about to receive——’”
“Enjoy your supper, Sergeant,” I said. “My own appetite is gone entirely. I will see you served with the best of everything, then you will excuse me if I step out and try to bear this misery on my own.”
I made sure he had every good thing on the table, and I will own I half wished the lot of it would choke him. The head gardener, Mr. Begbie, wandered in at that very moment with his weekly accounts, and Cuff immediately steered the conversation round to roses, arguing the merits of grass paths over gravel walks before they dived headlong into a shouting match about white moss roses, and whether they needed to be budded to dog-rose stock to grow properly. Mr. Begbie swore you had to do it, Cuff swore you did not, and they appealed to me like a pair of brawling boys. I know nothing at all about growing roses, so I took the neutral path judges take when the scales of justice hang perfectly even. “Gentlemen,” I said, “there is much to be said on both sides.”
In the quiet lull that impartial line bought me, I laid Lady Verinder’s written note down on the table under Cuff’s nose. He read it in half a minute, and was immediately sharp and focused again: he recalled Superintendent Seegrave’s report, fished out the part that spoke of the three Indian jugglers, and asked me for the name and address of the famous traveller who knew the Indian languages and customs that Seegrave had mentioned. I wrote the details on the back of my lady’s note, and Cuff said he would track the man down first thing when he went to Frizinghall in the morning.
“Do you expect that to turn up anything?” I asked. “Superintendent Seegrave swore those Indians were as innocent as unborn babies.”
“Superintendent Seegrave has been wrong about every single conclusion he has drawn so far,” Cuff said. “It is well worth finding out if he was wrong about the Indians, too.” With that, he turned right back to Mr. Begbie and picked up the rose argument exactly where he left off. I shut the door on them, and left them to it.
I met Penelope loitering in the passage, waiting for Miss Rachel to ring for her so she could finish packing for the trip. She told me Rachel had declared the house was utterly unendurable now there was a policeman under the same roof, that she could not stand his presence for another hour. When she heard her departure was delayed till two, she flew into a screaming, violent passion. Lady Verinder rebuked her sharply, then sent Penelope out of the room, having some private thing to say to her daughter that no one else could hear. My girl was low as mud. “Nothing is right, father,” she said. “Nothing is the way it used to be. I feel like some terrible misfortune is hanging over all of us.”
I felt the exact same way, but I put a brave face on it for her. Miss Rachel’s bell rang just then, and Penelope ran up the back stairs. I headed for the hall to check the glass to see if the weather was shifting, and just as I reached the swing door leading from the servants’ quarters, it flew open violently from the other side. Rosanna Spearman ran right past me, her face streaked with pain, one hand clamped hard to her heart like a red hot pang had stabbed her through there.
“What is the matter, my girl?” I asked, stopping her. “Are you ill?”
“For God’s sake, do not speak to me,” she gasped, twisting herself right out of my grip and running for the servants’ staircase. I called out to the cook, who was nearby, to go look after her. Two other people had heard the ruckus too: Sergeant Cuff slipped soft as a shadow out of my sitting room and asked what the noise was, and I told him it was nothing. Then Mr. Franklin pulled open the swing door, and beckoned me into the hall, asking if I had seen Rosanna.
“I did, sir,” I said. “She just passed me, looking distraught, acting very strange.”
“I am afraid I am the innocent cause of that upset, Betteredge,” he said. “I cannot explain it, but if the girl is mixed up in the loss of the Diamond, I swear to you she was two minutes away from confessing everything to me, of all people, not two minutes ago.”
As he said that, I thought I saw the swing door creak open a tiny crack from the other side. Someone was listening. The door clicked shut before I could reach it, and I caught a glimpse of the hem of Sergeant Cuff’s neat black coat vanishing round the far corner of the passage. I did not want to stir up more mischief than we already had, so I told Mr. Franklin it was only one of the dogs that had wandered in, then asked him what had happened between him and Rosanna.
He pointed to the billiard table. “I was knocking the balls about,” he said, “trying to get this whole miserable Diamond mess out of my head. I looked up, and there was Rosanna Spearman standing right beside me, like a ghost. I was so startled I did not know what to do at first. She looked so anxious, I asked if she wanted to speak to me. She said, ‘Yes, if I dare.’ I knew the suspicion hanging over her, so I felt awkward, did not want to push her. I held my billiard cue and kept tapping at the balls to make the situation less awkward, and I think I mortified her without meaning to. She turned away, and I heard her whisper, ‘He would rather look at billiard balls than look at me!’ and before I could stop her she was gone. Tell her I meant no unkindness, will you? And if she still wants to speak, no matter what trouble I get into, send her to me in the library.”
I went to the servants’ quarters, and found Rosanna had locked herself in her room, turned down every offer of help, and only asked to be left alone to rest. I reported back to Mr. Franklin, who went up to bed. I was putting out the last of the lights and latching the windows when Samuel came to tell me the gardener had gone home, and Sergeant Cuff was nowhere to be found downstairs.
I went up the stairs to check if he had turned in to the bedroom we had prepared for him. Halfway up the second landing, I heard quiet, steady breathing coming from the corridor that led straight to Miss Rachel’s bedroom. There he was: curled up on three chairs pushed tight across the passage, a red handkerchief tied round his grizzled head as a pillow, his black coat rolled up under his neck. He woke instantly, quiet as a hound, the moment I stepped near.
“Good night, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “And mind, if you ever take to growing roses, the white moss rose grows far better if you do not bud it on the dog-rose, no matter what the gardener says.”
“What are you doing here?” I asked. “Why are you not in your proper bed?”
“I am not in my proper bed,” Cuff said, “because this miserable world is full of people who cannot earn their money easily and honestly at the same time. Rosanna Spearman came back from the Shivering Sand tonight, exactly the same hour Miss Verinder announced she was leaving the house. Whatever Rosanna hid, it is plain to me Miss Rachel could not run off until she knew it was hidden safely. The two of them have already communicated once in secret tonight. If they try to meet again when the whole house is asleep, I want to be right here in their path to stop it. Do not blame me for spoiling your sleeping arrangements, Mr. Betteredge. Blame the Moonstone.”
“I wish to God the Moonstone had never stepped foot in this house!” I burst out.
Sergeant Cuff looked down ruefully at his lumpy three-chair bed, and nodded, dead serious. “So do I,” he said.

The night passed without a single incident, and I was heartily glad to note that Sergeant Cuff’s unblinking vigilance had caught no sign of any secret meeting between Miss Rachel and Rosanna Spearman.

I’d fully expected the Sergeant to set off for Frizinghall at first light, but he lingered about the house, loitering by the doors and pausing to stare off down the paths as if he was waiting for something specific to turn up. I left him to his own devices, of course, and wandered out to the grounds not long after, where I ran straight into Mr. Franklin on his usual favourite walk along the edge of the shrubbery. We’d barely exchanged two words when Sergeant Cuff stepped out from behind a hedge, joining us unannounced. Mr. Franklin received him haughtily, I won’t deny it — all he got for a polite good morning was a sharp, “Have you anything to say to me?”
“I do, sir,” Sergeant Cuff replied, calm as ever. “It touches the theft inquiry I’m running here. You worked out yesterday exactly where this line of questioning was leading. It’s only natural, in your position, that you’d be shocked and distressed, and that you’d take your anger at the family scandal hanging over this house out on me.”
“What do you want?” Mr. Franklin cut him off, short and sharp.
“I want to remind you, sir, that up to this point, I have not been proven wrong. Bear that in mind, along with the fact I am an officer of the law, acting with the full sanction of the lady of this house. Under those circumstances, is it not your duty as a good citizen to share any special information you might hold that could help this inquiry along?”
“I have no special information,” Mr. Franklin said flatly.
Sergeant Cuff brushed that answer off like it had never been spoken. “You could save me a whole lot of wasted time running inquiries out of the house, if you’d choose to understand what I’m saying and speak plainly.”
“I don’t understand you,” Mr. Franklin snapped back. “I have nothing to say.”
“One of the female servants — no names needed — spoke to you in private last night, sir.”
Again Mr. Franklin cut him short. Again he said, “I have nothing to say.”
Standing silent by their side, I thought back to the swing door I’d seen shifting last night, and the flash of coat-tails I’d spotted vanishing down the back passage. No doubt Sergeant Cuff had got within earshot of that private conversation between Mr. Franklin and Rosanna before I’d interrupted him, and suspected the girl had unburdened at least part of her secret to Mr. Blake.
The thought had barely crossed my mind when who should appear at the far end of the shrubbery walk but Rosanna Spearman herself, with Penelope right on her heels, trying her hardest to tug her back to the house. Rosanna froze when she saw Mr. Franklin was not alone, clearly at a total loss for what to do next, and Penelope hung back a step behind her. Mr. Franklin spotted the two girls the same instant I did. Sergeant Cuff, that wily old fox, acted like he hadn’t noticed them at all.
It all happened in a single breath. Before either Mr. Franklin or I could speak, Sergeant Cuff struck in smoothly, raising his voice loud enough to carry all the way down the path, as if he was just continuing our earlier conversation.
“You needn’t fear harming the girl, sir,” he said, pointedly. “Quite the opposite, in fact. I’d recommend you trust me with what you know, if you hold any regard at all for Rosanna Spearman.”
Mr. Franklin caught the trick at once. He also acted like he’d not seen the two girls, and shouted back just as loud, “I take no interest whatever in Rosanna Spearman.”
I glanced down the path. The second those words left Mr. Franklin’s mouth, Rosanna spun on her heel. Where she’d been fighting Penelope to get to Mr. Franklin just a moment before, she now let my daughter slip her arm through hers and lead her back to the house without a single protest.
The breakfast bell rang the second the two girls vanished around the corner, and even Sergeant Cuff had to admit his trap had fallen flat. He leaned in to murmur to me, “I’m heading to Frizinghall now, Mr. Betteredge. I’ll be back before two.” He turned and left without another word, and for a few blessed hours, we were well rid of him.
“You’ll have to make this right with Rosanna for me,” Mr. Franklin said, once we were alone. “I seem fated to say the most awkward, hurtful things to that poor girl for no reason. You saw exactly what that Sergeant did — he laid a trap for both of us. If he could rattle me, or push her into a sudden outburst, either one of us might have slipped up and said something he could twist to his own ends. On the spur of the moment, that was the only way I could see out of it. It stopped her from stepping forward and saying something she’d regret, and it told that sly detective I saw right through his game. He was listening to us when we spoke last night, Betteredge — I’m sure of it.”
He’d done worse than listen, I thought to myself privately. He’d remembered I’d told him the girl was secretly in love with Mr. Franklin, and he’d calculated exactly what effect mentioning Mr. Franklin’s “regard for her” out loud would have on Rosanna. “As for listening, sir,” I said out loud, keeping that thought to myself, “if this keeps up much longer, we’ll all be in the same boat. Prying, peeping, eavesdropping — that’s all anyone in a house caught in this kind of mess does. A day or two more, and we’ll all go entirely mute, because we’ll all know everyone else is listening in to catch our secrets. Forgive my outburst, sir, but this horrid mystery hanging over the house goes to my head like strong liquor, and makes me half wild. I’ll keep my promise, first chance I get I’ll explain it all to Rosanna.”
“You haven’t said anything to her about last night yet, have you?” Mr. Franklin asked.
“No, sir.”
“Then don’t say anything now. I daren’t risk the girl seeking me out to talk in confidence, not with that Sergeant skulking about waiting to catch us together. My own actions are wildly contradictory, aren’t they? I can see no way out of this mess that isn’t dreadful to think of, short of the Moonstone being traced to Rosanna — and yet I cannot, and will not, help Sergeant Cuff hunt that poor girl down.”
It was unreasonable, no doubt, but it was exactly the way I felt too. I understood him perfectly. If you stop for a moment and admit you’re just a regular mortal man, you’d understand him too.
While Sergeant Cuff was making his way to Frizinghall, the state of the house and grounds was simple enough to sum up. Miss Rachel was still locked tight in her bedroom, stubbornly waiting for the carriage that was to take her to her aunt’s house, refusing to see anyone. My lady and Mr. Franklin breakfasted alone together. After the meal, Mr. Franklin made one of his sudden impulsive decisions, and set off at a quick pace for a long walk to quiet his frayed nerves. I was the only one who saw him leave, and he told me he’d be back long before the Sergeant returned.
The weather we’d seen hints of the night before had broken. Heavy pre-dawn rain had given way to sharp, blustery wind blowing in fresh off the sea. The clouds threatened more rain more than once, but the downpour never came. It was brisk, bracing, perfect walking weather if you were young and strong enough to lean into those big gusts sweeping off the water.
I attended my lady after breakfast, and helped her sort through the household accounts. She only mentioned the Moonstone once, and that was to forbid any talk of it between us for the time being. “We’ll wait till that man gets back,” she said, meaning the Sergeant. “We’ll have to talk of it then. There’s no rule that says we have to talk of it now.”
When I left my mistress, I found Penelope waiting for me in my own room. “Father, I wish you’d come and talk to Rosanna,” she said. “I’m dreadfully worried about her.”
I thought I knew exactly what was wrong, but it’s a firm rule of mine that as the wiser, calmer sex, us men ought to make women explain themselves before we step in to help — it makes them think through their own feelings more clearly, and stops them acting entirely on impulse. Penelope’s reason was plain enough, from her own lips: “I’m afraid Mr. Franklin hurt Rosanna terribly, and never meant to.”
“What was she doing out on the shrubbery walk in the first place?” I asked.
“Madness, that’s all I can call it,” Penelope said. “She was dead set on speaking to Mr. Franklin this morning, no matter what happened. I did everything I could to stop her, you saw that. If only I’d gotten her away before she heard those awful words——”
“Now, now,” I cut in. “Don’t lose your head. I can’t think of a single thing that ought to have alarmed her.”
“It didn’t alarm her, father. But Mr. Franklin said he didn’t care a farthing for her — and he said it in such a cold, cruel voice!”
“He only said it to throw the Sergeant off the scent,” I told her. “I told her that already,” Penelope went on, “but you see — Mr. Franklin’s been disappointing her, making her feel small for weeks and weeks now, and this is the last straw. She’s got no right to expect him to care for her, of course, it’s absurd that she’s forgotten her place like that. But she’s lost all her pride, all her common sense, everything. When Mr. Franklin said those words, father, she turned to stone. She went completely quiet, and she’s been going about all her chores ever since like she’s sleepwalking.”
I started to feel uneasy. Penelope’s account was so sombre it knocked my usual smug sense of being the more sensible sex right out of me. I thought back to the night before, when Rosanna had spoken to Mr. Franklin, looking already like her heart was half broken. Now fate had stung her right on that raw, tender wound again. It was sad — so sad, all the more so because the girl had no business pining after Mr. Franklin, no right to hurt like that.
I’d promised Mr. Franklin I’d speak to her, and this seemed the perfect time to do it. We found her sweeping the corridor outside the bedrooms, pale, neat in her usual plain print dress, as composed as ever. I noticed a strange dim, dull look in her eyes — not the look of someone who’d been crying, but of someone who’d been staring at a distant, invisible thing for hours on end.
“Cheer up, Rosanna!” I said. “Don’t fret over silly fancies. I’ve got a message for you from Mr. Franklin.” I explained the whole trick Sergeant Cuff had pulled, as gently and comfortingly as I could, framing it so she’d understand Mr. Franklin’s cruel-sounding words were never meant for her. For all my strict rules about the fairer sex, when I came face to face with that poor, heartbroken girl, all my gruff principles melted away.
“Mr. Franklin is very kind and considerate. Please thank him for me.” That was all she said. She sounded just as dreamy, far away, as Penelope had described. I doubted she’d fully taken in a word I’d said. “Are you quite sure you understand me, Rosanna?” I asked.
“Quite sure.” She echoed me like a machine, not a living girl, and never stopped sweeping. I took the broom gently out of her hands. “Come now, my girl. This isn’t like you. You’ve got something heavy on your mind. I’m your friend. I’ll stand by you, even if you’ve done something wrong. Come clean, Rosanna — tell me everything.”
A week before, speaking that way to her would have made the tears run down her face. There was no change in her eyes now. “Yes,” she said. “I’ll come clean. To Mr. Franklin.”
I hardly knew what to say. She was in no state to understand Mr. Franklin’s warning that she must not seek him out privately. I told her, carefully, that he’d gone out for a long walk. “It doesn’t matter,” she said. “I won’t trouble Mr. Franklin today.”
“Why not tell my lady, instead?” I pressed. “She’s always been kind to you, merciful and true — she’d help you unburden your mind.”
She stared at me for a long, steady moment, like she was etching my words into her memory. Then she took the broom back out of my hands, and moved slowly a little way down the corridor. “No,” she said, half to herself, as she swept. “I know a better way to unburden my mind than that.”
“What way?”
“Please let me get on with my work.”
Penelope stepped forward to offer to help her sweep. Rosanna turned her down politely, thanked both of us, and kept on working. There was no reaching her. I signed to Penelope to come away with me. We left her there, moving up and down the corridor, sweeping, like a woman caught in a waking dream.
“This is a job for the doctor,” I said. “This is far beyond me.” Penelope reminded me that Mr. Candy was still very ill, laid low by the chill he’d caught the night of the dinner party. His assistant, Ezra Jennings, was around, of course — but no one in these parts knew much about him. Mr. Candy had hired him under odd, unspoken circumstances, and for better or worse, none of us liked or trusted the man. There were other doctors over in Frizinghall, but they were all strangers to our household, and Penelope was sure that throwing a strange doctor at Rosanna in her current state would do her far more harm than good.
I thought about going to my lady, but she was already carrying the terrible weight of the stolen Moonstone and Miss Rachel’s strange behaviour, and I hated to add this new, distressing trouble to her plate. Still, something had to be done — the girl’s state was alarming, and my lady ought to know. I headed for her sitting room, but found it empty. She was shut inside with Miss Rachel, and there was no way I could interrupt them.
I waited in vain till the front staircase clock struck a quarter to two. Five minutes later, I heard my name being called from the drive outside the house. I knew that voice at once. Sergeant Cuff had come back from Frizinghall.

I trundled down the front drive steps only to run straight into Sergeant Cuff, lingering on the threshold. After all the sharp exchanges we’d had lately, my pride rebelled at letting him see I cared a fig for what he was up to — but curiosity burned hot enough to drown out all my good sense. Before I could clamp my mouth shut, the words were out: “Any news from Frizinghall?”

“I’ve questioned the Indian jugglers,” Cuff said, “and I’ve tracked down what Rosanna Spearman bought in town last Thursday, behind everyone’s back. The three of them walk free next Wednesday. Both Mr. Murthwaite and I are in full agreement: they traveled here for no other reason than to steal the Moonstone. Their whole scheme got blown to bits, of course, when the jewel vanished from the house that Wednesday night. They had no hand in the actual theft, no more than you did, Mr. Betteredge. But I can tell you this — if we don’t find that diamond first, they will. You haven’t seen the last of those three charlatans.”

Franklin Blake came back from his stroll just as the Sergeant spoke those startling lines. He mastered his curiosity far better than I had, brushed past us without a single word, and slipped straight into the house. Having already tossed my dignity to the dirt, I decided to get every penny’s worth out of the sacrifice. “That’s the Indians sorted,” I said. “What about Rosanna?”

Sergeant Cuff shook his head. “That mystery’s thicker than ever. I traced her steps to a linen draper’s shop in Frizinghall run by a man named Maltby. She didn’t spend a penny at any other draper, milliner, or tailor in town — all she bought at Maltby’s was a length of plain long cloth, carefully picked to an exact weight and weave, enough fabric to stitch a whole servant’s nightgown.

“My working theory?” he went on. “She snuck down to Miss Verinder’s room between midnight and three a.m. that Thursday, to hide the Moonstone while the whole house slept. On her way back to her own quarters, her nightgown brushed the still-wet fresh white paint on that bedroom door. She couldn’t scrub the stain out, and she couldn’t burn the spoiled gown without drawing attention to the gap in her personal linen inventory — so she bought the cloth to sew a replacement. If she’d been making a new nightgown for Miss Rachel, she’d have needed yards of lace, frills, and trim, and there’s no way she could have finished it all in one night. Plain long cloth means plain servant’s nightgown, no two ways about it. The only puzzle left now is why she hid the stained gown instead of burning it to ash. That secret’s buried at her favorite hiding spot out at the Shivering Sand. Search that stretch of beach, and we’ll have the full truth.”

I asked how he intended to pinpoint her hidden cache. He smiled, a thin, secretive little twitch of the mouth. “Sorry to disappoint, Mr. Betteredge — that’s a secret I’m keeping to myself.” (To spare you the same gnawing frustration I felt, I’ll note here he’d picked up a search warrant in Frizinghall that morning. His years of experience told him Rosanna almost certainly scribbled the exact location of her hiding spot on a scrap of paper, to guide her back even after shifting tides and time had erased her usual landmarks. Get that scrap, and he’d have all he needed.)

“Now, let’s drop speculation and get to work,” he said. “I told Joyce — the Frizinghall policeman Superintendent Seegrave assigned to me — to keep a constant eye on Rosanna. Where is he?”

The hall clock struck two right on cue. The closed carriage meant to take Miss Rachel to her aunt’s house, summoned to beat the incoming rain, rounded the drive right as he spoke. “One thing at a time,” Cuff said, stopping me before I could dash off to find Joyce. “Miss Verinder gets my full attention first.” He waved footman Samuel down from the rumble at the back of the carriage. “You’ll spot a friend of mine waiting in the trees just before the lodge gate,” he told him. “He’ll hop up into the rumble beside you without stopping the carriage. All you have to do is keep your mouth shut and pretend he isn’t there. Step out of line, and you’ll land in hot water.”

I felt my stomach twist right over. They were putting a spy on my young lady! A hidden shadow trailing her in the rumble of her own mother’s carriage. I could have gnawed my own tongue clean off for ever lowering myself to chat with Sergeant Cuff in the first place.

Lady Verinder was already waiting at the top of the steps, arms folded tight in her light garden cloak, not a single word for either of us, standing as still as a marble statue, lips pressed thin, waiting for her daughter. A minute later, Miss Rachel came down the stairs: soft butter-yellow silk jacket that hugged her narrow waist, a neat little straw hat twisted with a white veil, primrose gloves that fit her hands like a second skin, her black hair smooth as satin under the brim, two pearl drops glinting like dew on her shell-pink ears. She moved as straight as a lily stem, as lithe as a half-grown cat — but her face was nothing like the Rachel I knew. Her eyes were bright, sharp, fiercer than I’d ever seen them, her lips bleached completely pale, no trace of her usual smile left. She kissed her mother’s cheek quick, almost roughly, mumbled “Try to forgive me, mamma,” then wrenched her veil down so hard she tore the fabric, bolted down the steps, and threw herself into the carriage like it was a safe hiding place.

Sergeant Cuff moved just as fast. He stepped in front of the open carriage door before she could slam it shut. “What do you want?” Rachel snapped, voice sharp, from behind her veil.

“I only need one word with you before you leave, miss,” Cuff said. “I have no right to stop you visiting your aunt. But I have to tell you plainly: your leaving this house right now is a direct obstacle to me recovering your stolen Moonstone. You know that now. Decide for yourself if you go or stay.”

Rachel didn’t so much as pause to answer. “Drive on, James!” she called to the coachman. Cuff shut the carriage door without another word. Just as the latch clicked, Franklin came sprinting down the steps, hand outstretched. “Goodbye, Rachel!” he called.

“Drive on!” Rachel yelled even louder, ignoring him entirely, same as she’d ignored the Sergeant. Franklin stumbled back, thunderstruck — as well he might. The coachman glanced over at Lady Verinder, who stood on the top step with anger, grief, and raw shame warring openly on her face, and she gave him a sharp nod to start the horses. She turned and hurried back inside before the carriage had even cleared the steps. Franklin called after her, voice raw, that he was leaving too, thanked her for all her kindness. Lady Verinder half-turned to speak to him, then thought better of it, waved a gentle hand. “Come see me before you go, Franklin,” she said, voice breaking, and vanished down the hall to her rooms.

Franklin turned to me, eyes glistening. “Do me one last favor, Betteredge. Get me to the nearest train station as fast as you can.” He walked back into the house, completely unmanned by Rachel’s rejection — proof of just how deeply the boy loved her.

That left Sergeant Cuff and I alone at the foot of the steps. He was staring at the gap in the trees that framed a winding stretch of the carriage drive, hands stuffed in his pockets, softly whistling *The Last Rose of Summer*. “There’s a time and place for everything,” I snapped at him. “This isn’t the time for whistling.”

Right then the carriage came into view through the gap, heading for the lodge. There was a second man in the rumble behind Samuel, plain as day. “All right,” Cuff muttered to himself. He turned back to me. “You’re right. No more whistling. It’s time to press on with this investigation, no soft treatment for anyone. We start with Rosanna Spearman. Where is Joyce?”

We called for him, got no answer. I sent a stable boy to hunt him down. “You heard what I said to Miss Verinder,” Cuff noted while we waited. “You saw how she reacted. I told her leaving would stop me getting her diamond back — and she left anyway. Your young lady’s got a traveling companion riding in that carriage with her, Mr. Betteredge. And its name is the Moonstone.”

I said nothing. I’d have died before I doubted Rachel’s innocence for a second. The stable boy came back, dragging a very pale, trembling Joyce behind him. “Where is Rosanna Spearman?” Cuff asked.

“I can’t explain it, sir — I’m terribly sorry,” Joyce stammered. “I was so careful not to let her spot me following her, and there’s so many winding passages in the lower part of the house —”

“Are you telling me you let her slip right past you?” Cuff cut him off, as calm and flat-voiced as ever.

“I lost track of her nearly an hour ago, sir.”

“Go back to your regular beat in Frizinghall,” Cuff said. “This line of work’s a little too far above your pay grade, Mr. Joyce. Good morning.” The man slunk off, shoulders hunched.

I could barely wrap my head around Rosanna vanishing. My mind was spinning in fifty different directions at once, and I couldn’t find the words to say a single thing. “No, Mr. Betteredge,” Cuff said, like he’d read the exact thought bouncing around in my skull. “Rosanna’s not slipping through my fingers that easy. As long as I know where Miss Verinder is, I can track her accomplice. I stopped them from meeting last night, fine — now they’ll just meet up at her aunt’s house in Frizinghall instead. The investigation’s just moving a little earlier than I planned, from this estate to that house. Right now, I need you to round up all the servants for me.”

The old detective fever hit me like a flash flood. I forgot I hated Sergeant Cuff, grabbed his arm all conspiratorial. “For heaven’s sake, what are you going to do with the servants now?”

The great Cuff froze dead in his tracks, tilted his head up at the sky in a kind of mournful rapture. “If this man only knew how to grow roses,” he said, apparently talking about me, “he’d be the most perfect human being on the face of the earth.” He sighed, tucked his arm through mine, and got back to business. “Rosanna did one of two things: she either headed straight for Frizinghall to meet up with Rachel before I can get there, or she detoured first to the Shivering Sand to dig up her hidden cache. First we need to find out who saw her last before she left the house.”

We questioned every servant we could round up, and found the last person to spot her was Nancy the kitchen maid. Nancy saw Rosanna slip out the back door clutching a letter, stop the butcher’s delivery man just as he dropped off his order, and ask him to post it for her when he got back to Frizinghall. The delivery man pointed out that the address on the envelope, Cobb’s Hole, was far closer than Frizinghall — posting it there meant it wouldn’t be delivered until Monday, since Saturday’s last post run had already gone. Rosanna told him the delay was exactly what she wanted, and made him swear he’d post it anyway. No one in the house saw her after that.

“Well?” I asked, when we were alone again.

“Well,” Cuff said, “I’m heading to Frizinghall. I’m going straight to the post office to check that address. If it’s the one I suspect, I’ll be paying Mrs. Yolland — Rosanna’s old friend who lives right by the Shivering Sand — a visit first thing Monday.”

We headed for the stable yard to order the pony-chaise harnessed. And that’s where a whole new, unforeseen clue about the missing Rosanna Spearman fell right into our laps.

Word of Rosanna Spearman’s disappearance had rippled fast through the house’s outdoor staff. They’d spent the last hour poking through the grounds for clues, and had just collared a scrawny, quick-witted urchin nicknamed Duffy—hired on odd days to weed the garden—who swore he’d seen the missing girl barely half an hour prior. Duffy was dead certain she hadn’t been walking through the fir plantation: she’d been sprinting, flat out, heading straight for the shoreline.
“Does this boy know every inch of this coast?” Sergeant Cuff asked me at once.
“Born and bred on the bay,” I told him. “Knows every tide pool and hidden sandbank better than he knows his own prayers.”
“Duffy,” the Sergeant called, “fancy earning a whole shilling? Then come with me. Keep the pony-chaise hitched and waiting, Mr. Betteredge, till I get back.”
He was off for the Shivering Sand at a lope my seventy-year-old legs could never hope to match. Duffy, true to the feral high spirits of local lads, whooped loud enough to scare every rook out of the fir trees and trotted hot on the detective’s heels.
I can’t rightly parse the restlessness that seized me while they were gone. I fumbled through a dozen meaningless, half-finished chores in and out of the house, none of which I can now recall, my mind buzzing like a hive of angry bees. I had no sense of how much time had passed before Duffy came skidding back down the drive, crumpling a scrap torn out of Cuff’s pocket notebook in his fist. The pencil scribble read: Send me one of Rosanna Spearman’s boots, and hurry.
I dispatched the first maid I spotted to fetch it from Rosanna’s room, then sent Duffy back ahead to say I’d follow with the boot myself. It wasn’t the fastest way to obey Cuff’s order, I’ll own—but I was still stubbornly determined to look after that girl if I could, to see for myself what new dark mystery the detective had stumbled on before I handed over the last of her things. That protective streak, tangled up with the same detective fever that had gripped the whole house for days, had me setting off for the shore at the fastest hobbling run a man of my years could manage.
By the time I hit the dunes, the sky had gone black as pitch. Horizontal rain lashed my face in great stinging sheets, and I could hear the roar of the surf booming over the sandbank at the bay’s mouth long before I caught sight of the water. A little further on, I found Duffy huddled for shelter behind the lee of a sandhill, then spotted Sergeant Cuff: the only dark, solitary figure standing on the empty expanse of storm-lashed beach.
He waved me sharply to the north. “Stay on that hard sand!” he shouted. “Come down to me slow.”
I reached him gasping, chest heaving so hard I could not force a single one of my hundred unspoken questions past my lips. The look on his face curdled my blood: there was raw, unmasked horror in his eyes I’d never seen on that usually stony, composed face before. He snatched the boot out of my hand, pressed it into a half rain-blotted footprint sunk deep in the sand, pointing due south toward the jagged rocky ledge they called the South Spit. It fit to a hair, not a single line of the tread out of place.
Cuff did not speak. He just pointed down at the boot in the print, then turned to follow the track all the way to where the sand met the rock. The South Spit was already half awash with the rising tide, and the glistening, churning surface of the Shivering Sand quicksands heaved higher by the second. Cuff paced back and forth in grim, dogged patience, pressing the boot to every subsequent footprint, but the trail never turned back. There was no sign at all of steps walking away from the Spit.
At last he gave up. He stared first at me, then out at the dark water swallowing the quicksand deeper and deeper, and a cold, dumb, trembling numbness crawled all over my skin. I dropped to my knees in the wet sand.
“She came back to whatever hiding place she’d found,” Cuff muttered to himself. “She must have slipped on the rocks. A terrible accident.”
But I knew better. I replayed the last hour I’d seen Rosanna, blank and slow-faced, sweeping the corridor, her eyes hollow. I remembered her telling me months before that the Shivering Sand felt like it was pulling at her, that she was sure her grave was waiting for her there. My own daughter was just her age—if life had dealt her the same hard, lonely hand Rosanna had drawn, she might have ended up right here too. The thought choked me.
Cuff lifted me gently to my feet, turned me away from the sight of the quicksand, just as all the outdoor servants and the local fisherman Yolland came pelting down the beach toward us, faces white with alarm. In a handful of plain words, Cuff showed them the footprints, laid out what he knew had happened, then turned to Yolland.
“Tell me,” he said, nodding at the raging sea. “Could any boat have reached those rocks to pull her off, in this weather?”
Yolland jerked his chin at the giant piling rollers, sending plumes of foam twenty feet high against the headlands on either side of the bay. “No boat built by mortal man could cut through that surf. She’d be swamped before she got within a hundred yards of the Spit.”
Cuff glanced back at the footprints, which the rain was now fast blurring out of existence. “That’s proof she couldn’t have left by land. That’s proof she couldn’t have escaped by sea.” He paused a minute, working out the timelines. “Rosanna was seen running for this shore an hour ago. How high was the water on the outer side of the Spit—the side not covered by quicksand—at that time?”
“On today’s tide?” Yolland shook his head. “An hour back, there wasn’t enough water there to drown a kitten. Barely ankle deep.”
“On the Shivering Sand side?”
“Less,” the fisherman said. “The quicksands were just barely awash, no deeper than that.”
“Then the accident happened in the quicksand,” Cuff said, turning to me.
My tongue finally came loose. “It was no accident!” I burst out. “She came here because she was sick of her life. She meant to end it here.”
Cuff recoiled, shocked. “How can you be so certain?”
The rest of the men crowded around, but Cuff herded them back gently, said I was an old man, the shock had unhinged me, give me a minute to catch my breath. He turned back to Yolland. “Is there any chance of dragging her back, once the tide goes out?”
“None,” Yolland said, quiet. “What the Shivering Sand takes, the Shivering Sand keeps forever.” He stepped closer to me then, his voice soft. “Mr. Betteredge, I’ve fished these waters my whole life. Four feet out from the Spit, there’s a solid shelf of rock half a fathom under the sand. If she’d slipped by accident, she would have landed on that shelf, water barely up to her waist. She could have stood up, waded back. She had to wade out, or jump out, into the deep quicksands past that shelf, to vanish like this. No accident, sir. She went there on purpose.”
No one argued with him. The man knew these sands better than anyone alive, and we all fell silent. We’d barely turned to trudge back up the beach slope when the under-groom came running down the sandhills, his face somber, holding out a crumpled little note to me.
“Penelope sent me, Mr. Betteredge,” he said. “She found this locked in Rosanna’s desk drawer.”
It was her last note, scrawled in her thin, scratchy handwriting, addressed only to me.
You have forgiven me a hundred times for my mistakes, sir. When you next pass the Shivering Sand, forgive me one last time. I have found the grave I always knew was waiting for me here. I lived, and I died, grateful for every bit of kindness you ever showed me.
That was all. It was little enough, but it broke me. Tears come easy when you’re young, just starting out in the world. Tears come just as easy when you’re old, and nearing the end of yours. I burst out crying.
Sergeant Cuff took a step toward me, meaning to offer comfort, I don’t doubt. I flinched back from him.
“Don’t touch me,” I said. “Your hounding of that poor girl drove her to this.”
“You’re wrong, Mr. Betteredge,” he said, quiet and steady, unoffended. “We can talk it all through once we are back under cover.”
I leaned on the groom’s arm to steady myself, and we all trundled back through the lashing rain, up the beach, toward the house. I did not know then that far worse trouble, far darker secrets, were waiting for us the second we stepped back over the threshold.

The news of Rosanna’s death had run ahead of us up the drive, and we found the entire household staff already in the grip of wild panic. As we passed Lady Verinder’s bedchamber, the door flew violently inward, and my mistress burst out among us, Franklin Blake trailing helplessly behind her, doing his level best to calm her frayed nerves. She was quite beside herself with horror, one hand raised to shake wildly at Sergeant Cuff.
“*You* are answerable for this!” she shrieked. “Gabriel! Pay that wretch his fee at once—drag him out of my sight, and be done with him!”

Sergeant Cuff was the only one of us still in full possession of his wits, the only person fit to meet her rage head-on. “I am no more to blame for this terrible tragedy than your ladyship is,” he said, soft but unshakable. “If in half an hour’s time you still insist I leave this house, I will accept your dismissal. But I will not touch your ladyship’s money to go.”

His tone was perfectly respectful, but it carried a weight that quieted her. She let Franklin lead her back into her chamber, and the door clicked shut behind them. Cuff’s sharp, observant gaze drifted over the huddle of maids, and he marked that while the rest were only shaken with fear, Penelope had dissolved into quiet tears. “As soon as your father has changed out of his wet clothes,” he told her, “come join us in his room. I have questions for you.”

Before that half hour was up, I had dried off, changed into dry linens, and lent Sergeant Cuff a spare set of my own clothes that fit him well enough. Penelope slipped in soon after, and I pulled her straight onto my knee, holding her tight. I don’t think I ever felt so proud of my good, dutiful daughter as I did in that moment, and I prayed over her, blessing her quiet heart. She tucked her face against my chest and looped her arms around my neck, and we sat silent for a minute, both of us thinking of the poor dead girl who had eaten at both our hearts. Cuff stepped over to the window and stared out at the grounds to give us space, and I thanked him for the kindness of it.

Folks of rank get every luxury, and that includes the luxury of wallowing in their feelings as long as they please. Us working folk have no such privilege. Necessity that spares our betters has no pity on us: we learn to tuck our grief and soft feelings back down, and trudge on with our duties as patiently as we can. I don’t complain of it, I only note it as fact. Penelope and I were ready to talk the moment Cuff was.

He asked her first if she knew what had driven her fellow servant to kill herself. As you might guess, Penelope confirmed it was unrequited love for Mr. Franklin Blake. When he asked if she had ever shared that suspicion with anyone else, she said no—she’d kept quiet, for Rosanna’s sake.
I added my own two cents right then. “And for Mr. Franklin’s sake too, my dear. If Rosanna truly died for love of him, it was with no knowledge or fault of his. Let him leave this house today, if he must go, without the useless, cruel weight of that truth hanging over him.”
“Quite right,” Sergeant Cuff said, and fell quiet again, turning Penelope’s account over in his head against some private theory he’d not breathed a word of to any of us.

The half hour was no sooner up than my lady’s bell rang sharp. On my way to answer it, I ran straight into Franklin coming out of his aunt’s sitting parlor. He told me Lady Verinder was ready to see Sergeant Cuff now, and added that he had a short word he wanted to speak to the Sergeant first. We paused in the hall beside the pinned-up railway timetable on our way back to my room.
“Are you truly planning to leave us, sir?” I asked him. “Give Miss Rachel a little time, surely she’ll come back to her senses.”
“She will come to her senses,” Franklin said, “the moment she learns I am gone for good, and she will never set eyes on me again.”
I thought he spoke out of resentment for the way Rachel had snubbed him, but it was far worse than that. My lady had noticed ever since the police first arrived in the house that the bare mention of Franklin’s name was enough to send Rachel’s temper flaring out of control. He had loved his cousin too much to admit that hard truth to himself, not until she stormed off to her aunt’s house without a word to him. Now his eyes were opened, and he had made the only resolution a man of any pride could make: to leave the house for good.

What he had to say to Cuff he said openly, with me standing right there. He explained that Lady Verinder was ready to own she had spoken far too hastily earlier, and asked if Cuff would consent to take his full fee now, leave the Moonstone investigation exactly where it stood, and be on his way.
Sergeant Cuff shook his head. “No, sir. My fee is paid for doing my duty. I will not take a penny of it until my work here is done.”
“I don’t follow you,” Franklin said.
“I will explain,” Cuff answered. “When I took this post, I swore I would shine every necessary light on the matter of the missing Diamond. I am ready now to keep that promise. I will go to Lady Verinder, lay every fact of this case out plain before her, and tell her exactly what steps to take to recover the Moonstone. Once that is done, my responsibility is discharged. Then she may choose for herself whether to let me continue my work, or send me off. Only then will I take my fee.”
Those words reminded us all that even a detective on the force has a reputation to guard, and no one could argue his logic. There was no more to be said on the matter.

As I stood up to lead him to my lady’s chamber, Cuff asked if Franklin wished to sit in on the interview. Franklin said he did not, unless Lady Verinder specifically requested it. He leaned in and whispered to me as we turned to go: “I know exactly what that man is about to say about Rachel. I love her far too well to sit here and hear those accusations, and keep my temper. Leave me by myself.”
I left him there, miserable, leaning his weight on the windowsill ledge, his face buried in his hands. Penelope was peeping around the doorframe, dying to slip in and comfort him. If I was in Franklin’s shoes, I’d have called her over at once. When one woman has used you ill, there is no comfort like spilling your troubles to another—nine times out of ten, she will take your side without a second’s hesitation. I would not be surprised if he did call her in once my back was turned, and I will say for my daughter: she would have done anything in her power to soothe Mr. Franklin Blake.

Sergeant Cuff and I proceeded on to my lady’s room. The last time we met with her, she could barely bring herself to lift her eyes from the book she had spread open on her table. This time, there was a marked change. She met Cuff’s gaze straight on, her own as steady and unflinching as his. The hard, proud Verinder family grit blazed in every line of her face, and I knew instantly: Sergeant Cuff had finally met his match. No woman of Lady Verinder’s mettle, strung up and braced to hear the very worst secrets he could lay bare, would flinch an inch.

The hush in the parlour hung thick as wool after we three settled into our seats, and the first words came soft and steady from my lady. “Sergeant Cuff,” she said, “there was no doubt some small excuse for the unkind, hasty way I spoke to you half an hour back. But I will not hide behind it. I say plainly, and with full sincerity, that I regret it, if I wronged you.”

That gentle grace in her voice and manner softened the dour old detective right away. He begged leave to justify himself, framing it as nothing more than the respect he owed his mistress. There was no way he could be blamed for the terrible shock that had just befallen us, he insisted—for his whole chance of solving the case rested on not doing a single thing to rouse Rosanna Spearman’s suspicions, and he appealed to me to confirm he’d kept to that rule. I could, and I did, swear he’d done no such thing to startle the poor girl. For a minute I thought we’d put the whole awkward matter to rest right there, as any sensible folks would.

But Sergeant Cuff had no intention of stopping there. You’ll see in a minute he was clearly set on forcing the most painful conversation imaginable between my lady and himself.
“I’ve heard folks say Rosanna killed herself over a heartbreak no one could fix,” the Sergeant said, “and that might be true enough. But my own reading points the other way. I believe some unbearable anxiety tied straight to the missing Moonstone drove her to that dreadful end. I don’t pretend to know exactly what that anxiety was—but with your ladyship’s leave, I think I know exactly who can confirm if I’m right or wrong.”

My lady waited a beat, then asked, “Is that person in this house?”
“They’ve left the house, my lady.”

That answer pointed so straight at Miss Rachel it could have been an arrow. A silence fell over us so heavy I thought it would never lift. Lord, how the wind howled around the eaves that night, how the rain lashed against the window panes like handfuls of thrown pebbles, while I sat there holding my breath waiting for one of them to speak again!

“Speak plainly,” my lady said at last. “Do you mean my daughter?”
“I do,” Sergeant Cuff said, no beating around the bush, plain as day.

My lady had her chequebook laid out on the table when we came in, no doubt ready to pay the Sergeant his fee and send him on his way. She tucked it back into the drawer, and my heart ached to see how her old, kind hand trembled—the same hand that had heaped favours on me and every other loyal servant in this house for decades, the hand I pray God will lay on mine when my time comes to retire for good.

“I had hoped,” she said, slow and quiet, “to pay you for your work and send you off without ever speaking Miss Verinder’s name openly in this context. My nephew Franklin must have told you as much before you came in.”
“Mr. Blake passed his message along, my lady. And I gave him my reason for ignoring it—”
“There’s no need to explain. You’ve said enough now to know you can’t turn back. I owe it to myself, and I owe it to my child, to make you stay here and lay every last suspicion out in the open.”

Sergeant Cuff glanced at his watch. “If there was more time, my lady, I’d write my full report instead of speaking it aloud. But time is too precious to waste putting pen to paper if we want to finish this inquiry. I’m ready to lay it all out now, painful as it will be for both of us.”

My lady stopped him before he could start. “I can make this less painful for you, and for my old friend Mr. Betteredge here, if I speak first. You suspect Miss Verinder of deceiving all of us, hiding the Moonstone for some private reason of her own? Is that true?”
“Quite true, my lady.”
“Very well. Before you begin, I will tell you as her mother: she is utterly, completely incapable of doing any such thing. You have known her for barely two days. I have known her every single day since she was born. Say whatever you like against her, lay out your evidence as sharp as you please—you will not offend me. I know full well your long experience has simply tricked you into misreading the facts. Mind you, I have no secret information you don’t. I am every bit as shut out of Rachel’s confidence as you are. I speak only because I know my child.”

She turned to me and held out her hand. I kissed it in silence. Then she faced Sergeant Cuff again, steady as a rock, and told him he could go on.

The Sergeant bowed. My lady’s words did nothing to shake his conviction, not even an inch—only his sharp, hatchet face softened for a second, like he felt sorry for her. He settled back in his chair and started his case against Miss Rachel plain as day: “You must see this from my point of view, my lady, from my 20 years working confidential family inquiries. The one lesson I’ve learned that matters here is this: high-ranking young ladies do sometimes carry private debts they dare not breathe a word of to their families. Sometimes it’s the milliner, sometimes the jeweller, sometimes it’s a purpose I won’t name to spare you distress. Keep that in mind, and see how the facts here have forced me back on that experience whether I wanted to be or not.”

He paused a moment, then kept going, with that horrid, unarguable clarity no one could wriggle away from: “Superintendent Seegrave told me Miss Verinder refused to let him question her, spoke to him with a rudeness and contempt no one could explain. I wrote it off at first to his own clumsiness. But later, after we found the paint smear on the door, and Mr. Blake’s evidence confirmed that smear was tied straight to the theft, Miss Rachel spoke to me herself. I saw three suspicious things: she was still violently agitated, more than 24 hours after the Moonstone went missing. She treated me the exact same rude way she treated Seegrave. And she was furious with Franklin Blake, the one person who’d worked harder than anyone else to find her diamond. My experience told me exactly what that meant. She had secret debts she dared not admit, debts she had to pay—and that the only way to pay them was to secretly pledge the Moonstone to a moneylender. That is the conclusion the facts forced me to draw.”
“And I told you already,” my lady answered, “the facts have led you wrong.”

I said nothing. Robinson Crusoe, God knows how, had popped straight into my muddled old head. If Sergeant Cuff had been dropped that second onto a desert island, with no Friday for company and no ship to take him off, he’d have been exactly where I wanted him to be. (Nota bene: I’m a good average Christian, so long as no one pushes my Christianity too far. And I’d wager most of you reading this are exactly the same.)

Sergeant Cuff ploughed on, no sign of being bothered by our silence: “To test that conclusion, I suggested we search every wardrobe in the house, both to find the stained dress that left the paint mark, and to see what Miss Verinder would do. You consented, Mr. Blake consented, Mr. Ablewhite consented. Only Miss Verinder flatly refused to let anyone touch her things. That was proof enough for me I was right. You all saw her drive off to Frizinghall even after I warned her leaving would ruin any chance of recovering her jewel. You all saw her publicly insult Mr. Blake on the front steps, the very man who’d handed me the first clue that broke the case open. If she’s not hiding the Moonstone, what do those things mean?”

He looked right at me then. It was downright frightening to hear him stack proof after proof against Miss Rachel, and know every single fact he stated was true, even as every bone in my body wanted to defend her. Thank God I’ve always been the sort of man who’s constitutionally better than reason. That let me hold fast to my lady’s side, and keep a brave face on. Take my advice, friends: cultivate a healthy superiority to logic, and you’ll clip the claws of every sensible busybody who tries to nag you for your own good.

“Now for the part that ties Miss Verinder and the late Rosanna Spearman together,” Cuff went on. “The second Rosanna brought that washing-book into this room, I recognised her, and my whole theory shifted. I knew instantly she must be her accomplice. She was a reformatory woman, a master thief in her day, and she had ties to one of the only moneylenders in London reckless enough to advance huge sums on a famous gem like the Moonstone without asking awkward questions. She was the perfect go-between. Every odd thing Rosanna did after that—her long walks by the shore, the hidden seam she stitched into that stained nightgown, her refusal to speak to any of the other maids—all of it lined up perfectly with that theory. Even my lady fell quiet, daunted by how tight his chain of clues looked.

“Now, I have two plans to wrap this case up,” Cuff said. “The first is a sure thing. I’d put an experienced female operative in Rosanna’s old job to watch every move Miss Verinder makes, track every letter she sends or receives, every visitor she sees. I’d send one of my men to stake out that moneylender’s shop in London, to grab the Moonstone the second it arrives there. It’ll cost time and money, but we can’t lose.”

My lady rejected that plan out of hand, sharp as anything. “That is unacceptable. Tell me your other plan.”
“The bold experiment,” Cuff said. “Miss Rachel is hot-tempered, impulsive, not the sort of woman who can keep up a mask of deceit for long. If we hit her with a sudden, terrible shock she never sees coming, her better nature will force her to confess everything. I want to show up at Frizinghall unannounced and tell her, no warning at all, that Rosanna Spearman is dead. The shock will make her break.”

My lady didn’t hesitate a single second. “Yes. We’ll do that.”
“The pony chaise is ready out front,” Cuff said, making to leave.
My lady held up a hand to stop him. “I will not let you go to my daughter with that news. I have the right, as her mother, to put this test to her myself. You will stay here. I will drive to Frizinghall.”

For once in his whole life, the great Sergeant Cuff stood speechless, staring like any ordinary dumbfounded man. My lady rang the bell and ordered her waterproof cloak. It was still pouring rain, and the big closed carriage had already left with Miss Rachel earlier that day. I begged her not to go out in that storm, offered to come with her and hold the umbrella for her. She refused every plea.

When the pony chaise pulled around to the front steps, she turned to Sergeant Cuff in the hall. “You can count on two things,” she said. “I will press Miss Verinder as hard as you ever could to get the truth. And I will send word back to you, either in person or by letter, before the last London train leaves tonight.”

Then she stepped up into the driver’s seat, took the reins in her own hands, and spurred the pony off down the muddy, rain-slick road to Frizinghall.

No sooner had my mistress left us than I had a moment to turn my thoughts back to Sergeant Cuff. I found him tucked into a snug corner of the hall, poring over his memorandum book, the corners of his mouth twisted up in that sour, vicious little curl of his I knew so well.

“Making notes on the case?” I asked.

“No,” said the Sergeant. “Checking what my next professional engagement happens to be.”

“Oh,” I said. “So you think it’s all wrapped up here, then?”

“I think,” answered Sergeant Cuff, “that Lady Verinder is one of the cleverest women in all England. I also think a rose is far better worth looking at than a diamond. Where is the gardener, Mr. Betteredge?”

Not another word could I drag out of him about the Moonstone. He’d lost every scrap of interest in his own inquiry, and was hell-bent only on hunting down our gardener. An hour later, I heard the two of them shouting at each other in the conservatory, with that accursed dog-rose right at the root of the whole quarrel.

Meantime, I had business of my own to see to: confirming whether Mr. Franklin still planned to leave on the afternoon train. Once he’d heard the full account of the closed-door talk with Lady Verinder and how it had ended, he immediately changed his mind, deciding to wait for news to come back from Frizinghall. This perfectly natural shift in plans — which for any ordinary person would have amounted to nothing at all — had one thoroughly annoying outcome for Mr. Franklin. It left him adrift, with nothing but empty idle time on his hands, and that was all it took for every last one of his mixed, foreign sides of character to come spilling out, one right after another, like rats bolting out of a sack.

One minute he was acting like an Italian-Englishman, the next a German-Englishman, the next a French-Englishman, drifting in and out of every sitting room in the house, with nothing to ramble on about but the way Miss Rachel had snubbed him, and nobody to vent to but me. I found him, for example, in the library, sat right under the map of Modern Italy, with no clue how to cope with his heartache other than talk it to death. “I’ve got all kinds of worthy aspirations, Betteredge — but what am I supposed to do with them now? I’m full of dormant good qualities, if only Rachel would have helped me bring them out!”

He was so eloquent painting the picture of his own neglected merits, and so pathetic when he finished lamenting over it, that I was at my wits’ end trying to think how to console him — till it occurred to me there was no better cure for this kind of mood than a wholesome dose of *Robinson Crusoe*. I hobbled off to my room, hobbled back with that immortal book under my arm, and found the library empty. The map of Modern Italy stared back at me; I stared right back at it.

I tried the drawing room. His handkerchief was on the floor, proof he’d drifted in — and the empty room was proof he’d drifted straight back out again. I tried the dining room, and found Samuel stood there with a biscuit and glass of sherry, staring blankly at thin air. A minute before, Mr. Franklin had rung furiously for a small light refreshment. Samuel had dashed to bring it to him, and Mr. Franklin had vanished before the bell downstairs had even finished ringing from the hard yank he’d given it.

I found him at last in the morning room, stood at the window drawing messy hieroglyphics with his finger in the condensation on the glass. “Your sherry’s waiting for you, sir,” I said. I might as well have talked to one of the four walls; he was sunk so deep in his own tangled thoughts there was no pulling him back to the surface. “How do *you* explain Rachel’s conduct, Betteredge?” was the only answer I got.

Not having that answer ready, I pulled out *Robinson Crusoe*, which I remain fully convinced holds the solution to every mystery if you just search long enough for it. Mr. Franklin shut the book on the spot, and immediately launched into that muddled German-English gibberish of his. “Why not look into it?” he said, like I’d personally objected to the idea. “Why the devil lose your patience, Betteredge, when patience is all you need to get at the truth? Don’t interrupt me. Rachel’s conduct is perfectly intelligible, if you just do her the common decency to take the Objective view first, the Subjective view next, and the Objective-Subjective view to wrap things up. What do we know? We know the loss of the Moonstone last Thursday morning threw her into a state of nervous excitement she hasn’t recovered from yet. Do you mean to deny the Objective view, so far? Very well, then — don’t interrupt me. Now, when a person’s in that state of frayed nerves, how can you expect her to behave the way she normally would to anyone around her? Arguing that way, from the inside out, what do we land at? The Subjective view. I defy you to contradict it. Very well then — what follows? Good Heavens, the Objective-Subjective explanation follows, of course! Rachel, properly speaking, isn’t *Rachel* right now — she’s Somebody Else. Do I mind being treated cruelly by Somebody Else? You’re unreasonable enough, Betteredge, but you can hardly accuse me of that. So where does that leave us? It leaves me perfectly happy and comfortable, in spite of your confounded narrow English prejudice. Where’s the sherry?”

By that point my head was so muddled I wasn’t entirely sure if it was my own head or Mr. Franklin’s. In that terrible state, I somehow managed to pull off three very Objective acts: I fetched Mr. Franklin his sherry, I retreated to my own quarters, and I soothed myself with the most calming pipe of tobacco I have ever smoked in my life.

Don’t imagine I got rid of Mr. Franklin that easily, though. Adrift again, he wandered out of the morning room and into the hall, then found his way to the servants’ wing, smelled my pipe smoke, and suddenly remembered he’d been fool enough to give up smoking entirely for Miss Rachel’s sake. In the blink of an eye he burst in on me, cigar case in hand, and went off on that same everlasting subject, this time in his neat, witty, cynical French way. “Give me a light, Betteredge. Is it conceivable a man can smoke as long as I have and never work out that there’s a full system for handling women hidden right in his cigar case? Follow me carefully, I’ll prove it in two words. You pick a cigar, try it, and it disappoints you. What do you do? Throw it away and try another. Now see the parallel! You pick a woman, try her, and she breaks your heart. Fool! Take a lesson from your cigar case. Throw her away and try another!”

I shook my head at that. Clever as it no doubt was, my own experience ran dead against it. “Back when the late Mrs. Betteredge was alive,” I said, “I felt many a time tempted to try your philosophy, Mr. Franklin. But the law says you have to smoke that cigar once you’ve chosen it, sir.” I winked to drive the point home. Mr. Franklin burst out laughing, and we were as merry as crickets till the next weird facet of his character turned up in due course. That’s how things went between the two of us — while the Sergeant and our gardener were still at each other’s throats over roses — passing the time before news came back from Frizinghall.

The pony chaise returned a full half hour earlier than I’d dared to expect it. My lady had decided to stay on, for the time being, at her sister’s house. The groom brought back two letters from his mistress: one addressed to Mr. Franklin, the other to me. I sent Franklin’s letter on to him in the library, where his wanderings had led him for the second time. My own letter I read in my room. A cheque fell out the second I opened it, and before I’d even digested the written words, I knew Sergeant Cuff was well and truly dismissed from the Moonstone inquiry.

I sent word to the conservatory asking the Sergeant to come speak to me right away. He appeared, his mind still full of the gardener and the dog-rose, swearing no man alive or dead had ever been as stubborn as Mr. Begbie. I asked him to set that trivial nonsense aside and pay full attention to a serious matter. That was enough to pull him out of his daze, and he noticed the letter in my hand.

“Ah,” he said, all worn out. “You’ve heard from her ladyship. Have I anything to do with that, Mr. Betteredge?”

“You can judge for yourself, Sergeant.” I proceeded to read him the letter, with all the emphasis and discretion it deserved:
> I ask you to inform Sergeant Cuff that I kept the promise I made to him. This is the result, so far as Rosanna Spearman is concerned. Miss Verinder solemnly declares she has never spoken a private word to Rosanna since that unhappy woman first entered our house. They never met, not even by accident, on the night the Diamond was lost, and no communication of any kind passed between them from the Thursday morning the alarm was first raised in the house, to this present Saturday afternoon, when Miss Verinder left us. After I told my daughter suddenly, and in so many plain words, of Rosanna Spearman’s suicide — this is what came of it.

I looked up when I reached that point, and asked Sergeant Cuff what he made of the letter so far. “I’d only offend you if I spoke my mind,” he answered. “Go on, Mr. Betteredge. Go on.”
