Remembering this man had the audacity to complain about our gardener’s stubbornness, my tongue itched to rewrite his words for him. But my good old Christian resolve held firm. I went on reading steadily:
> Having appealed to Miss Verinder in the way the officer thought best, I next spoke to her in the way I thought most likely to impress her. Twice, before my daughter left this house, I warned her privately that she was exposing herself to suspicion of the most unbearable, degrading kind. I have now told her, in the plainest terms, that those fears have come true.
> Her answer, on her own solemn oath, is as clear as words can make it. First, she owes no private money to any living person. Second, the Diamond is not now, and never has been, in her possession, after she put it in her cabinet on Wednesday night.
> The confidence my daughter has placed in me goes no further than this. She stays stubbornly silent when I ask her to explain the Diamond’s disappearance. She refuses, through tears, when I beg her to speak out for my sake. “The day will come when you will know why I am careless of being suspected, and why I am silent even to you. I have done much to make my mother pity me — nothing to make my mother blush for me.” Those are my daughter’s own words.
> After all that has passed between the officer and me, I think — stranger as he is — he should be made acquainted with what Miss Verinder has said, just as you are. Read him my letter, then place the enclosed cheque in his hands. In ending all further claim on his services, I have only to say I am convinced of his honesty and his intelligence — but I am more certain than ever that the circumstances of this case have fatally misled him.

That was the end of the letter. Before I handed over the cheque, I asked Sergeant Cuff if he had any comment to make. “It’s no part of my duty, Mr. Betteredge,” he answered, “to pass remarks on a case I’ve finished with.” I tossed the cheque across the table to him. “Do you believe that last part of her ladyship’s letter?” I said, indignantly.

The Sergeant looked at the cheque, lifted his dreary eyebrows in acknowledgement of her ladyship’s generosity. “This is such a generous estimate of the value of my time,” he said, “that I feel bound to give something back for it. I’ll bear the amount of this cheque in mind, Mr. Betteredge, when the right occasion comes round.”

“What do you mean?” I asked.

“Her ladyship has smoothed things over very cleverly for the present,” said the Sergeant. “But this particular family scandal is the kind that bursts right up again when you least expect it. We’ll have more detective work on our hands, sir, before the Moonstone is many months older.”

If those words meant anything, and the way he spoke them meant anything, it all boiled down to this: my mistress’s letter had convinced him that Miss Rachel was hardened enough to resist every appeal, and that she’d lied to her own mother — good God, under what terrible circumstances! I don’t know how anyone else in my shoes would have replied to him. I said plain and simple: “Sergeant Cuff, I consider that last observation an insult to both my lady and her daughter!”

“Mr. Betteredge, if you take it as a warning to yourself, you’ll be far closer to the mark.”

Hot and angry as I was, that calm, unshakable confidence of his shut me right up. I walked to the window to calm myself down. The rain had stopped — and who should I spot in the courtyard but Mr. Begbie, the gardener, waiting outside to pick right back up with Sergeant Cuff on their dog-rose argument. “My compliments to the Sairgent,” said Mr. Begbie the second he laid eyes on me. “If he’s minded to walk to the station, I’m agreeable to go with him.”

“What!” cried the Sergeant, right behind me. “You’re not convinced yet?”

“The de’il a bit am I convinced!” answered Mr. Begbie.

“Then I’ll walk to the station!” says the Sergeant.

“Then I’ll meet you at the gate!” says Mr. Begbie.

I was still furious, as you know — but no man’s temper could hold out against a disruption like that. Sergeant Cuff noticed my mood shift, and leaned in to prod it along. “Come, come! Why not treat my view of the case the same way her ladyship does? Why not say the circumstances have fatally misled me?”

To take things the way my lady took them was a privilege worth enjoying — even if it was Sergeant Cuff offering it to me. I cooled slowly down to my usual level. I regarded any opinion of Miss Rachel that didn’t match my lady’s or my own with lofty contempt. The only thing I couldn’t manage was to drop the subject of the Moonstone! I know my own common sense should have told me to let the matter rest, but there — the virtues the current generation brags about hadn’t been invented back in my day. Sergeant Cuff had hit me right on a raw spot, and even though I looked down on him with contempt, that tender spot still tingled. Before I knew it, I was deliberately dragging him back to the subject of her ladyship’s letter.

“I’m perfectly satisfied myself,” I said. “But never mind that. Go on, act as if I’m still open to being convinced. You think Miss Rachel can’t be trusted to keep her word, and you say we’ll hear more about the Moonstone soon. Back your opinion, Sergeant,” I finished, all casual and airy.

Instead of taking offence, Sergeant Cuff seized my hand and shook it till my fingers ached. “I swear to heaven,” this strange officer said solemnly, “I’d take a job as a domestic servant tomorrow, Mr. Betteredge, if I had a chance to work side by side with you. To say you’re as transparent as a child is paying children a compliment nine out of ten of them don’t even deserve. There, there, we won’t start arguing again. I’ll tell you what I think on easier terms than that. I won’t say a word more about her ladyship, or about Miss Verinder — I’ll just play prophet, for once, and for your sake. I warned you already you haven’t seen the last of the Moonstone. Very well. Now I’ll tell you, as a parting gift, of three things that are going to happen in the future, that will force themselves on your attention whether you like it or not.”

“Go on!” I said, completely unabashed, just as casual as ever.

“First,” said the Sergeant, “you’ll hear something from the Yollands — when the postman delivers Rosanna’s letter at Cobb’s Hole, next Monday.” If he’d thrown a bucket of cold water over my head, I doubt I’d have felt it half as unpleasantly as those words. Miss Rachel’s claim of innocence had left every part of Rosanna’s strange behavior — the new nightgown she’d sewn, the stained nightgown she’d hidden, all the rest of it — completely unexplained. It had never once occurred to me till Sergeant Cuff forced it right to the front of my mind.

“Second,” the Sergeant went on, “you’ll hear of the three Indians again. You’ll hear of them hanging around the neighborhood if Miss Rachel stays here, or in London if she moves to London.” Now that I’d written those three jugglers off and fully convinced myself of my young lady’s innocence, I took this second prophecy easily enough. “That’s two of the three things you say are coming,” I said. “Now the third!”

“Third and last,” said Sergeant Cuff, “sooner or later, you’ll hear something of that London moneylender I’ve mentioned to you twice before. Give me your pocket-book, and I’ll write down his name and address, so there’s no mistake if it really comes to pass.” He scribbled it on a blank leaf: *Mr. Septimus Luker, Middlesex-place, Lambeth, London.*

“There,” he said, pointing to the address. “Those are the last words on the Moonstone I’ll trouble you with for now. Time will show if I’m right or wrong. Meantime, sir, I carry away a real personal liking for you, that I think does both of us credit. If we don’t meet again before I retire from the force, I hope you’ll come visit me at the little house near London I’ve had my eye on. There will be grass walks in my garden, Mr. Betteredge, I promise you. And as for the white moss rose —”

“The de’il a bit ye’ll get white moss rose to grow, unless ye bud it on the dogue-rose first!” yelled a voice through the window. We both turned

I’d had the pony-chaise harnessed and waiting all day, just in case Mr. Franklin was stubborn enough to catch the overnight train out. When his luggage came thumping down the stairs, followed by the man himself, I knew for once in his flighty, indecisive life, he’d stuck to a resolution.
“So you’re really set on this, sir?” I said, meeting him in the front hall. “Why not bide a day or two longer? Give Miss Rachel another chance to come round.”
All that fancy foreign polish he’d picked up on his travels had melted right off him now that goodbye was staring him in the face. Instead of answering out loud, he pressed Lady Verinder’s latest letter into my palm. Most of it repeated what I’d already gotten in her earlier note, but there was a postscript about Miss Rachel at the end that explained exactly why Mr. Franklin was so dead set on leaving, no questions asked.
“You will likely wonder,” her ladyship wrote, “how I could let my own daughter keep me completely in the dark. A diamond worth twenty thousand pounds is missing, and I am forced to infer that the riddle of its disappearance is no riddle at all to Rachel—that some unfathomable vow of silence has been forced on her by people I have never met, for a purpose I cannot even begin to guess. You might think no mother would put up with being trifled with in such a way. But in Rachel’s current state, I have no choice. She is a wreck of jangled nerves, pitiable to see. I dare not broach the subject of the Moonstone again until time has softened her agitation. To that end, I already sent the Sergeant Cuff packing. The mystery that stumps us stumps him too—no stranger can help us here. His presence only adds to my distress, and the very sound of his name sends Rachel into fits.
“My own plans are settled as far as they can be. I intend to take Rachel to London shortly, partly for the distraction of a complete change of scene, partly to consult the best nerve specialists in the city. May I ask you to meet us there? My dear Franklin, you must learn patience as I have, and wait for a kinder hour. The invaluable help you gave us in searching for the stolen jewel is still an unforgivable sin in Rachel’s current state of mind. You moved through this affair blindfold, and your efforts to uncover the truth inadvertently threatened to lay her secret bare, piling more weight on her already overburdened shoulders. I cannot defend the perversity that makes you responsible for consequences neither of us could have imagined. She will not listen to reason—she can only be pitied. It grieves me to say it, but for now, you and Rachel are far better apart. The only advice I can offer is to give her time.”
I handed the letter back to him, genuinely sorry. I knew how deep his love for our young lady ran, and I could see her mother’s account of her suffering had cut him to the quick. “You know the old proverb, sir,” I said. “When things are at their worst, they’re bound to mend. They can’t get much sorrier than they are right now, Mr. Franklin.”
He folded the letter up tight, and my little bit of comfort didn’t seem to land at all.
“When I came down from London with that accursed diamond,” he said, “I would have sworn there wasn’t a happier household in all of England. Look at us now! Scattered, divided, the very air of the place reeking of mystery and suspicion. Do you remember that morning out at the Shivering Sand, when we talked of my uncle Herncastle, and that cursed birthday gift? The Moonstone has done his work of vengeance for him, Betteredge, in ways the old colonel never could have dreamed up himself.”
With that he shook my hand hard, and stepped out to the pony-chaise.
I followed him down the steps. It was a wretched sight, watching him leave the old house where he’d spent every happy summer of his boyhood like this. Penelope, red-eyed and upset by all the trouble we’d had, came hurrying round to say goodbye. Mr. Franklin kissed her forehead, and I waved a hand like to say he was more than welcome. A handful of the other maids peeked round the corner, crying quiet, too—he was the kind of gentleman every last woman on the place adored. At the last minute I held the chaise back, begging him as a favor to write and let us know where he’d gone. He hardly seemed to hear me, staring around at every old tree and gable, as if he was memorizing the whole property for a last goodbye. “Tell us your destination, sir!” I called, hanging on to the side of the chaise to drag the answer out of him. Mr. Franklin pulled his hat down low over his eyes. “Destination?” he echoed, hollow. “I’m off to the devil, for all I care.” The pony reared at the words, like even the beast was scandalized. “God bless you, sir, wherever you end up!” I called out, before he whipped the pony around and vanished down the drive. A sweet, kind, foolish man he was, through all his faults—he left a gaping empty spot in the whole house when he left that day.
That long summer Saturday night closed in dull as ditchwater.
I kept my spirits from sinking by clinging fast to my pipe and my well-thumbed copy of *Robinson Crusoe*. The rest of the women (bar Penelope) gossiped nonstop about Rosanna’s suicide, all stubbornly convinced the poor girl had stolen the Moonstone and drowned herself for fear of being caught. My daughter, of course, stuck firm to her own theory that the girl had killed herself over unrequited love for Mr. Franklin. Oddly enough, that story left exactly the same loose end as Rachel’s claim of innocence: no explanation for Rosanna’s secret trip to Frizinghall, no explanation for all her strange meddling with the stained nightgown. Pointing that out to Penelope did as much good as throwing a raindrop at a waterproof coat. Truth be told, my daughter inherited my tendency to ignore inconvenient logic, and she’s long since outstripped me at it.
Sunday rolled around, and the closed carriage we’d left at Mr. Ablewhite’s returned empty. The coachman brought a verbal message for me, and written instructions for my lady’s personal maid and Penelope.
The message said Lady Verinder was set to leave for her London house with Rachel on Monday. The written notes laid out what clothes the two maids needed to pack, and told them to meet their mistresses in town at a set hour. Most of the rest of the household would follow shortly. Rachel had been so unwilling to step foot back in the house after all the trouble here, her ladyship had decided to go straight to London from Frizinghall, no detours home. I was ordered to stay behind at the country house, to see to the property indoors and out until further notice. The few remaining servants would be put on board wages.
That reminder of Mr. Franklin’s words about our scattered, broken household made me uneasy about him. The more I turned his fate over in my head, the more worried I got. By the Sunday post, I sent a letter to his father’s valet, Mr. Jeffco, an old acquaintance of mine, begging him to let me know what Mr. Franklin planned to do once he got to London.
Sunday evening was even duller than Saturday. We finished our supposed day of rest the way half the country does every week—all of us nodding off in our armchairs long before bedtime.
I don’t know how the rest of the household fared on Monday, but that day gave me a right good shake. The first of Sergeant Cuff’s three predictions came true right on schedule: I heard from the Yollands.
I’d just waved Penelope and my lady’s maid off on the London train with all the luggage, and was ambling around the grounds checking the hedges, when I heard someone call my name. I turned, and came face to face with Limping Lucy, the fisherman’s daughter. Barring her lame foot and a leanness that I’ve always thought a terrible flaw in a woman, she was a good-looking enough girl: sharp dark eyes, a clear voice, and a fine thick head of chestnut hair. Her crutch was a constant burden, and her temper was a whole other flaw you couldn’t miss.
“Well, my dear,” I said. “What can I do for you?”
“Where’s that man you call Franklin Blake?” she said, fixing me with a glare hot enough to burn, leaning all her weight on her crutch.
“That’s no way to speak of a gentleman,” I told her. “If you mean my lady’s nephew, you’ll refer to him as Mr. Franklin Blake, if you please.”
She limped one step closer, looking like she could have swallowed me whole. “*Mr.* Franklin Blake?” she snarled. “Murderer Franklin Blake would suit him far better.”
My years of putting up with my late wife’s temper came in handy then. When a woman tries to rattle you, turn the tables and rattle her first. They’re ready for every snappy defence you can make, but never for that. One word was all it took. I looked at her pleasantly, and said, “Pooh!”
Her temper blew sky high. She balanced on her good foot, lifted her crutch, and pounded it three times hard into the grass. “He’s a murderer! He’s a murderer! He’s a murderer! He killed Rosanna Spearman!” She screamed the words at the top of her lungs. A couple of the gardeners working nearby glanced up, saw who it was, knew exactly what Lucy’s tantrums were like, and went right back to their work.
“He killed Rosanna Spearman?” I repeated. “What on earth makes you say that, Lucy?”
“What do any man care? If she’d only thought the way I do, she’d still be alive today!”
“She was always kind enough to me, poor soul,” I said. “And I always tried to be kind to her, for what it’s worth.”
I spoke as soft as I could. I hadn’t the heart to tease her any more. I’d noticed her temper first, but now I noticed the raw, ragged misery in her face. Misery in working folk, you’ll find, is very often rude. My words melted her. She bowed her head down, resting it on the top of her crutch.
“I loved her,” she said, quiet as a whisper. “She’d had a terrible life, Mr. Betteredge—vile people had hurt her, led her astray, and none of it had spoiled her sweet temper. She was an angel. We could have been happy together. I had it all planned: we’d move to London, live like sisters, make our living with our needles. Then that man showed up, and spoiled every last bit of it. He bewitched her. Don’t tell me he didn’t mean it, don’t tell me he didn’t know. He ought to have seen it. He ought to have pitied her. ‘I can’t live without him,’ she’d say to me, ‘and he never even looks at me.’ Cruel, cruel, cruel. I told her no man was worth grieving that much over. And she said, ‘There are men worth dying for, Lucy, and he is one of them.’ I had all our savings put aside, I’d talked it all over with my ma and pa. I was going to take her far away from all the shame she felt here. We’d have had a tiny little lodging, lived quiet, happy. She had a fine education, you know, beautiful handwriting, quick with a needle. I’m educated too, I can write well enough, I’m just not as fast with the needle as she was. We’d have got by. And then this morning? Her letter came. Told me she was done with living. Said goodbye forever. Where is he?” she cried, lifting her head from the crutch, flaming back through her tears. “Where’s this fine gentleman I’ve got to speak of with respect? The day’s not far off when the poor rise up against the rich, Mr. Betteredge. And I pray to Heaven they start with him. I pray Heaven they start with him.”
There was a good Christian girl for you, pushed so far past her breaking point her piety all fell to pieces. Even the parson himself couldn’t have lectured her out of that temper. All I did was keep her on track, hoping for some scrap of new information.
“What do you want with Mr. Franklin Blake?” I asked.
“I have to see him.”
“Is there something specific you need?”
“I’ve got a letter for him.”
“From Rosanna Spearman?”
“Yes.”
“She sent it to you, to pass on?”
“Yes.”
I thought for a second the dark mystery of the Moonstone was finally going to lift, that every secret I’d been dying to uncover was going to drop right into my lap. Then I felt that old detective fever Sergeant Cuff had left behind in the air prickle in my bones.
“You can’t see Mr. Franklin,” I told her.
“I have to. And I will.”
“He left for London last night.”
Lucy stared hard at my face, saw I was telling the truth, and turned on her crutch to head back to Cobb’s Hole without another word.
“Wait!” I called after her. “I’m expecting news of him tomorrow. Give me the letter, and I’ll post it straight to him.”
Lucy steadied herself, looked back over her shoulder.
“I have to put it in his hands myself,” she said. “No other way will do.”
“Shall I write and tell him what you said?”
“Tell him I hate him. And you’ll be telling him the truth.”
“Yes, yes—but what about the letter—?”
“If he wants it, he can come back here, and get it from me.”
With that she limped off down the path. The detective fever burned away all my dignity. I chased after her, pestering her to tell me more, but it was no use. It was my misfortune to be a man, and Limping Lucy got great joy out of disappointing me. Later that day I tried her mother, who could only cry and reach for the Dutch gin bottle. I found her father mending nets on the beach, who just grunted that it was “a bad job” and went back to his work. Neither of them knew a thing more than I did. The only remaining hope was the post the next morning, to write to Mr. Franklin Blake directly.
You can imagine how I hovered by the gate waiting for the postman on Tuesday. He brought two letters. The first was from Penelope, barely skimmed, telling me my lady and Miss Rachel had arrived safe in London. The second was from Mr. Jeffco, and the news it held shocked me: Mr. Franklin had already left England entirely.
The second he got to London, he’d gone straight to his father’s house. He’d arrived at the worst possible time: his father was up to his ears in parliamentary business, fiddling with some private bill the House was voting on. Jeffco led Franklin straight into his study. “My dear Franklin! What’s this surprise? Is something wrong?” “Yes, something’s wrong with Rachel. I’m devastated.” “Sorry to hear it, but I can’t stop to listen now.” “When can you hear me out?” “My dear boy, I won’t lie to you. Not until the end of the session. Good night.” “Thank you, sir. Good night.” That was the whole conversation inside the study, Jeffco wrote. Outside the door, it was even shorter. “Jeffco, what time’s the tidal train out tomorrow morning?” “Six forty, Mr. Franklin.” “Wake me at five.” “Off abroad, sir?” “Off wherever the railway decides to take me.” “Shall I tell your father, sir?” “Tell him at the end of the session.”
Next morning Mr. Franklin was gone, bound for the continent. No one, not even him, had the faintest idea where he was going. He might turn up next anywhere in Europe, Asia, Africa, or America. Jeffco said the odds were perfectly even across all four corners of the globe.
That news closed off the last chance I had to bring Lucy and Mr. Franklin together, and killed all my progress solving the mystery. Penelope’s theory that Rosanna had killed herself over unrequited love for Mr. Franklin was confirmed as far as the rest of the household was concerned, and that was all. No one could say if the letter Rosanna left for Franklin was the confession Sergeant Cuff had suspected she wanted to tell him, or just a private farewell note about her hopeless, secret love. It stayed sealed in Limping Lucy’s hands, hidden from everyone, even her own parents. We all begged her to tell us what it said, but none of us could get a word out of her. The servants, still clinging to the belief that Rosanna had stolen the diamond and hidden it, poked and prodded all along the rocks by the Shivering Sand, and found nothing. The tide ebbed and flowed, summer turned to autumn, and the quicksand that had swallowed Rosanna’s body swallowed her secret right along with it.
You already know the Tuesday post brought me Franklin’s travel news and the update that Rachel and her ladyship were settled in London. Wednesday came and went, nothing new. Thursday brought another long letter from Penelope.
She wrote that the great London doctor they’d consulted for Rachel had earned his guinea by saying the best cure was amusement. Flower shows, operas, balls—an endless round of parties had been planned, and to her mother’s shock, Rachel threw herself into every single one of them. Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite had called, still as sweet-tongued as ever with his cousin, even after the cold shoulder he’d gotten on her birthday. Much to Penelope’s disgust, Rachel had welcomed him warmly, and even signed her name up to one of his ladies’ charity committees on the spot. Lady Verinder was reported to be low-spirited, and had held two long closed-door meetings with her lawyer. Penelope rambled on about Miss Clack, that poor relation I mentioned sitting next to Godfrey at the birthday dinner, who had a very fine taste in champagne. She was astonished Miss Clack hadn’t come crawling over to the house yet, as she always did, to mooch off her ladyship. That bit’s not worth mentioning, I know, except for one thing: I’ve been told the next part of this story is going to be narrated by that very same Miss Clack. If you ever hear her speak of your humble servant, do me a favor: don’t believe a single

The signing of Lady Verinder’s will was a far swifter affair than I had steeled myself to witness, hurried over with what I can only describe as indecent haste. Mr. Bruff the lawyer had no patience for the solemnity of the moment: he called for Samuel the footman to act as the second witness, and pressed a pen straight into my aunt’s hand before I could ready the few edifying, appropriate remarks I had prepared for such an occasion. It was all over in less than two minutes. Samuel trotted back downstairs, none the wiser for the spiritual guidance I might have shared, and Mr. Bruff folded the document up, glancing at me pointedly as if to will me to leave him alone with his client.
He might as well have tried to move St. Paul’s Cathedral with a sharp look as shift me. My mission of mercy was laid out clear before me, my bag of precious, soul-saving publications resting heavy and ready on my lap. To his credit (no doubt the product of his worldly legal training), he read the room fast. He reacted much the same way the surly cabman had when I first arrived: he uttered a most profane exclamation, beat a hasty, violent retreat, and left me mistress of the whole house.
As soon as the door clicked shut behind him, Lady Verinder sank back on her sofa, looking flustered, and brought up the will unprompted. “I hope you do not think yourself neglected, Drusilla dear,” she said. “I mean to hand your little legacy to you myself, out of my own hand, not tie it up in legal paper.”
It was a golden, heaven-sent opportunity. I seized it on the spot, yanking open my bag to pull out the top publication: the twenty-fifth early edition of that famous anonymous work (all but certainly written by the sainted Miss Bellows) *The Serpent at Home*. The worldly soul may be unaware of its design: it lays bare exactly how the Evil One lies in wait for us even in the most innocent, mundane corners of daily life, with chapters titled “Satan in the Hair Brush,” “Satan behind the Looking Glass,” “Satan under the Tea Table,” and more besides.
“Give this your attention, dear aunt, and you will grant me every request I have,” I told her, handing it open to a marked page of searing, burning eloquence, its subject: *Satan Among the Sofa Cushions*.
Poor Lady Verinder, reclining thoughtlessly on the very cushions the tract described, glanced at the page and handed it back more flustered than ever. “I’m afraid Drusilla, I can’t read that yet, not until I’m a little stronger. The doctor——”
The moment she spoke that man’s profession, I knew exactly what was coming. Over and over through my years of ministering to perishing fellow creatures, the notoriously infidel members of the medical trade had stepped between me and my sacred work, hiding behind the thin, miserable excuse that their patients needed quiet, and that the influence of Miss Clack and her books was the most disturbing force they could possibly face. That same blinded, materialist treachery was now trying to rob me of the only spiritual property my poverty could ever claim: my right to save my aunt’s soul.
“The doctor says I am not well today,” my poor misguided relative continued. “He forbids me seeing any strangers, and if I read at all, I am only to read the lightest, most amusing books. ‘Do nothing to weary your head or quicken your pulse,’ that was exactly what he said when he left me this morning.”
There was no choice but to yield, for the moment only. To argue openly that my ministry was infinitely more vital than any work a medical man could do would only make him take petty revenge on his weak patient, and threaten to abandon the case entirely. But there are more ways than one to sow good seed, and few people know them better than I do.
“You may feel stronger in an hour or two,” I coaxed. “Or you may wake tomorrow morning with a sense of something missing, and this little unassuming volume may be exactly what you need. You will let me leave it here, won’t you? The doctor can hardly object to that.”
I slipped it half under the sofa cushions, half poking out, right next to her handkerchief and her smelling bottle. Every time she reached for either, her hand would brush the book, and who could say? Sooner or later, the book might touch her.
With that small good work done, I decided to take my leave of her for a rest. As I turned to go, I glanced at the window, full of flower pots and boxes where Lady Verinder kept her beloved perishable blooms, forever getting up to lean over and smell them. A new happy thought struck me. “Oh, may I take a flower?” I asked, drifting over to the window unsuspected. Instead of picking one, I tucked a second tract from my bag in among the geraniums and roses, a little surprise for her to find later.
Why stop there? Why not hide a precious publication in every single room my aunt ever entered?
I said my goodbyes immediately, slipped across the hall and into the library. Samuel the footman, who had come up to see me out, assumed I had already left and trundled back downstairs. On the library table sat two of the “amusing” books the infidel doctor had recommended. I covered both of them out of sight with two of my own tracts. In the breakfast room, I found her favourite canary singing in its cage, the groundsel for its feed scattered on the table directly beneath it. I tucked a tract straight into the pile of seed. In the main drawing room, I slipped two more tracts between her sheet music on the piano, where she would find them when she sat down to play. In the back drawing room, I tucked one under a half-finished piece of embroidery I knew she was working on. The little curtained nook leading off that room, no proper door to separate it, had her old plain fan resting on the mantelpiece. I opened my ninth tract to a very specially marked passage, and used the fan as a bookmark to hold the page.
The only question left was whether I dared venture upstairs to the bedchamber floor, at the risk of being insulted by that sharp-tongued lady’s maid in her cap ribbons if she caught me. But what Christian worth the name fears a little insult? I crept up, ready to bear anything, and the whole upper floor was silent and empty—servants’ tea time, no doubt. Lady Verinder’s bedroom faced the street, and the miniature of my late dear uncle Sir John hung on the wall opposite the bed, seeming to smile at me, seeming to say “Drusilla, leave a book.” I tucked one tract beside the matches on her nightstand, and a second under the box of chocolate drops on the other side. Whether she reached for a light, or a sweet to soothe her restlessness, she would find a precious publication waiting to greet her.
Only one tract was left at the bottom of my bag, and only one room left unexplored: the bathroom opening off her bedroom. I peeked inside, and that still, small holy voice that never deceives whispered to me: you have met her everywhere else, Drusilla. Meet her at her bath, and the work is done. A dressing gown was thrown over a chair, with a deep pocket stitched into it. I slipped my last tract right inside that pocket.
I cannot put to words the exquisite sense of a duty perfectly fulfilled when I slipped out of the house unseen, my empty bag tucked under my arm, stepping out onto the London street. O worldly friends, chasing the phantom of pleasure through the guilty mazes of dissipation! Happiness is so simple to find, if only you will choose to be good. That night, when I folded my things, and thought of the true riches I had scattered from the attic to the ground floor of my wealthy aunt’s house, I felt as light and carefree as a small child. I sang one verse of the Evening Hymn, and fell asleep before I could finish a second.
I woke the next morning feeling years younger, bright and full of purpose. I had no intention of calling at Montagu Square for the sake of food, only for the certainty of finding my dear aunt, when my landlady’s maid knocked at my door to say Lady Verinder’s servant had called to see me. It was Samuel, that fresh-faced young footman with the teachable look, holding a huge heavy parcel under his arm that seemed to frighten him the moment he set it down. “My lady sends her love, Miss,” he stammered. “Said you’ll find a letter inside.” He looked half-ready to run for the door.
I detained him for a few kind inquiries, and learned that Lady Verinder, Rachel, and Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite had all gone out for a drive. Rachel was dressed for a ball that night, and Godfrey had arranged to come back for coffee to escort her. There was a morning concert advertised for the next day, and Samuel had orders to buy tickets for a large party, including one for Godfrey. I could scarce believe my ears. Our special meeting of the Mothers’-Small-Clothes-Conversion Society was scheduled that very night, summoned specifically to call on Godfrey’s advice when the community was quite overwhelmed by a mountain of donated trousers that threatened to swamp all our work. And the next day was the grand festival of the British-Ladies’-Servants’-Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision Society, where he was supposed to be the life and soul of our struggling little institution. Instead he was gallivanting off to balls and concerts with a crowd of worldlings. It was the most awful, shocking backsliding I had ever witnessed from a man we had all considered our Christian hero.
I turned my attention to the parcel that had so terrified Samuel. It was exactly what I feared: all twelve of my precious tracts, sent back to me by the doctor’s express order. The short note from my aunt simply said she dared not disobey her medical man, poor dear soul.
But I did not doubt for a moment what to do next. The true Christian never yields once she has embarked on a mission. Books had failed, thanks to the doctor’s obstinacy. So I would try preparation by little notes: short, edifying passages copied out in different hands by my sisters in the Mothers’ society, written as friendly personal letters, so no doctor’s watchful suspicion could ever mark them as tracts. Six I posted that very night, six I tucked into my pocket to hide around the house the next day.
By two o’clock I was back at the house. Lady Verinder had had a bad night, resting on the sofa in the room where we had signed the will, dozing. I assumed Rachel and all her party of pleasure-seekers, Godfrey included, had left for the concert already. I set to work at once: I set one of my little notes all alone on the library mantel, far from the rest of the unopened post, to draw my aunt’s curiosity. I dropped a second on the breakfast room floor, where the first servant to walk in would pick it up and hand it straight to their mistress.
I had just stepped into the main drawing room to place the rest, when I heard a soft, fluttering, considerate little knock at the front door. Before I could slip back to the library where I was supposed to be waiting, Samuel was in the hall answering it, and to my horror, he called up the stairs: “Upstairs, if you please, sir.” The footsteps of a man started up towards the drawing room floor.
I assumed it was the doctor, the man who had dared insult me by returning my sacred books. I could not bear to face him. I slipped fast into that little curtained nook off the back drawing room, pulling the heavy curtains shut tight to hide myself. I waited a minute, then two, but he never went towards Lady Verinder’s bedroom. He paced restlessly up and down the drawing room, talking quietly to himself. I parted the heavy curtains just the tiniest crack, to listen.
The words I heard him mutter, under his breath, were: “I’ll do it today.”
And the voice that spoke them was not the doctor’s. It was Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite’s.

My hand slipped from the edge of the curtain I’d been peering through. Do not mistake me, though – the overwhelming, mortifying awkwardness of being caught eavesdropping was not the first thought that crossed my mind. My sisterly devotion to Mr. Godfrey was still so fierce, I did not even pause to wonder why he had skipped the public concert we had all been expected to attend. No, I could only fixate on the startling, resolute line I had just heard him utter to the empty room: *“I will do it today.”*

What on earth was he planning to do? Something more deplorably unworthy of his spotless reputation than any misstep he had made yet? Would he renounce his faith? Abandon the Mothers’ Small Clothes Sewing Circle? Would his angelic smile never again grace our committee meetings? Would his matchless oratory never again rouse crowds to donate to good causes at Exeter Hall? The very idea of such catastrophes felt so grave that I was seconds away from bursting out of my hiding spot, ready to beg him in the name of every Ladies’ Benevolent Committee in London to explain himself – when a second voice cut through the quiet.

It boomed right through the curtain: loud, bold, lacking every single soft, feminine grace a young lady ought to possess. It was Rachel Verinder.

“Why have you come up here, Godfrey?” she said. “Why didn’t you go into the library?”
He laughed softly. “Miss Clack is in the library.”
“Clack in the library!” She dropped immediately onto the ottoman in the back drawing-room. “You’re quite right. We are far better off staying here.”

One moment before, I had been burning with agitation, unsure what to do next. Now I turned icy cold, and there was no doubt at all: I could not step out and show myself after what I had overheard, and the only other way out would have required me to climb into the fireplace. I was trapped in a full martyrdom, and so I did the only sensible thing: I noiselessly rearranged the curtain slits so I could see and hear perfectly, and faced my trial with the steadfast spirit of an early Christian saint.

“Don’t sit on the ottoman,” Rachel went on. “Pull a chair opposite me. I like people to sit facing me when I speak to them.”
He dragged the nearest seat over – a low, squashed armchair – and his tall, broad frame looked absurdly crammed into it. I had never seen his long legs look so awkward.
“Well?” she said. “What did you tell the people who came to take us to the concert?”
“Exactly what you asked me to, dear Rachel. That your mother was unwell today, and you did not feel right leaving her to go out.”
“That mamma was not at all well today? And that I didn’t quite like leaving her to go to the concert?”
“Those were my exact words. They were grieved to miss you at the performance, but they understood completely. They all sent their love, and expressed great hope that Lady Verinder’s indisposition would pass quickly.”
“You don’t think it’s serious, do you, Godfrey?”
“Not at all. I am fully confident all will be well in a few days.”
“I think so too. I was frightened at first, but I believe that now. It was very kind of you to go and make my excuses to people who are almost strangers to you. But why didn’t you go with them to the concert? It seems so unfair that you should miss the music too.”
“Do not say that, Rachel! If you only knew how much happier I am right here, with you!”
He clasped his hands and turned his face toward me, and my stomach lurched. It was that exact same soft, pleading, tear-pricked expression that had charmed thousands on the Exeter Hall platform when he begged for donations for destitute, starving communities across the empire. The man was using his charity speech face on *Rachel Verinder*.

“You ought to break your bad habits, Godfrey,” she said. “Do try to stop paying compliments, to please me.”
“I have never paid you a compliment in my life, Rachel. Successful love may flatter, I admit – but hopeless love, dearest, only ever speaks the truth.”
He pulled his chair closer, and reached for her hand as he said those last two words. There was a short silence. It was no wonder his oratory could thrill entire crowds: it had clearly stunned her too. I thought I understood now what he had meant when he said he would “do it today.” He was declaring his love, right that moment.

“Have you forgotten what we agreed on, Godfrey, when we talked out in the country?” Rachel said. “We said we would be cousins, and nothing more.”
“I break that agreement every time I see you.”
“Then stop seeing me.”
“Useless. I break it every time I think of you. Oh, Rachel! Only the other day you told me my place in your estimation was higher than it had ever been. Am I mad to build hope on those dear words? Am I wrong to dream that one day your heart might soften to me? Do not tell me I am wrong. Leave me that delusion to cherish, if I have nothing else!”
His voice trembled, and he lifted his white handkerchief to his eyes. Exeter Hall again. Nothing was missing from the performance but the cheering crowd, and the glass of water the committee always handed him mid-speech. Even Rachel’s stubborn, hard nature softened. I saw her lean a little closer, her tone shifting to one of unaccustomed interest.
“Are you really sure you care for me that much?”
“Sure! You know what I was, Rachel. Let me tell you what I am now. I have lost every interest in life but you. A transformation has come over me I cannot even explain. Would you believe my charitable work has become an unbearable nuisance? That the sight of any Ladies’ Committee now makes me wish I could flee to the very ends of the earth?”

I do not think the entire recorded history of religious backsliding could produce a more shocking apostasy than that. I thought of the Mothers’ Small Clothes, the Sunday-Sweetheart-Supervision society, all the dozens of other good works that leaned on Mr. Godfrey like a tower of strength. I thought of all those hardworking women on our boards who relied on him to keep their operations running – this very same man, who had just called their sacred work a nuisance, who had said he would run to the ends of the earth to avoid meeting with them. It took every scrap of my pious self-discipline to clamp a gloved hand over my mouth and swallow my righteous fury without making a sound. I did not miss a single syllable of what followed.

“You have made your confession,” Rachel said. “I wonder if it would cure you of this unhappy attachment to me, if I made mine?”
We both started. We assumed she was finally about to reveal the great unsolved mystery of the stolen Moonstone.
“Would you look at me and guess,” she went on, “that I am the most miserable girl alive? It is true. What greater wretchedness is there than to live degraded in your own eyes? That is my life now.”
“My dear Rachel, you could never have any reason to speak of yourself that way!”
“How do you know that?”
“Because I know you. Your silence has never made you less worthy in the eyes of your true friends. The disappearance of your precious birthday diamond may seem strange, your unexplained link to that event may seem stranger still—”
“Are you talking about the Moonstone, Godfrey?”
“I assumed you were referring to that—”
“I am not talking about the diamond at all. I can hear the Moonstone discussed by anyone, and not feel degraded in the slightest. If the full story of that Diamond ever comes out, it will be clear I took on a dreadful responsibility, and kept a terrible secret – but no one will ever be able to say I did anything mean. You have misunderstood me. It is my fault for not speaking plainer. Suppose you were not in love with me, but in love with some other woman?”
“Yes?”
“Suppose you found out that woman was utterly unworthy of you? That it would be a disgrace to waste another thought on her? That the very idea of marrying her made your face burn with shame?”
“Yes?”
“And suppose, in spite of all of that – you could not tear her out of your heart? Suppose the feeling she stirred in you back when you trusted her was something you could not shake? That the love that creature inspired is both the breath of my life, and the poison that is killing me – all at once? Go away! I must be out of my mind to speak this way. No, do not leave. I will not let you carry away the wrong impression. Listen to this: he does not know, and he never will know, what I have just told you. I will never see him again, no matter what happens. Do not ask me his name. Change the subject. Are you a good enough doctor, Godfrey, to tell me why I feel like I am suffocating? Is there a kind of hysteria that bursts out in words instead of tears? It does not matter. You will get over all this trouble I have caused you easily enough now, will you not? I have fallen in your estimation, haven’t I? Do not pity me. For God’s sake, go away!”
She twisted away, beating her hands wildly against the back of the ottoman, then dropped her head onto the cushions and burst into sobs. Before I could even process that display, I was horrified by Mr. Godfrey’s next move. I swear solemnly, he dropped to *both* knees at her feet. Then he put his arms around her, and the two words he roared out with the full, booming force of his public speaking voice were enough to freeze me solid.

That was all. But it was such a thunderous, passionate performance that Rachel did not even try to push his arms away. She sat there, stunned or fascinated, I could not tell which. My sense of propriety was completely scrambled. I was so torn between closing my eyes and stopping my ears that I did neither, and I clung to the curtain edge for dear life – doctors will confirm that suppressed hysterics always make people hold tight to something.
“Yes!” he said, his rich evangelical tone oozing with charm. “You are a noble creature! A woman who speaks the truth for truth’s own sake, who will sacrifice her own pride to spare the honest man who loves her pain – that is the most priceless treasure on earth. If a man marries such a woman, and wins only her esteem and regard, that is more than enough to ennoble his whole life. You spoke of your place in my estimation. Judge for yourself how high it is, when I beg you on my knees, to let me be the one to heal your poor wounded heart. Rachel! Will you honour me, will you bless me, by being my wife?”

I was just about to clamp my hands over my ears, when Rachel spoke the most sensible words I had ever heard pass her lips.
“Godfrey! You must be mad!”
“I have never been more reasonable. For your sake, as well as mine. Are you going to sacrifice your happiness to a man who does not even know how you feel, and who you are determined to never see again? Is it not your duty to yourself to forget this ill-fated attachment? And will you ever forget it moping around this house, the way you are now? Surround yourself with better, nobler interests than the trivial, empty pleasures of the world. A home with peaceful duties, a husband who loves and honours you – try that consolation, Rachel. I do not ask for your love. I will be content only with your affection and regard. The rest we can leave to your husband’s devotion, and to Time, which heals even the deepest wounds.”

She was already softening. Good heavens, what a terrible upbringing that girl must have had! How differently I would have behaved in her position.
“Do not tempt me, Godfrey,” she said. “I am already wretched enough and reckless enough as it is. Do not make me even more reckless.”
“One question, Rachel. Do you have any personal objection to me?”
“I! I always liked you. After what you have just said, I would be completely insensible if I did not respect and admire you too.”
“Do you know how many wives there are who only respect and admire their husbands, and still live perfectly happy lives together? How many brides walk down the aisle without perfectly pure hearts, and still have marriages that work out? The truth is, more women turn to marriage as a refuge than they will ever admit – and they are almost always happy with that choice. At your age, with your attractions, you will never be able to sentence yourself to a single, lonely life. It is only a matter of time. You could marry some stranger years from now, or you could marry the man at your feet, who prizes your respect and admiration more than any other woman’s love on earth.”
“Gently, Godfrey. You are putting a new thought in my head, when all my other prospects are closed off. I am so miserable and desperate right now, if you say one more word, I will marry you on your own terms. Take the warning and go!”
“I will not stand up from my knees until you say yes!”
“If I say yes, you will regret it, and I will regret it, when it is far too late.”
“We will both bless the day I pressed you, and you agreed.”
“Do you really feel as confident as you sound?”
“Judge for yourself. I speak from my own family. My own mother was once exactly where you are. She loved a man who was unworthy of her, and she married my father only out of respect and admiration. You saw how happy they were, living at Frizinghall. Is that not all the encouragement we could ever need?”
“…You will not rush me?”
“All the time in the world is yours.”
“You will never ask me for more love than I am able to give?”
“My angel! I only ask for you.”
“Take me.”

She accepted him. He burst into an unholy rapture, pulling her closer and closer until their faces touched – and I, fumbling to shut my eyes, was a single second too late. I had foolishly expected her to resist. She did not. For any right-thinking woman of my station, that told me everything I needed to know. I waited for them to walk off arm in arm to announce their engagement, but there was one last formality Godfrey insisted on. He sat down on the ottoman right next to her, unchallenged.
“Shall I speak to your mother?” he asked. “Or will you?”
She refused both options. “Do not tell anyone until mamma is better. I want this kept a secret for now, Godfrey. Go home, and come back this evening. We have been alone together for far too long.”

She stood up, and for the first time looked straight toward the small curtained nook I was hiding in.
“Who drew those curtains closed?” she exclaimed. “The room is stuffy enough without blocking all the air out.”
She stepped straight toward me, hand outstretched to yank the curtain open – and right before she could, the voice of the ruddy young footman echoed up the stairs, loud and panicked.
“Miss Rachel! Where are you, Miss Rachel?”
She sprang back from the curtain and ran for the door. The footman stumbled inside, all his colour drained away.
“Please come downstairs at once, Miss! Lady Verinder has fainted, and we cannot wake her.”

In the chaos that followed, I was completely forgotten. I slipped out of my hiding spot, down the hall, and Godfrey rushed past me in the hall, heading out to fetch the doctor. “Go help them inside!” he said, pointing toward the parlour. I found Rachel on her knees by the sofa, her mother’s head resting on her bosom. One look at my aunt’s face, knowing what I knew, was enough to tell me the dreadful truth. The doctor arrived only minutes later, sent Rachel out of the room, and told the rest of us that Lady Verinder was dead.

For pious folk looking for proof of how little the worldly value salvation, it will interest you to know that the doctor did not even glance at my collection of evangelical tracts laid out on the nearby table. Later that night, I peeked into the breakfast room and the library. My aunt had died without opening a single one of the pious letters I had sent her over the past weeks. I was so shocked by that loss that it never even occurred to me until several days later, that she had also died without ever giving me the small, promised legacy she had left me in her will.

The following tight, steadily more prickly exchange of notes passed between Franklin Blake and Miss Drusilla Clack, the pious, fiercely opinionated Verinder relative contributing her first-hand testimony to the growing collection of accounts being compiled to unpack the Moonstone’s baffling disappearance:

1.  Miss Clack presents her compliments to Mr. Franklin Blake. Enclosed with this note is the fifth chapter of her humble personal narrative. She writes that she finds herself entirely unfit to properly expand on the harrowing, unexpected event of Lady Verinder’s death as the gravity of the circumstances demand, and has for that reason attached a large bundle of extracts from precious, carefully preserved religious tracts in her possession, every page relevant to the terrible subject of sudden, unforeseen passing. She fervently prays those extracts will ring out like a trumpet blast to rouse her respected kinsman, Mr. Franklin Blake, to long-overdue serious reflection.

2.  Mr. Franklin Blake presents his compliments to Miss Clack, and thanks her for the fifth chapter of her narrative. He returns the full set of extracts sent along with it, and will refrain from noting any personal distaste he might hold for that particular category of devotional literature. He merely states that the proposed additions to her manuscript are entirely unnecessary for the strictly fact-finding purpose of the narrative he is assembling to solve the Moonstone’s mystery.

3.  Miss Clack sends her thanks for the return of her Extracts. She affectionately reminds Mr. Franklin Blake that she is a devout Christian, and therefore it is literally impossible for him to give her offence. She persists in holding the deepest, most earnest interest in his spiritual wellbeing, and pledges that the first time sickness lays him low, she will press the full use of her carefully curated Extracts on him a second time. In the meanwhile, before she begins writing the final chapters of her narrative, she wishes to know if she has permission to make her humble contribution to the case record complete by drawing on the new, recent discoveries that have come to light around the Moonstone’s baffling theft.

4.  Mr. Franklin Blake regrets having to disappoint Miss Clack. He can only restate the exact instructions he gave her the day she began drafting her narrative: she is strictly requested to limit her writing only to her own personal, first-hand experience of people and events, exactly as they are recorded in her private diary. All later discoveries relating to the mystery are to be left to the pens of the actual, on-the-scene witnesses who saw those events unfold, as per the ground rules of the compilation.

5.  Miss Clack writes again, apologising for troubling Mr. Franklin Blake with yet another letter. Her Extracts have been sent back to her, and her carefully considered, mature views on the subject of the Moonstone have been forbidden from inclusion. She is painfully aware that, by worldly standards, many would say she has been firmly put in her place. But she learned perseverance in the school of adversity, and she will not be dissuaded. Her reason for writing is simple: does Mr. Franklin Blake, who has prohibited every other proposed addition to her manuscript, also prohibit this full sequence of their correspondence from appearing in her published narrative? Some explanation for the position his interference has placed her in, as a serious authoress, is only a matter of basic justice, and she is most eager that their letters be allowed to speak for themselves without editorial meddling.

6.  Mr. Franklin Blake agrees to Miss Clack’s proposal, on the strict condition that she treats this note as his final word on the matter, and that this consent closes all further correspondence between the two of them on the subject of her narrative.

7.  Miss Clack writes one last time, stating that it is an act of Christian duty, before their correspondence ceases, to inform Mr. Franklin Blake that his last letter — clearly written with the deliberate intent to offend her — failed utterly to achieve that uncharitable goal. She affectionately requests Mr. Franklin Blake to retire to the privacy of his own room, and reflect carefully on whether the devout training that can elevate a poor, weak woman like herself entirely out of the reach of insult is not far more worthy of his admiration than he is currently disposed to admit. The moment he sends her a note acknowledging that admiration, she solemnly pledges to mail the full complete set of her precious Extracts straight back to him, no further discussion required.

To this final note, no reply of any kind was ever received. No further comment is needed.
(Signed) Drusilla Clack

My next task is to lay out every scrap of new information I hold relating to the Moonstone—more specifically, the long-running Indian plot to steal the gem—information that, trivial as it might sound on first glance, bears such sharp relevance to events still unfolding that no reader who cares to untangle this case can afford to skip over it.

A week to ten days after Miss Verinder departed her family’s estate, one of my clerks stepped into my private office, card in hand, to tell me a gentleman waiting downstairs requested an audience. I glanced at the card: the foreign name scrawled across the face escapes me now, but the handwritten English line at the bottom I can recall word for word: *Recommended by Mr. Septimus Luker*.

The sheer audacity of a man of Luker’s shady, disreputable standing daring to send a potential client to *me*—a solicitor of established, unblemished reputation—left me so stunned I sat silent for a beat, wondering if my eyes had played tricks. My clerk, noting my bewilderment, volunteered his own first impression of the visitor below.
“Remarkable-looking fellow, sir. Swarthy enough that the whole office pegged him for an Indian, or something of that Oriental sort.”

That one line tied straight to the note on the card, and I knew instantly the Moonstone was at the root of both Luker’s peculiar recommendation and the stranger’s call. To my clerk’s obvious shock, I told him to show the gentleman right in.
Before any reader chides me for setting aside professional prudence for nothing more than idle curiosity, let me remind them: no other living soul in England can claim the deep, long-held connection to the Indian diamond saga that I do. I was the one Colonel Herncastle trusted with his secret plan to evade assassination at the hands of the three devotees. I received his regular letters confirming he was still alive and in hiding. I drafted his will, leaving the Moonstone to Miss Verinder. I talked his hesitant executor into carrying out the bequest, on the chance the jewel might prove a rare and valuable gift for the Verinder family. And finally, I argued Franklin Blake out of his scruples, and convinced him to carry the diamond himself to Lady Verinder’s Yorkshire estate. If anyone has a right to take personal interest in every last detail related to the Moonstone, that man is me.

The moment the visitor stepped across my threshold, I felt a jolt of certainty: I was face to face with one of the three Indians, almost certainly their chief. He was dressed head to toe in immaculate European clothing, but nothing could hide his swarthy complexion, his long, lean, lithe frame, or the quiet, graceful, grave courtesy of his manners—all dead giveaways of his Oriental origin to any pair of eyes paying the least bit of attention. I gestured to a chair, and asked him to state his business.

He apologized in fluent, carefully chosen English for disturbing my work, then laid a small parcel on my desk. It was wrapped first in cloth of gold, then a second layer of fine silk. When he peeled both away, he revealed an ebony casket, exquisitely inlaid with tiny gemstones across every inch of its surface.
“I have come, sir,” he said, “to ask you to lend me a sum of money. This casket will stand as my surety, to guarantee my debt will be repaid in full.”
I tapped the line on his calling card. “And you were directed to me on Mr. Luker’s recommendation?”
He bowed.
“Might I ask why Mr. Luker himself did not advance you the funds you require?”
“Mr. Luker informed me, sir, he had no money free to lend.”
“And so he sent you straight to me?”
The Indian nodded, and tapped the card himself. “It is written there.”

Short, direct, no wasted words. I had no doubt if the Moonstone had been sitting on my desk that day, this polite, graceful man would have cut my throat without a single second of hesitation. That small flaw aside, though, he was the perfect model of a client. He made no attempt to waste my time, as every other British stranger I’d ever met in my position would have done.
“I am sorry you wasted the trip,” I told him. “Mr. Luker was entirely mistaken to send you here. Like every other solicitor of my standing, I do arrange loans for clients, but I never lend to strangers, and I never accept such a high-value, unproven surety as your casket as collateral.”

Instead of arguing, wheedling, or pressing me to bend my rules as any other man would have, the Indian only bowed again. He wrapped his casket back up in its silk and gold cloth wrappings without a single word of protest, and stood to leave.
“Will you extend the courtesy of answering one last question, before I take my leave?” he asked.
I bowed back. One question on parting? Most of my clients would have badgered me for fifty.
“Supposing, sir, that it had been both possible and customary for you to lend me that money,” he said, “what is the shortest length of time in which I would be allowed to pay the debt back?”
“Per standard British lending practice,” I answered, “you would be fully within your rights to repay the entire sum, if you wished, exactly one year from the day the loan was first advanced to you.”

He gave me the lowest, deepest bow of all, then slipped out of the room so softly, moving with that noiseless, supple, cat-like grace, that I admit it gave me a small, unshakable jolt. When I collected my wits enough to think it through, I landed on one very clear conclusion. The whole performance: the fancy gemmed casket, the formal application for a loan, the carefully polite demeanor—none of it was real. It was all a deliberate ruse, designed to get him alone in my office long enough to ask that one final question, and get that exact answer about the one-year repayment window. The only moment he had ever dropped his perfectly masked, unreadable, unflappable facade was the exact second I gave him that piece of information, when he looked me dead in the eye for the first and only time during our meeting.

I was just turning that over in my head, trying to work out what on earth he wanted with a 12-month timeline, when a letter was delivered to my desk. It was from none other than Septimus Luker himself. The letter oozed sickening, servile apology, begging me to grant him a private audience so he could explain his actions to my full satisfaction.
Curiosity won out over professional propriety for the second time that day. I wrote back to schedule an appointment for him to come to my office the following afternoon.

Luker was every bit the vulgar, ugly, cringing, tedious little man I expected him to be, not worth wasting many words on here. The gist of his story took barely five minutes to hear.
The day before the Indian had visited my office, he had turned up on Luker’s doorstep. Luker had recognized him instantly: the chief of the three Indian devotees who had loitered around his house months before, the very man who had once broken in, blindfolded him, gagged him, and robbed him of his bank receipt for the Moonstone. Luker had been so paralyzed with terror that he was convinced he was about to be murdered on the spot.
The Indian had played the perfect unknowing stranger, pulled out the gemmed casket, and made the exact same loan request he would later make to me. To get rid of him as fast as humanly possible, Luker immediately said he had no funds to lend. When the Indian asked who the most respectable, safest solicitor in London was for a man looking to take out a private loan, my name popped out of Luker’s mouth before he even thought, purely because he was so panicked my name was the first one he could drag up from his memory.
“The sweat was pouring off me like rain, sir,” the sniveling creature finished. “I didn’t know what I was saying. I beg you to overlook it, Mr. Bruff, I was truly scared out of my wits.”
I excused him graciously—if only to get him out of my sight as fast as possible. Before he left, I asked one last question: had the Indian asked him any odd, notable question before he left his house?
Yes. He had asked the exact same parting question, about the earliest possible repayment date for a loan, and Luker had given him the exact same answer: one full year.

What did it all mean? Luker’s story gave me no clues at all. My own wits, sharp as they are, couldn’t untangle the puzzle. That evening I had a dinner engagement to attend, and I headed up the stairs to my dressing room in a foul, frustrated mood, never suspecting that the short walk to my wardrobe would double as the straight path to the answer I had been chasing.

The undisputed star of the dinner party guest list was Mr. Murthwaite, the famed Eastern traveller. Fresh off years of trekking through uncharted, danger-ridden territories, he’d become a darling of London society—everyone adored a man who’d cheated death so many times just to come back and tell the tale. Now he’d announced he was setting off again to push deeper into the unmapped wilds of Central Asia, and that bold indifference to his own safety had revived all the fading public fascination around him. The odds were stacked so heavily against him surviving this second journey that every guest at the table treated him like a living ghost: the sort of eminent dinner companion you could enjoy knowing the next news you heard of him would almost certainly be a report of his murder somewhere in the mountains.

As soon as the ladies withdrew to the drawing room, the gentlemen naturally fell to arguing politics, that dreary national pastime all Englishmen are obligated to indulge in the second no women are around to moderate them. I happen to be one of the most un-English Englishmen alive when it comes to politics—all that back-and-forth strikes me as the most tedious, profitless waste of breath imaginable. Glancing down the table after the first bottle of port had made its round, I spotted Murthwaite clearly shared my low opinion: he was leaning back in his chair, very politely and dexterously fighting off a nap, not wanting to offend his host by admitting the political bickering was putting him to sleep.

I decided it was well worth testing if a well-timed reference to the Moonstone would jolt him fully awake, and if it did, hear what this man who knew Indian customs far better than any of us in Gray’s Inn made of the latest twist in the Indian conspiracy that had turned up in my office only the day before.
“If I’m not mistaken, Mr. Murthwaite,” I said, “you were acquainted with the late Lady Verinder, and took some interest in that bizarre string of events that ended with the Moonstone going missing?”

The great traveller snapped wide awake in an instant, and fixed me with a sharp, curious look. “Who are you, sir?”
I told him I was Mr. Bruff of Gray’s Inn Square, the lawyer who’d drawn up Colonel Herncastle’s will, and who’d managed the old soldier’s affairs right up to his death.

Murthwaite shifted his chair sharply, turning his back on every other man at the table—Conservative and Liberal alike—so he could focus all his attention entirely on me.
“Have you heard anything of the three Indian pilgrims lately?” he asked, no preamble.
“I have every reason to believe one of them paid me a visit at my office yesterday,” I replied.

That answer staggered even the unshakable Murthwaite. I laid out every detail of what had happened to Mr. Luker, then what had played out in my own office, exactly as I’ve recorded it here.
“His last question clearly had a purpose,” I added. “Why on earth would he care what exact time a moneylender allows a borrower to pay back their loan and redeem their pledge?”
“Can’t you see his motive, Mr. Bruff?” he asked.
“I’m ashamed to say I don’t,” I admitted.

Murthwaite leaned forward, eager to walk me through it. “Let’s retrace the entire conspiracy from the moment you drew up Herncastle’s will. It’s in your best interest, and in Miss Verinder’s, to understand every thread of this plot, so you can act fast if the Indians make their next move. First off: the three men we’re dealing with now are not the same three Brahmins who followed Colonel Herncastle to England decades ago. They’re all under forty, which makes them the next generation of successors, sent here by their order to pick up where their predecessors left off. And they didn’t just inherit the mission—they inherited the small, tight underground network the first three pilgrims built here: a pool of available cash, a handful of shadowy English fixers who operate in London’s immigrant underbelly, and the quiet loyalty of a small community of Indian expats working menial jobs across the city who share their faith. It’s not a formidable operation by our Western standards, but it’s effective.”

“Now, when did the Indians get their first real shot at the Moonstone?” he asked.
“The day Colonel Herncastle died,” I answered immediately. “They must have known the diamond was no longer locked in the bank vault under his name.”
“Exactly. They got a copy of his will from Doctors’ Commons via one of their fixers, found out the jewel was bequeathed to Rachel Verinder, and figured out the safest way to go after it was to wait until it was brought up to Lady Verinder’s Yorkshire estate, rather than risk a heist on the London bank. That’s why they turned up in Frizinghall disguised as travelling jugglers, laying low to wait for their chance.”

I nodded, but raised the obvious problem. “But Franklin Blake spotted one of their spies following him in London, raced north hours earlier than planned, and stashed the Moonstone in the Frizinghall bank before the Indians even knew he was in the county. For weeks before Rachel’s birthday, they must have thought the diamond was right in Lady Verinder’s house—why didn’t they make any attempt to break in? I don’t buy that story of their clairvoyant little boy, either. That’s nonsense to a practical man like me.”
“To me too,” Murthwaite laughed. “That clairvoyance trick is just romantic theatre for them—they use mild mesmerism on the boy to make him reflect what they’re all already thinking, and call him a seer to stoke their own morale on this long, dangerous errand. No magic involved. The real reason they didn’t storm the house? No Indian will ever take an unnecessary risk. They knew per the will that the Moonstone would be Rachel’s absolute, unchallenged property on her 18th birthday. Why risk tangling with Franklin Blake, who’d already proven he could suspect and outwit them, when they could wait a few more weeks for the diamond to belong to an innocent young girl who would no doubt parade it around her neck for every guest to see? And their patience paid off—they got to the house on her birthday, and saw the Moonstone pinned to the front of her dress, just as they predicted. I told everyone at the party that night they should cut the diamond into smaller, unidentifiable stones to break the curse of its legend. But then it vanished that same night, the Indians got locked up as vagrants the next day, and their entire first plan fell apart.”

He pulled out his pocket book, flipped to a marked page. “Their second chance came while they were still in that Frizinghall jail. I was staying nearby at the time, and the prison governor came to me with a letter delivered to the Indians, postmarked Lambeth, written in Hindustani. I copied the original and my translation down right here.”

He pushed the open book across to me. The letter ran short, no signature, no date:
*In the name of the Regent of the Night, whose seat is on the Antelope, whose arms embrace the four corners of the earth.
Brothers, turn your faces to the south, and come to me in the street of many noises, which leads down to the muddy river.
The reason is this.
My own eyes have seen it.*
“The Regent of the Night is the Moon God in Hindu mythology,” Murthwaite explained. “The man who sent that letter was a foreign workman Mr. Luker had just fired from his shop on Lambeth. He was the Indians’ inside man, who’d gotten the Moonstone after it vanished from Yorkshire, and passed word to the conspirators in jail exactly where it was being kept. As soon as they were released, they caught the first train to London, and camped outside Luker’s house to watch him. Luker got the diamond into his own banker’s strongroom before they could break in, and they ended up with nothing but his bank receipt, proving the Moonstone was locked away there.”

That was when it clicked. I jumped in my seat. “I see now! The Indians know Luker took the diamond as a one-year pledge. They were asking me exactly what hour the loan matures, so they’ll know the very first minute the thief who pledged the diamond can walk into that bank, pay back the money, and take the Moonstone back!”

Murthwaite nodded. “If the diamond was pawned around the end of last June, that date will be the very last week of June, 1849. I’ll be thousands of miles deep in Central Asia then, far away from all English news. But you, Mr. Bruff? You’d be a fool not to mark that date down in your diary, and plan to be in London when it rolls around.”
“You think something serious will happen?” I asked.
“I think I’d be far safer trekking with the most violent fanatics in the mountains than I would be walking through that bank door with the Moonstone in my pocket,” he said gravely. “They’ve been defeated twice already. I would bet every penny I own they will not fail a third time.”

That was the last we spoke of the subject. Coffee was brought in, the rest of the guests rose to stretch their legs, and we all made our way upstairs to join the ladies in the drawing room.
I immediately made a note of the date, and I’ll write it here now, to close my part of this narrative:
With that, I hand over the pen I’ve no further right to use, to the next narrator of this story:
Contributed by Franklin Blake.

Spring of 1849 found me drifting across the sun-scorched wastes of the Near East, my travel plans tossed over after a last-minute whim that altered every stop I’d mapped out months prior, every detail I’d forwarded to my lawyer and banker back in London. That sudden shift left me no choice but to dispatch a trusted servant to the city I’d cut from my itinerary, to collect my piled-up letters and waiting remittances from the English consul there, with orders to rejoin my encampment at a fixed time and place.
A mishap entirely out of his control delayed the man a full week. For seven long days my small party and I waited, camped on the ragged edge of the desert, the sand piling higher at our tent flaps every windless noon. He finally stumbled to the tent opening at the end of that stretch, purse full of my money and a stack of letters in his hand, his face drawn.
“I’m afraid I bring bad news, sir,” he said, and pointed to one envelope edged all around with black mourning crepe, its address scrawled in the familiar handwriting of Mr. Bruff, my lawyer.
There is no torment sharper than the sick suspense of that moment. I ripped the black-edged letter open first. It told me my father was dead, and I was heir to his entire vast fortune. The wealth that had landed so unexpectedly in my lap came heavy with responsibility, and Mr. Bruff begged me to lose not a single hour hurrying back to England. By daybreak the next morning, I was already on the road toward the nearest port bound for home.
My old friend Gabriel Betteredge’s portrait of me the day I left England, all that time ago, is a little overdrawn, if I am honest. He took one of Rachel Verinder’s sharp, playful jabs at my patchwork foreign education completely seriously, and convinced himself he actually saw the fussy French, brooding German, and dramatic Italian sides of my character my cousin had only ever mocked for a joke — traits that never existed anywhere but in the old steward’s warm, whimsical imagination. Setting that silly exaggeration aside, though, he told the unvarnished truth when he wrote that I left England raw to the bone, gutted by the worst disappointment of my entire life, cut deep by Rachel’s rejection of me.
I’d gone abroad determined that if change and distance could help me forget her, I would. I still believe it is a lie to say absence and new sights do nothing to dull that kind of grief: they force your attention away from staring fixed, unblinking, at your own sorrow. I never forgot Rachel — not for a single day — but the sharp, cutting edge of the memory softened little by little, as time, miles, and a hundred strange new scenes slipped between her and me.
But the second I turned my ship’s prow toward England, all that slow-won healing unspooled just as steadily. The closer I drew to the country she lived in, the closer I got to the chance of seeing her again, the tighter her old hold on me coiled around my chest. When I left England, her name was the last I would have let cross my lips. When I stepped off the boat and met Mr. Bruff in his office, she was the very first person I asked about.
He told me every last thing that had happened while I was gone, every event that picks up where Betteredge’s earlier narrative left off — save for one secret. At that point, Mr. Bruff was not at liberty to tell me the private, unspoken reasons that had led Rachel and Godfrey Ablewhite to call off their engagement. I did not press him on the delicate subject. It was relief enough, after the sick jolt of learning she had once nearly married that man, to know she had thought better of it, and broken the engagement of her own free will.
Once I had the full story of the past, my next questions all circled right back to Rachel, naturally. Who was caring for her after she left Mr. Bruff’s house? Where was she living now?
She was under the guardianship of Mrs. Merridew, a widowed sister of the late Sir John Verinder, whom her mother’s executors had asked to take charge of her. The two got on famously, I was told, and were settled for the season at Mrs. Merridew’s house in Portland Place.
Half an hour after hearing that news, I was hailing a cab for Portland Place — I didn’t even work up the courage to admit where I was going to Mr. Bruff. The footman who answered the door could not say for certain if Miss Verinder was at home, so I sent my card upstairs to settle the question straight away. He came back down a minute later, his face perfectly blank, to tell me Miss Verinder was out.
