I might have suspected almost anyone else of deliberately hiding from me. But I could never suspect Rachel. I left word I would call again at six that evening. At six o’clock, I was told a second time that Miss Verinder was not at home. No message had been left for me. The footman politely confirmed she had received my card.
There was no way to miss the plain meaning: Rachel was refusing to see me. I had no intention of accepting that treatment without at least trying to find out why. I sent my name in to Mrs. Merridew, asking for a private interview at whatever hour was most convenient for her.
She received me immediately, a soft, comfortable little elderly lady in a cozy sitting room, full of polite surprise and regret — for my sake, of course. But she could not offer me a single explanation, she said, nor could she press Rachel on a matter that was entirely a question of private feeling. She repeated that line over and over, with unshakable gentle patience, and that was all I got for my trouble.
My last remaining option was to write to Rachel. The next day I sent my servant round with the note, strict orders to wait there until he got a reply. The note that came back was exactly one sentence, no more: “Miss Verinder begs to decline entering into any correspondence with Mr. Franklin Blake.”
I was so stung by that cold, deliberate insult I could barely think straight. Mr. Bruff walked in to speak to me on business before I had calmed down, and I brushed the business aside entirely to lay the whole mess out for him. He was just as clueless as Mrs. Merridew. No slander about me had ever reached Rachel’s ears, he swore. She had never once spoken my name while she stayed under his roof. She had not even once asked, through all my long years away, if I was alive or dead.
I pulled Lady Verinder’s old letter out of my pocketbook — the one she had written to me from Frizinghall the very day I left her Yorkshire house, back when the Moonstone first vanished — and pointed Bruff to two lines I had never been able to forget:
“The valuable assistance which you rendered to the inquiry after the lost jewel is still an unpardoned offence, in the present dreadful state of Rachel’s mind. Moving blindfold in this matter, you have added to the burden of anxiety which she has had to bear, by innocently threatening her secret with discovery through your exertions.”
“Is it possible,” I asked him, “that the anger she felt toward me back then is still just as bitter, a year later?”
Mr. Bruff looked genuinely distressed. “If you force me to answer straight,” he said, “I can read no other meaning into her behavior.”
I rang the bell at once, told my servant to pack my portmanteau and send out for a railway guide. Bruff stared at me, bewildered, and asked where I was going.
“To Yorkshire,” I said. “By the very next train.”
“Whatever for?”
“Mr. Bruff,” I told him, “the help I gave that investigation into the Moonstone, entirely by accident, was an unpardonable offense to Rachel nearly a year ago — and it still is. I will not accept that. I am going to dig up the secret behind why she stayed silent to her own mother, and why she hates me. If time, work, and money can do it, I will track down the thief who stole the Moonstone.”
The good old lawyer tried to remonstrate with me, to talk me down, to do his duty and talk me into sense. I did not hear a word of it. No power on earth could have shaken my resolution that moment.
“I am picking up the investigation exactly where I left it,” I went on. “I will follow it step by step, all the way to the present day. There are gaps in the evidence I could never fill on my own — gaps Gabriel Betteredge can supply. And that is exactly where I am going.”
By sunset that same evening, I was standing on the familiar old terrace of the Verinder country house, staring at the peaceful, ivy-draped old home I had not seen in so long. The gardener was the first person I spotted in the quiet grounds. He told me he had left Betteredge an hour earlier, sunning himself in his usual corner of the back yard. I knew that spot like the back of my own hand, so I set off to find him myself.
I wandered down the familiar old paths, rounded the corner, and peered through the open gate of the back yard. There he was — my dear old friend, from all the happy days that felt long gone — sat in his old spot in his creaky beehive chair, pipe clamped between his teeth, his dog-eared copy of *Robinson Crusoe* open across his lap, two of the house dogs dozing on either side of his feet.
The last slanting rays of the setting sun threw my long shadow across the yard. The dogs caught sight of it, or caught my scent on the breeze, and jolted up with a low growl. Betteredge jumped right after them, quieted them with a single sharp word, then shaded his failing old eyes with one hand, squinting curiously at the figure standing in the gate.
My own eyes were full of tears. I had to pause for a long, quiet moment before I could trust myself to speak to him.

“Betteredge!” I said, nodding straight at the tattered, page-curled copy of *Robinson Crusoe* resting on his knee, the same well-worn volume he’d carried through half his life, “did Crusoe himself warn you this evening to expect a visit from Franklin Blake?”

“By the Lord Harry, Mr. Franklin!” the old man cried, scrambling to his feet with my help, “that’s exactly what he did!” He swayed for a moment, glancing back and forth between his beloved book and my face, clearly unable to decide which of the two had surprised him more. The verdict fell in the book’s favour: he held it open in both hands, staring at the pages with a look of unutterable, giddy anticipation, as if he half expected Robinson Crusoe himself to step out of the print and shake our hands.

“Here’s the exact line, sir!” he blurted once he’d recovered his wits. “As I live by bread, it was right under my nose the second you knocked! Page one hundred and fifty-six, word for word: ‘I stood like one Thunderstruck, or as if I had seen an Apparition.’ If that isn’t a prophecy of your sudden turning up, there’s no sense left in the English language!” He slammed the covers shut hard enough to puff a cloud of old dust off the top edge, freed one hand at last to clasp mine warmly.

Naturally, I expected him to flood me with questions at once. But no—hospitality won out first, as it always did for the old servant the moment any member of the Verinder household turned up at the door, no matter the circumstances.

“Walk in, Mr. Franklin,” he said, stepping back with that quaint, old-fashioned bow of his. “I’ll ask what brings you later—first we’ll get you comfortable. There’s been sad changes since you left, you know. The manor’s shut up, all the other servants are gone. Never you mind that! I’ll cook your dinner myself, the gardener’s wife will make up a bed for you, and if there’s a bottle of that famous aged Latour claret left in the cellar, it’s going straight down your throat tonight. You’re heartily welcome, sir—welcome home!” The old fellow fought valiantly against the hush and gloom of the empty, shuttered house, greeting me with all the warm, easy courtesy he’d extended to me as a boy.

It grieved me to disappoint him. But this was Rachel’s house now. After the bitter, unexplainable quarrel that had driven us apart in London, no amount of affection for Betteredge could make me cross that threshold. My self-respect forbade it, plain and simple. I took him by the arm and led him out into the overgrown garden, where I had no choice but to lay the full truth out before him. Torn between his loyalty to Rachel and his fondness for me, he fidgeted and frowned for a long minute, before delivering his verdict in his usual unvarnished style, steeped in that no-nonsense “Betteredge school” of positive philosophy he was so famous for.

“Miss Rachel has her faults, I’ll never deny that,” he began. “Riding the high horse every now and then is one of ’em. She’s been trying to ride roughshod over you, and you’ve put up with it. Lord, Mr. Franklin, you ought to know women better than that by now! You’ve heard me talk of my late wife, haven’t you?”

I’d heard him invoke the late Mrs. Betteredge dozens of times, holding her up as living proof of the inherent perversity of the female sex, and he called on her example now.

“Very well, then. Every woman’s got her own way of climbing on that high horse. My missus would mount hers every time I turned down something she’d set her heart on. Sure as I came home from work, she’d holler down the kitchen stairs that my brutal treatment had left her too broken to cook my dinner. I put up with it for a while, just like you’re putting up with Miss Rachel now. At last my patience ran out. I marched upstairs, picked her up gentle, carried her holus-bolus straight into the best parlour where she received her visitors, and said ‘That’s the proper place for you, my dear.’ Then I went back down, locked myself in the kitchen, took off my coat, rolled up my sleeves, and cooked my own dinner. Ate every bite of it with a fine appetite, had my pipe and my glass of grog afterwards, scrubbed every pot and pan, polished the knives, swept the hearth until the whole kitchen was brighter and cleaner than she’d ever made it. Then I unlocked the door and let her in. ‘I’ve had my dinner, my dear,’ I said, ‘and I hope you find I’ve left the kitchen exactly how you like it.’ For the rest of her life, Mr. Franklin, I never had to cook a single meal again! Moral of the story: you put up with Miss Rachel’s nonsense in London, don’t put up with it here in Yorkshire. Come back to the house with me!”

It was quite unanswerable. I could only tell my dear old friend that even his formidable powers of persuasion could not move me that day.

“It’s a lovely evening,” I said. “I’ll walk to Frizinghall and stay at the inn there, and you must come and breakfast with me tomorrow morning. I’ve something very important to tell you.”

Betteredge shook his head gravely. “I’m right sorry to hear that. I’d hoped you’d come back to find things smooth and pleasant between you and Miss Rachel again. But if you’re set on your own way, sir, there’s no need to trudge all the way to Frizinghall for a bed. You can get lodgings far closer. Hotherstone’s Farm is barely two miles off, down the valley. You can’t object to staying there on Miss Rachel’s account, now can you?” he added with a little sly grin. “Hotherstone lives on land he owns free and clear, no ties to the manor at all.”

I remembered the place the second he named it: a cosy farmhouse tucked beside the prettiest trout stream in that part of Yorkshire, with spare rooms the farmer and his wife let out to artists, anglers, and passing tourists. It was far more pleasant a spot to stay for the coming weeks than any Frizinghall inn.

“Are the rooms free?” I asked.

“Mrs. Hotherstone herself begged me just yesterday to recommend her lodgings to any visitors passing this way.”

“Then I’ll take them, Betteredge, gladly.”

We walked back to the yard where I’d left my travelling bag. Betteredge slipped a stick through its handle, heaved it over his shoulder, and fell back into the bewildered daze my arrival had stirred in him earlier. He stared disbelievingly at the shuttered manor house, then swivelled around to stare at me even more disbelievingly.

“I’ve lived a long while in this world,” the dearest of all old servants grumbled, “but I never thought I’d live to see this sight. There stands the Verinder house, here stands Mr. Franklin Blake—and, damme if the pair of them aren’t turning their backs on each other, and you’re going to sleep in a farmer’s lodgings!” He led the way out of the yard, shaking his head and growling playfully. “There’s only one miracle left that can top this. Next thing I know, Mr. Franklin, you’ll be paying me back that seven-and-sixpence you borrowed from me when you were a boy to buy a fireworks show.”

That little jab put him in far better humour. We passed through the lodge gates, and the moment we were clear of the manor grounds, Betteredge’s duties of hospitality fell away, and his long-buried curiosity burst to the surface. He slowed his step to walk level with mine.

“Fine evening for a stroll, Mr. Franklin,” he said, as if we’d just accidentally bumped into each other on the lane. “If you’d gone to that Frizinghall inn, I’d have had the honour of breakfasting with you tomorrow, wouldn’t I?”

“Of course you would. You can still come and breakfast with me at Hotherstone’s Farm instead.”

“Much obliged, sir. But it wasn’t exactly breakfast I was after. You said you had something important to say to me? If it’s no secret,” he said, dropping all pretense at once, “I’m burning to know what’s brought you rushing down here all of a sudden like this.”

“What brought me here before, years back?” I asked.

“The Moonstone, Mr. Franklin. But what brings you now?”

“The Moonstone again, Betteredge.”

The old man stopped dead in his tracks, staring at me through the gathering grey twilight as if he could not trust his own ears. “If that’s a joke, sir, I’m getting too dull in my old age to see the funny side of it.”

“It’s no joke,” I said. “I’ve come back to pick up the investigation that was dropped when I left England. I’m here to do what no one has done yet—find out who stole the Diamond.”

“Leave that cursed jewel be, Mr. Franklin! Take my advice, and let it rest. That accursed Indian stone has led every soul who got near it astray. Don’t waste your youth, your money, and your temper meddling with the Moonstone. How do you expect to succeed, saving your presence, when Sergeant Cuff himself made a total mess of the case? Sergeant Cuff!” he repeated, shaking his forefinger at me sternly. “The greatest police detective in all of England!”

“My mind is made up. Even Sergeant Cuff’s reputation won’t daunt me. By the by, do you know where he is these days? I may need to speak to him before long.”

“The Sergeant can’t help you, Mr. Franklin.”

“Why not?”

“Something happened in police circles right after you left England. The great Cuff retired. He’s got a tiny cottage down at Dorking now, and he’s up to his eyebrows growing roses. I had a letter in his own hand last month saying he’d managed to breed a white moss rose without budding it on dog rose stock first. Old Mr. Begbie, the head gardener up at the manor, is going to travel down there any day now and admit Cuff finally beat him at his own game.”

“It doesn’t matter much,” I said. “I’ll do without Sergeant Cuff. I’m relying on you to help me get started.”

I must have spoken a little carelessly, because Betteredge looked distinctly piqued. “You could do a lot worse than rely on me, sir, I can tell you that.” The sharpness in his tone, and the faint, unmistakeable restlessness that followed it, told me he was holding back some secret he was hesitant to share.

“I expect you to help me sift through all the fragments of evidence Sergeant Cuff left behind,” I said. “I know you can do that. Can’t you do more than that?”

“What more could you possibly ask of me, sir?” he asked, feigning perfect humility.

“I expect more than that, from the way you just spoke.”

“Mere boasting, Mr. Franklin,” the old man insisted stubbornly. “Some folk are born boasters, and they never grow out of it till their dying day. I’m one of them.”

There was only one way to move him: I appealed to his loyalty to Rachel, and to his long fondness for me.

“Betteredge, would you be glad to hear that Rachel and I were friends again, no hard feelings between us?”

“I’ve served this family my whole life, sir, if you need to ask that you’ve got no sense at all!”

“You remember how Rachel treated me just before I left England? No one, not even her own mother, could work out why she was so mortally angry with me for trying to get her diamond back.”

“As well as if it was yesterday!”

“Exactly. And when I came back from travelling just last week, I found she was still that angry. She won’t let me see her. She won’t answer any of my letters. The only possible way I can clear this whole mess up, the only chance Rachel has left me to make things right, is to solve the mystery of the Moonstone once and for all.”

The words shook him. He asked a question that proved his last defenses were crumbling. “You’re not doing this out of spite, are you, Mr. Franklin?”

“I was angry when I left London, yes,” I said. “But that’s all gone now. All I want is for Rachel to talk to me, to clear up whatever secret she’s been hiding.”

“And you’re not afraid, sir—if you dig up the truth, that you’ll find out something about Miss Rachel that will change how you see her?”

I understood that fierce, protective loyalty to his young mistress that made him ask that. “I trust her as completely as you do. Whatever her secret is, it can never make me think less of her.”

All of Betteredge’s remaining scruples vanished. “If helping you is wrong, Mr. Franklin,” he exclaimed, “I’m as innocent of seeing that as an unborn babe! I can put you right on the path to solving this, if you’ll go the rest of the way yourself. You remember that poor girl we had working at the manor, Rosanna Spearman?”

“Of course I do.”

“You always thought she had some kind of confession about the Moonstone that she was dying to tell you, didn’t you?”

“I could never explain her strange behaviour any other way.”

“You can set that doubt to rest right now, Mr. Franklin.”

I stopped dead in my tracks, squinting to make out his face in the dark. “What do you mean?”

“Steady, sir! Rosanna Spearman left a sealed letter behind her, addressed straight to you, and no one else.”

“Where is it?”

“With that friend of hers who lives down at Cobb’s Hole. You must have heard of Limping Lucy, the fisherman’s daughter who walks with a crutch?”

“Aye, the one with the bad leg.”

“That’s her. She refused to hand that letter over to anyone but you. You’d already left England before I could even write to tell you about it.”

“Come on then, Betteredge—let’s go get it right now!”

“Too late tonight, sir. All the folk down on the coast hoard their candles, and they all turn in at sundown. You’ll knock on Lucy’s door and no one will answer.”

“Nonsense! We can be there in half an hour.”

“You might, sir. And you’d still find the door bolted fast.” He pointed to a warm, glimmering light glowing below us in the valley, and at that exact moment I heard the soft bubbling of the trout stream through the still evening air. “There’s Hotherstone’s Farm, right there. Make yourself comfortable tonight, come and fetch me tomorrow morning, and we’ll head down to Cobb’s Hole together—first thing, whenever you like.”

We headed down the winding path towards the light, the murmur of the stream growing louder at our feet.

My memories of my night at Hotherstone’s Farm are nothing but a jumble of vivid, disjointed impressions: the loud, warm welcome from the farmhands, a supper so massive it could have fed an entire village of East End laborers, a bedroom so spotless I could have eaten off the floor—ruined only by that abominable leftover of our ancestors’ foolish fads, a feather bed. I tossed for hours, striking match after match to light my tiny bedside candle, and heaved such a huge sigh of relief when the sun finally crested the horizon that I could have cheered.

We’d agreed the night before with Betteredge that I could rouse him any time I liked on our way to Cobb’s Hole, and my desperate eagerness to get my hands on Rosanna Spearman’s letter meant “any time” was as soon as humanly possible. I didn’t stop for breakfast, just stuffed a crust of bread in my pocket and set off, half-convinced I’d catch the old steward still snoring under his covers. To my immense relief, he was every bit as wired for the day ahead as I was: standing on his doorstep, walking stick already in his hand, waiting for me.

“Good morning, Betteredge. How are you feeling?”
“Terrible, sir. Got a new disease I invented myself, don’t want to alarm you but you’ll catch it before the morning’s out.”
“Devil I will, eh?”
“Feel that hot twist in the pit of your stomach? That thump behind your eyes? Ah, not yet? It’ll hit you the second we reach Cobb’s Hole. I call it detective-fever. First caught it hanging around Sergeant Cuff.”
“Right, right, and the cure’s opening Rosanna’s letter, I take it? Come on, let’s go.”

Early as we were, the fisherman’s cottage kitchen was already bustling. Betteredge introduced me, and Mrs. Yolland performed a rare social ritual reserved only for guests of distinction: she set a bottle of Dutch gin and two polished clean pipes on the table, and opened with, “What news from London, sir?”
Before I could fumble an answer to that impossibly broad question, a figure limped out of the dark corner of the room: a gaunt, wild girl with hair of remarkable beauty and a burning, fierce sharpness to her eyes. She thumped over on her crutch to my table, staring at me like I was an object of equal parts terrible fascination and horror.
“Mr. Betteredge,” she said, never taking her eyes off me. “Say his name again, please.”
“This gentleman,” Betteredge replied, leaning hard on the word “gentleman” out of respect, “is Mr. Franklin Blake.”

The girl turned her back on me and vanished. I barely registered Mrs. Yolland stammering apologies for her daughter Lucy’s odd behavior, or Betteredge translating those apologies into polite English for me. My entire attention was fixed on the thud of her crutch: thump-thump up the wooden stairs, thump-thump across the upper room, thump-thump back down the stairs. There she was in the open door, letter in hand, beckoning me outside.
I left the trailing stream of apologies behind me and followed her, her crutch thumping faster and faster down the slope of the beach. She led me behind a line of beached fishing boats, out of sight and earshot of the few villagers up and about, and stopped dead to face me for the first time.
“Stand there. Let me look at you.”

There was no mistaking the raw, unfiltered loathing on her face: she viewed me with total abhorrence and disgust. No other woman had ever let me see that look directed at me before, and it was impossible to endure it for long. I tried to redirect her to the obvious matter at hand.
“I believe you have a letter for me?”
“Say that again.”
I repeated the line, like a reciting schoolchild.
“No,” she muttered, half to herself, eyes still fixed unflinchingly on me. “I can’t see what she saw in his face. Can’t guess what she heard in his voice.” She dropped her gaze, resting her head wearily on the top of her crutch, and her voice went soft, the first gentle tone I’d heard from her. “Oh my poor dear lost Rosanna, what could you ever have seen in this man?”
She snapped her head back up, furious again. “Can you eat and drink? Can you sleep? When you see a poor working girl, do you feel no remorse at all?”
“Certainly not. Why on earth would I?”

She thrust the letter so hard at my chest it nearly poked me. “Take it! I never saw you before in my life. God forbid I ever lay eyes on you again.”
With that, she turned and limped away as fast as her crutch would carry her. I assumed, as anyone would, that the girl was unhinged. I turned to the far more urgent mystery in my hands. The envelope was addressed in Rosanna’s handwriting: *For Franklin Blake, Esq. To be placed directly in his own hands, by Lucy Yolland, no other person to be trusted with it.*

I broke the seal. Inside was a short letter, and a smaller slip of paper tucked beneath it. I read the letter first:
> Sir — If you are curious to understand my strange behaviour towards you during your stay at Lady Verinder’s house, follow every instruction written on the enclosed memorandum — and do it with no other person present to watch you.
> Your humble servant,

I turned to the slip. Every word was underlined at the end, sharp and deliberate:
> 1. Go to the Shivering Sand exactly at the turn of the tide.
> 2. Walk out on the South Spit of rocks until the South Spit Beacon lines up perfectly with the coastguard station flagstaff above Cobb’s Hole.
> 3. Lay a stick or other straight object on the rocks, dead along that aligned line. Make sure one end of the stick sits right at the rock’s edge, overlooking the quicksand.
> 4. Starting from the end of the stick pointing to the beacon, feel through the seaweed along the stick for the hidden metal chain.
> 5. Once you find the chain, follow it until it stretches over the rock’s edge and dangles down into the quicksand. Then pull the chain.

I’d barely finished reading the last underlined line when Betteredge’s voice boomed out behind me. The old steward had finally fully succumbed to his self-diagnosed detective-fever.
“I can’t stand it a second longer, Mr. Franklin! What does that letter say? For heaven’s sake, man, tell me what it says!”

I handed him both documents. He barely glanced at the first letter, but the memorandum made him yell so loud the seagulls took off from the beach.
“Sergeant Cuff said it! From first to last, sir, the Sergeant swore Rosanna wrote down the hiding place of whatever she stashed at the Shivering Sand! And here it is! The secret that stumped every last one of us, even the great Cuff, just waiting for you to dig it up! The tide’s low right now, any fool can see that. How long till it turns?” He craned his neck to yell at a local boy mending a net a short way off. “Tammie Bright! When’s the tide turn?”
“Hour from now!” the boy yelled back.

We checked our watches. “We can hike round the coast path to the quicksand, get there with loads of time to spare,” Betteredge said. “What do you say, sir?”
“Come on.”

On the walk over, Betteredge helped me piece back all the half-forgotten clues from the time of Sergeant Cuff’s investigation: Rosanna sneaking off to Frizinghall when the whole household thought she was bedridden with sickness, locking her bedroom door every night and burning her candle till dawn, buying that japanned tin case and two heavy dog chains from Mrs. Yolland, Cuff’s absolute certainty that Rosanna had hidden something in the Shivering Sand without ever guessing what that something could be. By the time we stepped out onto the South Spit rocks, all those loose ends were tied tight in my head.

We lined up the beacon and the coastguard flagstaff exactly as the memo directed, laid my walking stick along that line, and checked our watches again: twenty minutes still till the tide turned. I suggested we wait on the dry warm beach instead of the slippery wet rocks, but no sooner had I sat down than Betteredge announced he was leaving me.
“What for?” I asked, confused.
“Read the letter again, sir.”
A quick glance reminded me: Rosanna’s explicit order was that I had to make the discovery completely alone.
“Leaving you at a time like this is agony, sir,” Betteredge said, quietly earnest. “But she died a terrible, lonely death, poor soul. I feel I owe it to her to respect that last little fancy of hers. I’ll hang around in the fir plantation up the path, wait till you come get me. Don’t take too long, though. Detective-fever is hell to sit through when you’re stuck waiting for the secret to break.”

The wait felt endless, stretched thin by suspense. I lit a cigar to pass the time. The day was perfect, warm and bright, the air so fresh just breathing felt like a luxury. Even the Shivering Sand looked harmless that morning, its brown surface glinting golden in the sun, hiding the horror of the shifting quicksand beneath a pretty, smiling sheen. Before I’d finished my cigar, I saw it: the faint heave of the sand, that strange, shuddering ripple that crept across its entire surface like some unseen thing deep under the sand was waking up. The tide had turned.

I tossed my cigar and headed back to the rocks. I followed the memo’s instructions, feeling along the stick through the seaweed, and sure enough, half-buried in a crack in the rock, my fingers hit cold metal: the chain. It was tangled in a thick mat of seaweed that I couldn’t pull loose, so I knelt right at the edge of the rock, my face only a few feet above the heaving quicksand. A stupid, superstitious chill ran through me: what if Rosanna’s ghost rose up out of that shivering sand to point the way? I closed my eyes, my blood running cold even in the warm sun, and pushed my stick down into the sand.

I hit metal on the very first try.
I hauled the chain up, and there at the end of it, rusted fast to the links, was the japanned tin case. My hands were shaking so bad I nearly dropped it, but I pried the lid open, and found it full of thick, dry linen, perfectly preserved by the sealed case, plus a crumpled letter addressed to me. I laid the linen roll out on the dry sand and smoothed it flat. It was a nightgown. On the underside, bright and unmistakable, was the smudge of wet paint that had been ruined on Rachel Verinder’s boudoir door the very night the Moonstone vanished.

Sergeant Cuff’s voice rang in my head as clear as if he was standing right beside me: Find the garment with that paint stain on it. Find out who it belongs to. And if that person can’t explain how they were in that room between midnight and three a.m., you don’t have to look far for the hand that stole the Diamond.

I didn’t even need to open the letter in my pocket. I flipped the nightgown over to check the stitched name mark in the collar, the one every household stitched into all its linen. The ink was faded, but the letters were perfectly familiar, stitched right there:

I stared at the letters, unblinking. The sun was still shining. The bay was still glinting. Betteredge was tramping over the sandhills towards me, unable to wait a second longer. I’d left London swearing I’d hunt down the thief who stole the Moonstone, no matter how much time, work, or money it cost. I’d cracked the secret the Shivering Sand had guarded from every other living soul, stumped even the great Sergeant Cuff.
And the irrefutable, paint-stained proof was staring me right in the face. The thief I’d sworn to bring to justice was me.

I will waste no breath describing my own sensations in that first moment after I laid hands on the nightgown.

The shock of what I saw short-circuited every last scrap of thought and feeling in my body. I have no memory at all of how I acted when Betteredge found me on the beach—all I have is his word for it: that I cackled like a fool when he demanded to know what ailed me, pushed the paint-stained linen into his hands, and bade him read the riddle for himself. The stretch of shore between us is a total blank to me now. The next clear memory I can pull back into focus is the path winding through the fir plantation, pine needles crunching soft under our boots, and Betteredge’s gruff, steady voice rumbling that we’ll both be able to face whatever this mess throws at us, the second we’ve downed a glass of good strong grog.

The scene shifts next to Betteredge’s tiny, sun-dappled sitting room, tucked away at the back of the servant’s wing. I’d sworn never to set foot inside Rachel Verinder’s house ever again, and I don’t even remember making the choice to cross the threshold. All I feel is the blessed cool, quiet shade of the small room, and the ice-cold well water he stirs into my spirits, a luxury I’d never have touched at ten o’clock in the morning on any ordinary day. Under other circumstances, that drink would have knocked me flat. Right then, it tightened every frayed nerve in my body, sharpened my wits, just as Betteredge promised it would. We were both ready to face the truth, at last.

I suspect anyone reading this will think my behavior absurd, at best. Stuck in a situation with no parallel I can name, what do I do first? Do I lock myself away from every other living soul? Sit down to pick apart this impossible, undeniable fact staring me straight in the face? Catch the very first train back to London to bring in top detectives and launch a full, urgent inquiry? No. I take shelter under the very roof I swore I’d never disgrace myself by entering again, and I sit tippling spirits and water at the kitchen hour, side by side with an old family steward. What rational person would act that way, caught in my position? I can only plead that the sight of Betteredge’s weathered, familiar face was the only comfort I had left in the world, that his well-mixed grog pulled me back from total collapse when nothing else could. I offer that as my only excuse, and tip my hat to all the perfectly dignified, logically consistent souls who never once stumble in an emergency, from the cradle to the grave.

“Now, Mr. Franklin, there’s one thing we can count on for certain,” Betteredge said, flinging the nightgown down hard on the table between us, pointing at it like it was a living, breathing enemy that could hear every word. “The whole damn thing’s a lie, to start with.”

That comforting take didn’t match the thought that was ringing in my head. “I am as innocent of stealing the Moonstone as you are,” I told him. “But the evidence is staring us right in the face. The paint on the nightgown, my name stitched to the cuff—those are facts.”

Betteredge lifted my glass and pushed it back into my hand, firm but gentle. “Facts?” he repeated. “Take another sip of that grog, sir, and you’ll get over this silly habit of believing in facts. Foul play, that’s what this is,” he went on, dropping his voice to a confidential murmur. “Somebody’s been playing a dirty trick, and you and I are going to find out who. Was there anything else in that tin case, besides the nightgown, when you reached in?”

The question jolted my memory, and I fished the thick, closely written letter out of my coat pocket. I flipped straight to the signature at the end. Rosanna Spearman.

The name lit a spark in my fogged brain, and a sharp new suspicion flared to life. “Wait a second!” I exclaimed. “Rosanna Spearman came to my aunt’s house straight out of a reformatory, didn’t she? She was a convicted thief once, right?”

“No denying that, Mr. Franklin. What of it, now?”

“What of it? How do we know she didn’t steal the Moonstone herself? How do we know she didn’t deliberately smear my nightgown with that paint to frame me for the whole crime?”

Betteredge laid a heavy hand on my arm, stopping me before I could say another word. “You will be cleared of this mess, beyond all shadow of doubt, sir. But I pray you aren’t cleared that way. Read what she wrote. You owe it to the poor girl’s memory to read every line.”

I felt the quiet rebuke in his earnest tone, and it stung. I knew I’d slandered her thoughtlessly, before I even knew what had driven her to hide my nightgown. “You can make up your own mind about her,” I said. “I’ll read the letter out loud.”

I started in, and the very first line of her confession made the letter slip right out of my fingers. “Sir—I have something to own to you. A confession which means much misery, may sometimes be made in very few words. This confession can be made in three words. I love you.”

I stared up at Betteredge, stunned. “What in God’s name does this mean?”

He shifted in his chair, avoiding my eye. “You were alone with Limping Lucy this morning, sir, weren’t you? Didn’t she say a single word about Rosanna Spearman?”

“Not a word. She never even named her.”

“Go back to the letter, then, Mr. Franklin. Plain and simple, I can’t find it in my heart to distress you more than you’ve already been distressed. Let her speak for herself. And for your own sake, man, finish that grog.”

I picked the letter up off the floor and started reading again.

“It would be very disgraceful to me to tell you this, if I was a living woman when you read it. I shall be dead and gone, sir, when you find my letter. That’s what makes me bold. Not even my grave will be left to mark where I was. I can tell you the whole truth, with the quicksand waiting to swallow me the second I set down the last word.

“You’ll find your nightgown in my hiding place, all smeared with that wet paint, and you’ll wonder why I hid it, why I never breathed a word about it while I was alive. I only have one reason for all those strange things. I did them because I loved you.

“I won’t bore you with the whole sad story of my life before you came to Lady Verinder’s house. Lady Verinder pulled me straight out of the reformatory. I got sent to the reformatory after a stretch in prison. I went to prison because I was a thief. I was a thief because my mother had to turn to the streets when I was a tiny little girl, and she had to do that because my father, the gentleman, abandoned her without a penny. It’s a common, tired old story, you read it every other day in the newspapers.

“Lady Verinder was kind to me, and Mr. Betteredge was kind to me. Those two, and the matron at the reformatory, are the only good people I ever met in my whole life. I might have scraped by at that house, not happy, but surviving, if you hadn’t come visiting. I don’t blame you, sir. This is all my fault, every bit of it.

“Do you remember that morning you stumbled out from behind the sandhills, looking for Mr. Betteredge? You looked like a prince right out of a fairy tale. Like the lover every girl dreams of, that no real man ever lives up to. The second I saw you, something that felt like the happy life I’d never once gotten to live jumped right up inside me. Don’t laugh at this, if you can help it. Oh, if you could only understand how deadly serious this is to me!

“I went back to the house, and wrote your name and mine in my workbox, and drew a true lovers’ knot right under them. Then a little voice whispered to me, ‘Go look in the mirror.’ I did, and the sight of myself broke my heart—but I was too stupid to take the warning. I kept falling harder and harder for you, like I was a lady of your own rank, the prettiest creature you’d ever laid eyes on. I tried so hard, every single day, to get you to even look at me. If you knew how I cried myself to sleep every night, miserable and humiliated that you never so much as noticed me, you might have pitied me, and given me a tiny little look I could live on, just to get me through the day.

“I wouldn’t have been half so hurt, maybe, if you knew how much I hated Miss Rachel. I think I figured out you were in love with her before you did, yourself. She used to tuck roses in your buttonhole for you. Ah, Mr. Franklin, you wore my roses in that buttonhole more times than either you or her ever knew! The only little comfort I had back then was slipping my own rose secretly into your glass of water, swapping it for hers, and throwing hers straight in the bin.

“I might have borne it better if she was half as pretty as you all thought she was. No, I expect I’d have hated her worse, still. Imagine stripping off all her fine clothes, putting her in a servant’s dress, and taking all her trinkets away? No one’d call her pretty then. She was too thin, her figure was all wrong. But who knows what mad things men like? And fine young ladies can act as rude and lazy as they please, and never get sent packing, while a servant would get sacked on the spot for half the same behavior. It’s none of my business, I know. I shouldn’t be writing this way, or you’ll never finish reading my letter. But it makes my blood boil to hear everyone call Miss Rachel beautiful, when you know full well it’s all just her fancy gowns and her brash confidence doing all the work.

“Try not to lose patience with me, sir. I’ll get to the part you care about, the part where the Diamond went missing, as fast as I can. But there’s one thing I have to tell you first.

“My life wasn’t half so hard to bear back when I was a thief. It was only after the reformatory matrons taught me to feel my own shame, to try and be a respectable person, that the days got long and heavy and empty. Thoughts of the future started creeping up on me, and I felt every honest person around me judging me, even the kindest ones. That terrible, heart-breaking loneliness stuck to me no matter where I went, who I was with. I knew I should make friends with the other servants, but I couldn’t do it. They all looked at me like they knew what I’d been, like they were watching me to steal something. I don’t regret them making me try to be a better woman—but it was such a tired, lonely life. You came into it like a flash of sunshine at first, and then you forgot all about me, just like everyone else. I was fool enough to fall in love with you, and I couldn’t even get you to notice I existed. That hurt worse than anything.

“Now I’ll tell you the part that matters. Back in those bitter, lonely days, I snuck off two or three times on my afternoons off to my favourite spot—the beach right above the Shivering Sand. I used to say to myself, ‘I think this is where it ends. When I can’t bear it any more, this is where I’ll go.’ That place had a spell over me even before you arrived. I always had a weird feeling something big would happen to me there. But I never looked at that quicksand like it was the place I could go to end all my troubles, hide me forever, until the days I just told you about.

“That’s everything leading up to the morning everyone in the house started yelling that the Moonstone was gone.

“I was so sick of all the silly gossip among the other servants, all of them whispering about who to suspect first, and so mad at you for working so hard to hunt down the Diamond and send for the police, that I stuck to myself as much as I could, until the Frizinghall superintendent, Mr. Seegrave, turned up at the house.

“You remember how he started? He posted a guard on all the women’s bedrooms, and every last one of the maids stormed upstairs after him in a rage, demanding to know what he thought he was doing, insulting them like that. I went with the rest of them, because if I’d stayed back, he’d have marked me as suspicious immediately. We found him in Miss Rachel’s bedroom. He yelled that he didn’t want a crowd of women cluttering the room, pointed at that smudge of wet paint on the door, and said one of our long petticoats must have brushed against it, then sent all of us back downstairs.

“After we left Miss Rachel’s room, I stopped on the landing for a minute by myself, checking to see if any of that paint had gotten on my own gown. Penelope Betteredge was the only servant I was even halfway friendly with, and she walked past and saw me staring down at my skirt.

“‘You don’t need to waste time worrying about that, Rosanna,’ she said. ‘That paint on Miss Rachel’s door has been dry for hours. If Mr. Seegrave wasn’t such an idiot, I’d have told him that already. I’ve never been so insulted in my whole life.’

“Penelope had a hot temper. I calmed her down, and asked her how she could be so sure the paint was dry. ‘I was in that room with Miss Rachel and Mr. Franklin all yesterday morning, mixing their colours while they finished painting the door,’ she said. ‘I heard Miss Rachel ask if the door would be dry that evening, for the birthday party guests to see. Mr. Franklin shook his head and said it wouldn’t be fully dry for twelve whole hours. They didn’t finish until after luncheon, three o’clock in the afternoon. Do the math, Rosanna. That door was totally dry by three o’clock this morning.’

“‘Did any of the ladies go upstairs to look at it last night?’ I asked. ‘I thought I heard Miss Rachel warning everyone not to go near the door.’

“‘None of them made that paint smudge,’ Penelope said. ‘I helped Miss Rachel into bed at twelve o’clock last night, and I glanced at the door then. There wasn’t a mark on it.’

“‘Shouldn’t you tell Mr. Seegrave that?’ I said.

“‘I wouldn’t say a single word to help that fool Seegrave for all the money in the world.’

“She went back to her work, and I went to mine. My job that morning was to make your bed and tidy your room. That was the happiest hour of my whole day. I used to kiss the pillow your head rested on all night. No one’s ever folded your clothes half as neatly as I did, or polished every little knick-knack in your dressing case until there wasn’t a single speck of dust on any of them. You never noticed it, same as you never noticed me. Forgive me, I’m rambling. I’ll get on with it.

“So I went into your room that morning to tidy up, and there was your nightgown tossed across the bed, exactly the way you’d thrown it off when you woke up. I picked it up to fold it—and there it was, the smudge of that very same wet paint on the fabric.

“I was so shocked I ran straight out of the room, nightgown clutched in my hand, locked myself in my own bedroom, and shut the door so no one could interrupt me.

“As soon as I caught my breath, I thought back to what Penelope had just told me. This is proof, I thought, that you were in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room between twelve last night and three o’clock this morning!

“I won’t spell out what the first terrible thought was that popped into my head right then. You’d only get angry, and tear my letter up before you could finish it. Let me just say this: I thought it through as best I could, and I decided it couldn’t be true, because if you’d been in that room at that hour with Miss Rachel’s full knowledge, she’d never have let you walk away with that nightgown, carrying the evidence right off with you. But I still wasn’t 100% sure I was right. You know I hated Miss Rachel a little—there’s no use lying about that now. But I decided to lock that nightgown up in my drawer, wait, watch, and see what I could do with it. Back then, sir, I never even dreamed that you were the one who stole the Moonstone.”

I broke off reading right there, and pushed the letter across the table to Betteredge. I’d read all those earlier parts of her confession about how she’d loved me in total shock, and true, unfeigned distress. I already regretted the cruel, careless accusation I’d flung at her memory before I knew what she’d been through. But now that I’d gotten that far, the bitter resentment was creeping over me, hot and sharp. “You read the rest for yourself,” I told him. “If there’s anything I absolutely have to know, you can fill me in as you go.”

“I understand, Mr. Franklin,” he said, nodding slowly. “It’s only natural for you to feel that way. And Lord help us all, it was just as natural for her to feel the way she did.”

I am copying out the rest of her letter, exactly as it was written, from the original I still hold in my possession:

“Once I’d decided to hide your nightgown and see what use my love, or my strange, twisted bit of payback, could make of it, the first thing I had to work out was how to keep it hidden without anyone finding it on me. The only possible way was to make an exact replica of your nightgown before Saturday rolled around, when the laundress came with her inventory list of every single piece of linen in the house.

“I didn’t dare put the work off until Friday, in case some accident exposed the nightgown in my drawer. I decided to sew the whole new nightgown that very same day, Thursday, so long as I could finagle the time to myself, no one bothering me. First, I locked your real, paint-stained nightgown deep in my drawer, then went back to your bedroom—not because it needed tid

After naming Mr. Candy’s quiet, odd assistant Ezra Jennings, Betteredge seemed to decide they had wasted more than enough time on a trivial side detail. He settled back in his chair, elbows propped on the scarred oak table, and resumed reading the rest of Rosanna Spearman’s unfinished letter.

For my part, I stationed myself at the parlor window, staring out at the drizzling autumn shrubbery as I waited for him to finish. Slowly, the strange, unshakable impression Ezra Jennings had left on me faded entirely—odd, that in the middle of a crisis as overwhelming as mine, any stranger could have lodged in my thoughts at all. My mind snapped back to the hard facts of my impossible situation, and the plan I had finally forced myself to settle on through sheer force of will: catch the London express later that same day, lay every tangled thread of the Moonstone’s disappearance before my lawyer Mr. Bruff, and no matter the cost, no matter what barriers Rachel put up, force a private, uninterrupted interview with her. More than an hour still stretched before the train was due to leave. There was a slim, bare chance the unread rest of Rosanna’s letter held some small clue I could use before I left the very house where the priceless diamond had vanished. For that chance, I waited.

The letter ran on to its close:
> “You have no call to be angry with me, Mr. Franklin, even if I felt a flicker of triumph once, knowing I held every one of your life’s prospects in my two hands. But that triumph didn’t last long. Sergeant Cuff’s theory about the stolen Diamond meant he was bound to end up searching every last scrap of linen and clothing in the house. There wasn’t a corner in my room, there wasn’t a single hiding spot anywhere on the estate, I could be certain he wouldn’t dig through. How to hide the paint-stained nightgown so not even that sharp sergeant could find it? And how to do it without wasting a single precious minute? Those weren’t easy questions to answer. In the end, I landed on a trick you’ll probably laugh at. I stripped off my own clothes, and pulled your nightgown on over my skin. You’d worn it, you see—and for a little while, it felt like you were right there with me.
>
> “I hadn’t hidden it a moment too soon. Word came down from the servants’ hall ten minutes later: Sergeant Cuff wanted to see the house washing-book.
>
> “I fetched it for him, and carried it to my lady’s sitting room. Sergeant Cuff and I had crossed paths more than once years before, back when I was still in trouble. I knew he’d recognize me if he looked close enough—and I had no idea what he’d do when he found an ex-con servant working in a house where a royal jewel had gone missing. I just wanted the meeting over with, to get the worst of it out in the open.
>
> “When I handed him the book, he looked at me like I was a total stranger, and thanked me so politely it made my skin crawl. Those were bad signs, I knew. There was no telling what he’d say about me behind my back, no telling how soon he’d have me dragged off to the local lockup and strip-searched. That was right when you got back from seeing Godfrey Ablewhite off at the railway station. I snuck off to your favorite walk in the shrubbery, determined to catch you and speak—last chance I might ever get, for all I knew.
>
> “You never showed up. Worse, Mr. Betteredge and Sergeant Cuff walked right past the bush I was hiding behind, and the sergeant saw me clear as day.
>
> “I had no choice but to creep back to my work before something even worse went wrong. Just as I stepped out onto the path, you came back from the station. You were heading straight for the shrubbery, you definitely saw me—and you turned away like I had the plague, and walked right back into the house.
>
> * [Note in Franklin Blake’s hand: The poor girl was completely mistaken. I never noticed her at all. I had meant to take a turn in the shrubbery, but I remembered at that exact second my aunt would want to speak to me after I got back from the station, so I changed course and went indoors.]
>
> “I hurried back through the servants’ entrance. The laundry room was empty, so I sat down there all by myself. You know how the Shivering Sand at the coast gets in your head, whispering? Those old thoughts came back to me then. I wondered which was harder to bear: the way you didn’t even see me, or to jump straight into that quicksand and end all of it for good.
>
> “Don’t ask me to make sense of my own actions back then. I’ve tried, and I can’t.
>
> “Why didn’t I grab you when you walked past me like I didn’t exist? Why didn’t I yell out, ‘Mr. Franklin, I have something to tell you that affects you directly, and you will listen to me’? You were at my mercy, everyone says I had the whip hand. And better than that, I could have helped you. I never thought for a second that a gentleman like you would steal the Diamond just for the fun of it. No, Penelope heard Miss Rachel talk about your debts, Mr. Betteredge gossiped about how much you spent. It was obvious you took it to sell or pawn, to get the money you needed. I even knew a man in London who’d give you a huge sum for it, no awkward questions asked.
>
> “Why didn’t I speak? Why?
>
> “I was a thief once, remember. I’d run risks ten times worse than hiding that nightgown. I’d pulled off scams so big they got written up in the newspapers. This little trouble wasn’t anything I couldn’t handle. But the truth is plain, isn’t it? I loved you, heart and soul, behind your back. But standing in front of you? I was terrified. Scared you’d get angry at me. Scared of what you’d say, even after I found out your secret, if a plain crooked-shouldered housemaid dared to talk to you like an equal. I got as close as I dared when I spoke to you in the library, and you didn’t turn your back on me then. But when you walked past me like I was dirt? I couldn’t work up any anger to make me brave. I just felt the shame of it, like you were saying, ‘You’re nothing to me, how dare you speak up?’ You never said it out loud, but I heard it anyway. Madness, isn’t it? No explaining that.
>
> “I’m almost at the end now. Penelope found me sitting in the empty laundry room. She’d guessed my secret ages ago, and tried to talk me out of my stupid crush, kind as can be.
>
> “‘Ah, I know why you’re moping here,’ she said. ‘The best thing for you, Rosanna, is when Mr. Franklin’s visit ends. He’ll be leaving soon, mark my words.’
>
> “I’d never once thought of you leaving. I couldn’t speak, I just stared at her.
>
> “‘I just came from Miss Rachel,’ Penelope went on. ‘She’s furious the police are crawling all over the house. She says she’s going to tell my lady tonight she’s leaving for her Aunt Ablewhite’s place tomorrow. And if she goes, Mr. Franklin will find a reason to leave right after, you can be sure of it.’
>
> “I found my voice then. ‘Do you mean he’ll go with her?’ I asked.
>
> “‘He’d go gladly enough if she’d let him, but she won’t. She’s mad at him, won’t speak a word to him, even after he tried so hard to help her. No, if they don’t make up tomorrow, Miss Rachel will go one way, Mr. Franklin will go the other. I don’t know where he’ll head to, but he won’t stay here after she leaves.’
>
> “I choked down the despair that came over me. For a second, I even had a little flicker of hope, if Rachel was quarreling with you. ‘Do you know what they’re fighting about?’ I asked.
>
> “‘It’s all Miss Rachel’s temper,’ Penelope said. ‘And don’t go thinking Mr. Franklin will ever fight back with her. He’s far too fond of her for that.’
>
> “The words were no sooner out of her mouth than Betteredge called all the indoor servants to assemble in the hall, to go in one by one and be questioned by Sergeant Cuff.
>
> “My turn came after my lady’s maid and the top housemaid. Sergeant Cuff’s questions, tricky as they were, made it plain those two women—my worst enemies in the house—had ratted on me, told him they’d seen me creeping outside my room late on Tuesday, then again on Thursday night. He figured out I’d sewn a new nightgown in secret, but he got it all wrong: he thought the paint-stained nightgown was mine. And I saw something else, from the way he talked. He knew I was mixed up in the Diamond’s disappearance, but he didn’t think I was the main person responsible. He thought I was taking orders from someone else. I still don’t know who he thought that someone was.
>
> “But it was clear he was nowhere near the whole truth. You were safe as long as that nightgown stayed hidden, and not a second longer.
>
> “I couldn’t risk wearing it under my clothes any more. They could drag me off to the Frizinghall police station any minute, charge me with suspicion, and strip search me. As long as Sergeant Cuff was still letting me walk free, I had to choose right then: burn the nightgown, or hide it somewhere far away from the house, somewhere no one would ever find it.
>
> “If I’d loved you just a little less, I would have burned it. But how could I? It was the only proof I had that I’d saved you from being arrested, from having your whole life ruined. If I ever did talk to you, and you didn’t believe me, if you thought I was making it all up for some bad reason, what else could I use to make you trust me? It was the only claim I had to your gratitude, your confidence. I couldn’t bring myself to destroy it.
>
> “I decided to hide it at the place I knew better than anyone: the Shivering Sand.
>
> “As soon as the questioning was over, I made up some excuse about needing fresh air, and got leave to go out. I walked straight to Cobb’s Hole, to the Yollands’ cottage. Mrs. Yolland and her daughter Lucy are the only real friends I ever had. I didn’t tell them your secret, I’ve told no one. I just needed a place to write this letter, and a private spot to take the nightgown off me. I couldn’t do either safely back at the house, not when every servant was watching me.
>
> “I’m writing the last of this in Lucy’s bedroom, all by myself. When I finish, I’ll wrap the nightgown up tight, hide it under my cloak, and find something in Mrs. Yolland’s old junk drawer to wrap it in so it stays perfectly dry. Then I’ll walk to the Shivering Sand—don’t worry, I’ll make sure no footprints lead to the hiding spot—and bury it deep in the sand, where no one will ever find it unless I tell them exactly where to look.
>
> “What happens after that?
>
> “I’ll have one last chance to say all the things I haven’t been able to say to you. If you leave tomorrow like Penelope says, and I haven’t spoken to you before that, I’ll lose my chance forever. That’s the first reason. And the second: if I do speak and you get angry at me, I’ve got that nightgown hidden, ready to prove I’m not lying. If those two things together aren’t enough to make me brave enough to get past that fear of your coldness, then that’s the end of my efforts. And the end of my life.
>
> “Yes. If you turn me away again, the way you did before? Goodbye to a world that never gave me a scrap of the happiness everyone else gets. Goodbye to life, that nothing but a little kindness from you could ever make worth living. Don’t blame yourself if it ends that way. But try, do try, to feel a little sorry for me. I’ll make sure you find out what I did for you, once I’m past telling you. Will you say one kind thing about me, the same soft way you talk to Miss Rachel? If there are ghosts, I’ll hear you. I think I’ll tremble with happiness.
>
> “I have to stop now. I’m crying so hard I can barely see the paper. How will I find my way to the hiding spot if I let tears blind me?
>
> “Why look on the dark side, anyway? Maybe it will all turn out fine. Maybe I’ll find you in a good mood tonight, or tomorrow morning. Fretting won’t make my plain face any prettier, will it? Who knows, maybe all these pages are for nothing. I’ll tuck them in the hiding spot right alongside the nightgown, to keep them safe. Writing this was hard work. Oh, if we only end up understanding each other, how happy I’ll be to tear all of this up!
>
> “Your true lover and humble servant,

Betteredge finished reading in total silence. He folded the letter carefully back into its envelope, set it down on the table, and sat hunched over, staring at the floor, heavy with grief.
“Betteredge,” I said, “is there any clue at the end that I missed?”
He lifted his head slowly, sighing so deep his shoulders shook.
“Nothing to guide you, Mr. Franklin. Take my advice, and keep that letter in its envelope until all your troubles are over. Reading it when you’re already at the end of your tether will only break your heart. Don’t read it again now.”

I slipped the letter into my pocketbook. Looking back at Betteredge’s old narrative, I knew exactly why I couldn’t bear to re-read it right then. Twice, Rosanna had made her final desperate attempts to speak to me, and twice, all unknowing, I had cruelly rebuffed her. That Friday night, I’d found her hovering by the billiard table, and I’d assumed she was about to confess her own part in stealing the Diamond—so for her sake, to make it easier for her to come clean, I’d stared hard at the billiard balls and ignored her, wounding her so deeply she’d walked away crushed. Then the next day, the very day she knew I was about to leave, I’d run into her in the shrubbery with Sergeant Cuff. Cuff had made a point of asking me if I had any interest in Rosanna, and I’d loudly, flatly denied it, for her own sake to warn her the police were closing in. I’d thought I was protecting her. Instead, those words had been the final push that sent her straight to the Shivering Sand, to her death.

I set the ghost of that terrible, sad story aside, even though the memory of it still twisted in my chest. The tragedy of Rosanna Spearman, the suicide at the quicksand that had set every last strange piece of the Moonstone puzzle into place, was behind me now. It was time to turn my full attention to the living, to the long, slow, hard work of untangling the last of the secrets, and dragging the truth out of the dark that had wrapped itself around all of us for so long.

Gabriel Betteredge trundled beside me all the way to the railway station, silent for the first time in all the years I’d known him, the weight of Rosanna Spearman’s final letter hanging heavy between us. Tucked in my pocket was that very letter, and stuffed in a small sealed satchel was the nightgown smeared with Rachel’s birthday yellow paint — both destined for Mr. Bruff’s desk before I slept that night.

We didn’t speak a word until we’d cleared the lodge gates. “Before I catch the London train,” I said, breaking the hush, “I’ve got two questions to ask you. They’re about me, and they’ll probably shock you.”
“Shock me all you like, sir,” Betteredge groaned. “Anything that’ll get that poor girl’s letter out of my head for five minutes. Go on.”
“First question: was I drunk the night of Rachel’s birthday?”
The old steward nearly laughed out loud. “*You* drunk? The great flaw in your character, Mr. Franklin, is that you never touch a drop of liquor after dinner. But now you mention it — you did break habit that night, when you looked so peaked and ill we talked you into a little brandy and water to cheer you up.”
“I never drink brandy. It’s entirely possible —”
“Hold on a second. I poured you half a wineglass of our 50-year-old Cognac, and drowned the whole noble lot in nearly a full tumbler of cold water. A toddler couldn’t have got tipsy on it, never mind a grown man.”
I knew his memory for small household details was flawless. Intoxication was impossible. I moved on to the second question.
“Before I was sent abroad, you saw me every day as a boy. Did you ever catch me sleepwalking?”
Betteredge stopped dead, stared at me for a long beat, then nodded and kept walking. “I see your drift now, sir. You’re trying to explain how you got that paint on your nightgown without remembering it. Won’t work. You never sleepwalked once in your life. Hundreds of people would have spotted the habit if you’d had it, at home or on your travels, they’d have warned you, taken precautions. You’d know.”
He could only be right. But I clung stubbornly to the only two explanations that could get me out of the monstrous position I was trapped in — until Betteredge scattered both theories to the wind before we even reached the platform.
“Let’s test your logic,” he said, shrewd as ever. “Say you were blackout drunk, or sleepwalking, when you touched that paint and took the Moonstone. That explains the birthday night and the morning after. But how do you explain the Diamond turning up in London, pledged to Mr. Luker weeks later? Did you do all that in a blackout too? Was I seeing you off in the pony-chaise on that Saturday evening while you were sleepwalking? Did you trudge all the way to Luker’s pawnshop half-asleep after your train pulled in? Forgive me for saying it, Mr. Franklin, but this mess has addled your wits. The sooner you put your head next to Mr. Bruff’s, the sooner you’ll see your way out of this dead end.”
We reached the station with barely two minutes to spare. I scribbled my London address down for Betteredge, told him to write at once if anything new turned up, and promised I’d send news back the second I had it. Glancing over at the book stall as I turned to climb into my carriage, I spotted Mr. Candy’s strange, sharp-eyed assistant — Ezra Jennings, the man with the streaked piebald hair — deep in conversation with the stall keeper. Our eyes met, he tipped his hat politely, and I nodded back before jumping aboard as the train’s whistle blew. Absurd as it was, I found myself dwelling on the odd coincidence that I’d run into the strange man twice in the same single day, as the train pulled out for London.

I arrived long after Bruff’s city law office had closed, so I drove straight to his private Hampstead residence, and disturbed the old lawyer dozing alone in his dining room, his favourite pug curled on his lap, a decanter of his best port within easy reach.
He heard my full story without a single interruption, then immediately ordered extra lights and strong tea sent up to his study, and told his wife and daughters under no circumstances to disturb us for any reason at all. When the door was locked behind us, he first laid the nightgown out flat on his desk to inspect the paint stain, then read every line of Rosanna Spearman’s letter slowly and carefully.
Only when he’d turned the last page did he speak.
“Franklin Blake,” he said, “this is far more serious than you’ve yet realised. It concerns Rachel just as much as it concerns you. Her strange, cold silence all these weeks? It’s no mystery now. She believes you stole the Moonstone.”
I’d shrunk from spelling that conclusion out to myself, but it had hung over my head for days, nonetheless. That was exactly why I was determined to face her in person.
“Our first step,” Bruff went on, “is to break through that silence of hers. She’s kept quiet this long out of that stubborn, proud, private nature I know so well. We can’t let her stay quiet any longer. We have to make her tell us exactly what evidence she’s relying on to be so sure you took the Diamond. Nine times out of ten, this whole mess will fall apart the second she lets her reserve crack and speaks out.”
“That’s a comforting thought,” I said. “But I don’t see how you can be so sure.”
“Simple. I’m looking at this as a lawyer, not a friend. It’s a question of evidence. Right now, the whole case against you collapses at the very first hurdle. I grant the name stitched in the nightgown proves it’s yours. I grant the paint stain proves that nightgown left the smudge on Rachel’s door. But what single scrap of evidence is there to prove *you* were the person wearing it the night the Moonstone went missing?”
The objection hit me like a blow — I’d felt it lurking at the back of my mind for days, but never dared name it.
“I can see why Rosanna’s letter is so distressing for you to analyse fairly,” Bruff went on, picking up the pages. “But I have no personal stake here. I can read it like any other legal document. Even setting aside her history as a convicted thief, she admits straight out that she was an expert at deception. She writes that she was madly jealous of Rachel, that she deliberately swapped the roses in your buttonhole at the birthday dinner, that she saw a perfect chance for herself if you and Rachel fell out permanently. The odds are 99 to 100 that she was the one who showed that nightgown to Rachel, all to drive a wedge between the two of you. She wouldn’t have hesitated to steal the Moonstone herself if it meant ruining your happiness. She didn’t even kill herself then, remember — she was still planning to win you over. That was entirely in her character.”
“I thought of that the second I opened her letter,” I said. “But I felt so sorry for the poor dead girl, I couldn’t bring myself to say it out loud.”
“Does you nothing but credit, my boy. But let’s test the other scenario — say we *do* find proof you wore that nightgown that night. Clearing your name will be a nightmare. So let’s not borrow that trouble today. Let’s find out first if Rachel is only judging you on that nightgown, no other evidence.”
“Good God,” I burst out, “what right has she to suspect me of being a thief on any evidence at all?”
“A fair question, hot as you phrased it. It puzzles me too. Think — did something happen while you were staying at the house, not enough to make her doubt your honour, but just enough to plant a tiny, unreasonable seed of doubt about your judgement?”
I shot to my feet, shaken. I’d completely forgotten the incident.
A couple of months before the birthday, a hot-tempered French lawyer had tracked me down to Lady Verinder’s house, sent by a Paris restaurant keeper I’d borrowed a small sum from, after my bill for the debt had bounced. The two of us had yelled at each other right outside the drawing room, and Rachel and her mother had heard every word. Lady Verinder had paid the man off to send him packing, but Rachel had flown into a rage at my reckless carelessness, called me unprincipled, dishonourable, said there was no telling what stupid, selfish thing I might do next. We’d made up two days later, and I’d never thought of it again.
Bruff nodded gravely the second I finished telling him the story. “That’s exactly it. That old memory was sitting there, waiting, the second she saw that nightgown. It’s a predisposition against you we didn’t know about, and that’s one mystery solved. Now our only next move is to go talk to Rachel.”
“The grand difficulty,” he said, pacing the study, “is getting her to open up completely. Do you have any ideas?”
“I’ve decided to speak to her myself.”
Bruff stopped dead, stared at me like I’d lost my mind. “You? The very last person on earth she’d speak openly to about this?” He paced a couple more turns, then grinned suddenly. “Wait. In cases this strange, the rash way is often the best. Nothing venture, nothing win. You have one advantage I will never have. She’s still got that soft, stubborn little spot in her heart for you. Tap that, and she’ll tell you every single thing she’s been holding back. I’ve got a plan. My wife and daughters will invite her over to spend the day tomorrow — no mention of you, no hint of the trap. You sneak in through the back garden gate at three o’clock, go through the conservatory, cross the small drawing room, and the first door on the other side leads to the music room. She’ll be there all alone.”
He handed me the key to the garden gate the next morning, turning up at my lodgings while I was still at breakfast, looking faintly guilty. Rachel was coming, he said, his wife and daughters were fully in on the scheme and thought it was a brilliant idea, no qualms at all about tricking their guest for a good cause.
The letter from Betteredge waiting in my post bag that day was mostly just the old steward writing for the pleasure of talking to me, but he added a small note: Ezra Jennings had stopped him at the station the day he saw me, asked who I was, and Mr. Candy had been furious he’d missed the chance to speak to me, begging me to call on him next time I was anywhere near Frizinghall. I barely read the words. All I could think of was the coming meeting.
At three sharp, I turned the key in Bruff’s back garden gate, slipped inside, and locked it behind me. The paths were empty, no one but the birds and the bees around to see me. I walked through the conservatory, crossed the small drawing room, and paused with my hand on the music room door. Soft, plaintive piano chords drifted out from the other side, the same idle little melody Rachel used to play when we lazed around her mother’s drawing room, no troubles between us at all. The contrast between that old happy memory and the terrible secret hanging over us shook me so badly I had to stop a full minute to steady my breathing.
Then I drew myself up, turned the doorknob, and stepped inside.

Late that evening, my lodgings were unexpectedly graced by a visit from Mr. Bruff.

The sharp, unshakable confidence that usually defined the lawyer was entirely gone. For the first time in all the years I had known him, he shook my hand in heavy, exhausted silence.

“Have you come from Hampstead?” I asked, scrambling to break the weight of the unspoken tension hanging between us.

“I left there not half an hour ago,” he answered. “I know, Mr. Franklin, that you have finally uncovered the full truth of what Rachel saw that night. But I will say plainly: if I could have foreseen the cost of that knowledge, I would have left both of you in the dark forever.”

“You have spoken to Rachel, then?”

“I escorted her all the way back to her home in Portland Place; there was no question of letting her travel alone after what you two went through. I don’t hold you to blame for the shock of that unfortunate meeting—you saw her in my house, under my permission, after all. But I will not risk a repeat of that harm. She is young, stubborn, and strong-willed; with rest and time, she will heal. All I ask is your word that you will make no attempt to see her again, not until I give you explicit permission to do so. Can I depend on that?”

“After all the pain she has borne, and all I have suffered, you have my promise,” I said.

He visibly relaxed at that, set his hat down on the table beside him, and pulled his chair closer to mine.

“Good, that is settled. Now for the future—your future. The extraordinary turn this case has taken leaves us with two unassailable facts. First: Rachel told you the full, unvarnished truth of what she saw, plain as words can make it. Second: even though we both know some unthinkable, hidden mistake must be at the root of it, you cannot blame her for believing you guilty. She saw you take the Diamond with her own two eyes, and every circumstantial detail that followed seemed to confirm it beyond any possible doubt.”

“I do not blame her,” I cut in. “I only regret that she could not bring herself to speak plainly to me of it at the time.”

“You might as well wish Rachel was a completely different person,” Bruff laughed shortly. “No girl of her delicacy, who was already set on marrying you, could ever bring herself to call you a thief to your face. That is not her nature, and I know for a fact she has acted from that exact same instinct before, in a different situation that left her feeling just as trapped. Besides, she told me on the drive back to town tonight: even if she had spoken up then, she would no more have believed your denial than she believes it now. There is no answer to that, is there? Do not waste your time, do not waste your energy, trying to unpick every tangled thread of that night last year at Lady Verinder’s country house. We will get nowhere digging in the past we cannot fully unpack. Look to the future, instead, to what we *can* find.”

“Surely you see,” I objected, “that for me, the entire mystery is rooted firmly in the past?”

“Answer me this,” Bruff pressed, “is the Moonstone the source of all this harm, or is it not?”

“Of course it is.”

“Very well. We both know the Diamond was taken to London and pledged to Mr. Luker. We know you were not the man who pawned it—but do we know who was?”

“No.”

“We know the Moonstone is sitting in Luker’s bankers’ vault at this very moment. It is June now. By the end of this month, almost exactly a full year will have passed since that pledge was made. The man who pawned it will almost certainly come forward to redeem it the second that year is up. By the terms of Luker’s own agreement, he will have to pull the Diamond out of the bank himself to hand it over. All we have to do is post a watch outside the bank as the month draws to a close, and we will see exactly who he is. Do you see it now?”

I admitted, a little reluctantly, that the plan was at least original, if nothing else.

“It is as much Mr. Murthwaite’s idea as mine,” Bruff said. “He mentioned it to me in a conversation some weeks back. If he is right, the three Indian jugglers will be staking out that same bank at the end of the month too, and there may be serious trouble come of it. But whatever happens, that unknown man who pawned the Diamond is the one who framed you. He is the only person alive who can clear your name in Rachel’s eyes.”

“I will not deny your plan is daring, ingenious, and new,” I said. “But I have one objection. It demands that I wait.”

“All of a fortnight, more or less,” Bruff said. “Is that such an unbearable stretch of time?”

“To me, in this state, it feels like a lifetime. I cannot endure sitting idle, not when my name is tainted and I have no way to prove myself innocent. I have already thought of what I can do: I am going to consult Sergeant Cuff.”

“Cuff retired from the force years ago. You cannot expect him to take on the case now.”

“I know exactly where to find him. I can at least try.”

“Try, then,” Bruff said after a moment’s thought. “This case has grown so far beyond anything he could have imagined back when he was first investigating it, you might well reawaken his old professional interest. Come back to me with what he says. In the meanwhile, if you have not found any answers by the end of the month, you will not object to me posting that watch at the bank?”

“Of course not,” I said. “Unless I find the full truth long before that, and make the whole scheme unnecessary.”

Bruff smiled, picked up his hat to leave. “Tell Cuff that I say the whole truth of this case hinges entirely on finding the man who pawned the Moonstone. I want to hear what his old detective experience makes of that.”

We parted shortly after.

The next morning at dawn, I set out for the small Surrey town of Dorking, where Betteredge had told me Sergeant Cuff had settled into retirement.

After asking directions at the local inn, I found his cottage tucked down a quiet side lane, surrounded by a large walled garden, fronted by a tall hawthorn hedge. The gate, topped with neatly painted trellis, was locked. When I peered through the slats after ringing the bell, roses were everywhere: blooming in borders, climbing the cottage doorframe, peeking through every window. Far from the city’s dark alleys and unsolved crimes, the legendary thief-taker was living out his days in peaceful, rose-scented seclusion.

An elderly housekeeper opened the gate, and immediately dashed every last hope I had of securing Cuff’s help. He had left the day before, travelling to Ireland.

“On detective business?” I asked, bewildered.

The woman smiled. “Oh, he has no other business these days but roses, sir. A head gardener for a great Irish house found a new, secret trick for growing prize roses, and Mr. Cuff went all the way over to learn it.”

“Do you know when he will be back?”

“There is no telling, sir. He said he would come straight home if the new trick was worthless, or stay as long as it took to master it if it was worth his time. If you leave a message, I will make sure he gets it.”

I scribbled a note on my card, telling him I had a matter concerning the Moonstone to discuss, and left it with her. There was nothing for it but to turn back to London, empty-handed.

My frayed temper only grew worse after that wasted journey. I could not sit still, could not rest, until I had taken some new step toward cracking the mystery. That night, I sat up for hours smoking, spinning wild, improbable theories rooted in the abstract German philosophy I had studied in my youth, until I was so tangled in metaphysics I could barely convince myself the Moonstone was even a real, physical object.
