Accident rescued me from that fog the next morning. I pulled on the same coat I had worn for my disastrous meeting with Rachel, and rummaging in its pockets for a handkerchief, I found a crumpled, forgotten letter from Betteredge. I sat down at my desk to read it again, and realized I owed the old man a reply. His note was trivial, mostly: Ezra Jennings, Mr. Candy’s strange piebald-haired assistant, had told Candy he had seen me, and the doctor wanted a word with me the next time I was anywhere near Frizinghall. I found myself idly sketching portraits of Jennings on the blank page of paper I meant to use for my reply, until the plain act of writing a simple, ordinary letter to Betteredge cleared all that foggy metaphysical nonsense right out of my head.

With my mind clear at last, I decided to approach the mystery practically. I could not recall a single solid detail of the birthday dinner held the night the Moonstone vanished. I could not even remember how many guests had been there. That gap in my memory, I realized, was the exact place the clue I had been hunting for was hidden. I would get the full list of everyone who had attended that dinner, write to every single one of them, and ask them to recount every tiny, trivial thing they could remember about that evening. The small, forgotten detail no one had thought worth mentioning before would be the key that unlocked everything.

I was determined to head back to Yorkshire that very day to see Betteredge and get that guest list, but I missed the midday train by minutes, leaving me with three hours to kill before the next one departed. I realized three of the birthday guests were in London: Mr. Murthwaite, Godfrey Ablewhite, and Miss Clack. I drove straight to Bruff’s office to ask for their addresses, but he was far too busy to do more than dash my hopes. Murthwaite had already sailed back to the Middle East for his next expedition. Miss Clack had lost most of her money and moved to France to live cheaply. Godfrey Ablewhite? Bruff said I could try looking for him at his club.

At the club, a friend of Godfrey’s gave me his address, and two pieces of news I had never heard before: Rachel had turned down Godfrey’s proposal, but he had very quickly secured another engagement to a wealthy heiress, only for that arrangement to collapse suddenly over a dispute about marriage settlements. Worse, he had just inherited five thousand pounds from a rich old charity patron, and had left for the Continent the day before, bound for Ostend and Brussels, planning to be gone for a full three months.

My luck was rotten. Every promising lead was out of reach. I boarded the train north for Frizinghall at last, and arrived late that evening. The next morning I sent a carriage to fetch Betteredge from the old Verinder house, and while I waited for him, I remembered Mr. Candy had asked to see me. The doctor lived only a street away, so I went to call on him first.

I knew from what Betteredge had told me that Candy had never fully recovered from the terrible fever he’d caught on the night of the birthday dinner. But I was not prepared for the wreck of a man who greeted me. His eyes were dim, his hair had turned completely white, his face was gaunt and shriveled, his frame shrunk to half its former size. Only his gaudy, brightly coloured clothes and cheap jewellery remained the same, a cruel mockery of the lively, jokey, prank-loving little doctor I had once known.

“Mr. Blake, I have thought of you often,” he said, fidgeting restlessly. “I am so glad to see you again. Whatever you need, whatever I can do to help, you have only to ask.”

He could not hide his fidgety, childishly curious eagerness to know why I had come. I launched into the explanation I had rehearsed on the train: that we hoped to recover the lost Moonstone, and I was collecting every memory of the birthday night from everyone who had been present at the dinner. The words barely registered. He picked at his fingertips the whole time I spoke, his watery eyes blank and unfocused, obviously not hearing a single word I said after the first few. I switched subjects immediately.

“So that is why I have come to Frizinghall,” I said, cheerfully. “Now it is your turn. You sent me a message by Gabriel Betteredge, some time back——”

He dropped his fingers from his lap, and suddenly, all at once, brightened right up.

The doctor’s pretty young housemaid stood holding the street door open for me, bright morning sunlight flooding straight down the hall to catch Ezra Jennings full in the face the second I turned back to glance at him.

Betteredge had been entirely correct in his assessment: to any casual onlooker, the man’s appearance worked violently against him. His swarthy gipsy complexion, sunken fleshless cheeks, sharp gaunt facial bones, far-off dreamy eyes, and that uncanny streaked two-toned hair blended into a figure that defied age entirely—no one could say if he was twenty or fifty, and every odd quirk of him stacked up to put strangers instinctively on guard. But for all that, I could not shake a strange, unaccountable pull of sympathy that rooted me to the spot. My better sense, honed by years of worldly caution, told me to nod politely, confirm Mr. Candy looked shockingly unwell, and proceed on my way to my aunt Mrs. Ablewhite’s. But my fascination for this strange, lonely man held me fast, and I knew he had been waiting precisely for this chance to speak to me in private, away from the prying eyes of the household.

“You’re heading out too, Mr. Jennings?” I said, noticing he already had his hat in his hand. “I’m bound for my aunt’s place, not far from here.”
He replied that he had a patient to visit, and his route ran the exact same way as mine.

We stepped out together. The same pretty housemaid who had greeted me with bright grins and easy amiability when I bade her good morning pursed her lips tight the second Jennings tried to pass on a quiet, unassuming message about what time he would be back, and deliberately stared at the wall rather than meet his eye. The man was no favourite even under his own employer’s roof. Out on the steps, I remembered Betteredge had said no one anywhere in Frizinghall cared for him. What a miserable, friendless life he must lead, I thought.

Jennings had already broached Mr. Candy’s illness earlier, and now he held his tongue, clearly signalling that it was my turn to bring the subject up. I had my own urgent reasons to dig into the details of the doctor’s condition, so I spoke first.
“From how altered he looks today, his illness must have been far more serious than anyone let on?”
“It is nothing short of a miracle he survived,” Jennings replied.
“Is his memory never any clearer than it was when I saw him just now? He kept trying to speak to me about something before he fell sick—”
“Something that happened the night before he took ill?” Jennings cut in, reading my hesitation perfectly.
“Exactly.”
“His memory of that period is all but destroyed,” he said, his tone softening. “It would be kinder for him if he had lost all recollection entirely, instead of clinging to that half-echo of plans he made, things he swore he had to say or do. He cannot for the life of him name what those things were, and he’s tortured by the gap, constantly fighting to hide how broken his memory has become. Perhaps we would all be happier, if we could wipe the worst parts of our past clean away entirely.” He added that last line with a thin, sad smile.
“Surely there are memories no man would ever want to lose entirely?” I pushed back.
“That may be true for most people, Mr. Blake. It is not true for all.” His dark eyes locked on mine. “Do I gather that the lost memory Mr. Candy was fumbling to recover when you visited him this morning matters a great deal to you?”

He had landed right on the exact question I had been hoping to bring up, and every word he had spoken so far convinced me he was a man of fine, quiet breeding, the kind of unforced, unassuming self-possession no amount of mimicry can fake. I had no qualms about speaking frankly.
“I have a very great stake in recovering that lost memory. Can you suggest any way to jog his recollection?”
A sharp, bright spark of interest lit up his otherwise dreamy brown eyes.
“I’ve tried dozens of times already. His own memory is entirely beyond saving,” he said.
That disappointed me, and I did not hide it. “I was hoping for a far more hopeful answer than that.”
“Wait,” he smiled. “It may not be the final answer. We do not need Mr. Candy to remember at all. We can reconstruct that lost event without ever asking him to recall a single thing.”
I leaned forward, eager. “How?”
“I will spare you none of the messy professional details of his illness, if you are willing to hear them out.”
I assured him I was all attention. By that point we had left the last houses of the small town behind us. Jennings paused to pluck a small bunch of wild flowers from the hedgerow, holding them up gently. “They are so beautiful. Almost no one in England ever stops to notice them the way they deserve.”
“You did not grow up in England, then?” I asked.
“No. I was born in one of the colonies, raised there for half my childhood. My father was English, but my mother—” he cut himself off, shaking his head. “My apologies, we are straying from the subject. Those little flowers hold old, painful memories for me. Let us get back to Mr. Candy.” The small slip of his life story, paired with that bleak view of forgetting the past to be happy, confirmed what his face had already told me: he had suffered more than most men ever do, and there was foreign, non-English blood in his veins.

“You have heard, I assume, how he fell ill in the first place?” Jennings resumed. “The night of Lady Verinder’s dinner party, it poured without stopping. He drove home in his gig soaked to the skin, and found an urgent message waiting for him the second he stepped in the door. He foolishly drove straight out to see that patient without ever stopping to change his wet clothes. I was tied up with a serious case miles outside Frizinghall that same night, and when I got back at dawn, his groom was waiting for me in a total panic, to drag me up to his sickroom. The fever had already taken hold. It was a strange, undefined fever, no distinct diagnosis, and the two senior local doctors I called in immediately disagreed violently with me on treatment. They said his rapid pulse proved he needed a lowering diet of gruel, lemonade, barley water. I agreed his pulse raced—but I also saw it was shockingly weak, a sign his whole system was crashing, that he needed heavy doses of stimulants: champagne, brandy, ammonia, quinine. They threatened to walk out if I ignored their advice. I sent a man down to the cellar for a bottle of champagne, poured half a glass down Mr. Candy’s throat myself, and the two men left in a huff. I owed that man my life—he was the only person in the world who had ever been kind to me. I sat by his bedside for twenty hours out of every day, fighting Death through those terrible small hours between two and five a.m., when the life force of even the healthiest man is at its lowest. When wine stopped working, I used brandy. When that faded, I doubled the dose. After what felt like an eternity of waiting, his pulse slowed just a little, then steadied, then grew strong. I knew I had saved him, and I broke down crying, right there. The medical books are right: some men are born with nervous, sensitive constitutions. I am one of them.”

He brushed past his own display of emotion like it was a triviality, and went on. “I have spent years writing an unpublished medical book, about delirium and the workings of the nervous system, something no one else will ever read. It got me through all those long, silent hours at Mr. Candy’s bedside. I have long suspected that even when a delirious man cannot speak a single coherent sentence, his mind may still be thinking in perfectly logical, connected lines. I know shorthand. I wrote down every single disjointed word that fell from Mr. Candy’s lips while he was raving. Later, I filled in the gaps between those broken fragments like assembling a jigsaw puzzle, until I had stitched all his rambling lines together into a single coherent, intelligible narrative. Your name comes up in one of those long, delirium sessions. For an entire night, his mind was fixed entirely on something that happened between you and him, the night of Lady Verinder’s dinner party—something he had fully intended to tell you, before his sickness hit and wiped the memory clean.”

“Then it is exactly the lost memory he was trying to reach!” I burst out. “Let us turn back right now and look at those notes.”
“Absolutely impossible,” Jennings said, steady.
“Why?”
“Would you hand over the private, unguarded ramblings of your most helpless sick friend to a stranger, before you were certain there was a very urgent, justified reason to do so?”
I had no good argument against that, and fumbled to offer only vague, half-hearted excuses about a personal matter that did not involve the Moonstone.
Jennings shook his head. “I can promise you, not one single word Mr. Candy raved about that night has anything to do with the Diamond. No mention of the theft, no mention of the Moonstone at all.”

We reached the fork in the road just then. One path led to Mr. Ablewhite’s house. The other ran out towards a remote moorland village two or three miles distant. Jennings stopped at the second path.
“This is my way. I truly am sorry I cannot be of more help to you.” He bowed, turned, and started walking towards the moor.

I stood there for a full minute, watching him get further and further away, the only possible clue that could clear my name vanishing into the distance behind him. What was I doing, letting my stupid pride keep me from stopping him? I called his name before I could overthink it.
He turned right around and walked back to me.
“I was not fair to you just now,” I said. “My interest in that lost memory has nothing to do with the Moonstone. I am the prime suspect in the theft of the Diamond, and it is the most humiliating, painful thing I have ever had to say out loud. I cannot keep lying to you. If you help me, I will swear to keep whatever you tell me entirely secret.”

A terrible, raw emotion seized Jennings the second the words left my mouth. His gipsy-dark face bleached to a livid, greyish pallor, his soft brown eyes blazed wild and glittering, and his voice dropped low, sharp, and rock steady, a note I had never heard from him before.
“Before you place any trust in me at all, there is one small thing you need to know, the short story of why I even have a roof over my head under Mr. Candy’s roof. I never tell anyone my full story. It will die with me. But you have the right to know exactly who you are sharing your secret with. Shall we walk a little?”
We had gone a few hundred yards further along the road before he stopped at a gap in the rough dry-stone wall that separated the open moor from the highway.
“Would you mind stopping to rest for a moment, Mr. Blake?” he asked. “I am not half as steady as other men. Some things still shake me right down to my bones.”

---
On the 27th of June last year, I received direct orders from Sergeant Cuff to tail three men: suspects wanted for questioning over a potential murder, described as ethnic Indian. Tips had placed them on Tower Wharf that same morning, boarding a Rotterdam-bound steamer.
I caught a rival line’s steamer out of London the very next morning, Thursday the 28th, and tracked down the captain of the earlier Rotterdam vessel the moment I docked. He confirmed the three men had been passengers, but only for the run as far as Gravesend. Off that port, their apparent leader had asked what time they would reach Calais, and flew into what the captain described as extreme, unfeigned shock when told the ship was sailing straight for the Netherlands, not France. He and his two friends begged the captain to put them ashore, offering to forfeit their full passage fare rather than be carried off course. The captain, taking pity on three clearly lost foreign travellers, signalled a shore boat to pick them up, and the three men left the ship at Gravesend.
It was obvious to me the entire “wrong boat” ruse had been planned from the start to throw any pursuers off their trail. I turned back for England at once. I got off the steamer at Gravesend, traced the trio straight back to London, then followed their trail onwards to Plymouth. Inquiries at the Plymouth docks confirmed they had sailed 48 hours earlier aboard the *Bewley Castle*, an East Indiaman making a direct run to Bombay.
The second Sergeant Cuff got that news, he arranged an urgent overland telegraph to the Bombay port authorities, ordering them to board the *Bewley Castle* the moment it dropped anchor, before any passengers could disembark. That was the end of my official part in the case. I have heard no further updates on the matter since.

---
Sergeant Cuff has asked me to write down the full facts about three men, believed to be high-caste Hindoos, who sailed as passengers on my command, the direct run from Plymouth to Bombay last summer.
The three Hindoos joined my ship at Plymouth. For almost the entire voyage out, there was no complaint against their conduct. They were berthed in the forward quarters, and I barely crossed paths with them personally.
In the final stretch of the journey, we were struck dead calm for three full days and nights, drifting off the Indian coast. I do not have the ship’s log handy to pull the exact latitude and longitude, but the currents pushed us steadily in towards land, and when the wind finally picked up again we made port in Bombay in 24 hours flat. As any seaman will tell you, discipline on a ship always slackens in a long, unbearable calm. My crew and passengers were all worn thin by the swelter. Several of the paying passengers got the small dinghies lowered to row around, and swim after sunset when the air cooled enough, and no one on duty bothered to hoist the boats back to their stowed positions afterwards — they were simply left moored to the ship’s hull. No officers or men had the heart for strict routine while the heat and the frustrating lack of wind dragged on.
Nothing unusual was spotted or heard by the night watch on the third calm night. When dawn broke, the smallest of the dinghies was missing — and the three Hindoos were reported missing shortly afterwards. I have no doubt they stole the boat shortly after full dark, and with eight or more hours of head start on the perfectly calm water, chasing them was pointless by the time we noticed they were gone. Even if they were clumsy oarsmen, they would have made landfall well before sunrise.
It was only when I docked at Bombay that I learned the full reason for their escape. The port authorities blamed me for letting the ship’s discipline slip, as was their right. I offered my full apologies both to them and to my ship’s owners for the oversight. I have not heard a single word of the three Hindoos since the ship docked. I have no further facts to add.

---
My dear Bruff, do you recall that ragged half-savage adventurer you sat next to at a London dinner party in the autumn of 1848? The one you had that long post-dinner chat with, about the cursed Indian gem called the Moonstone and the secret conspiracy to steal it back from English hands? That was me.
Since that night, I have wandered most of Central Asia, then drifted back to the old northwest Indian territories where I cut my teeth as a young explorer. A fortnight ago, I found myself in Kattiawar: a little-known, wild province where even the farmers plough their fields with loaded muskets slung over their shoulders, and the entire population is fanatically devoted to the old, pre-colonial Hindu faith. The few scattered Muslim families in the interior villages dare not touch meat of any kind — anyone even suspected of killing a sacred cow is put to death without mercy by their pious neighbours. This remote region holds two of the holiest pilgrimage sites in the Hindu faith: Dwarka, birthplace of Krishna, and the ruins of Somnauth, the great sacred city sacked and destroyed by the conqueror Mahmoud of Ghizni all the way back in the 11th century.
I decided I would not leave Kattiawar without seeing the grand, weathered ruins of Somnauth one more time. I was roughly three days’ hard hike on foot from the ruins, and I set out alone. Before long I noticed pairs and trios of other travellers all heading the same direction. Dressed in the saffron robes of a Buddhist pilgrim from a distant northern province, fluent in the local language, lean and sun-browned enough that no one guessed at my European origins, I had no trouble passing among them unchallenged.
By the second day, the little groups of pilgrims had swelled to hundreds. By the third, there were thousands all slowly converging on Somnauth. A small favour I did for an injured high-caste traveller that afternoon won me the trust of a group of elite pilgrims, who invited me to join them for a secret night ceremony held on a low hill just outside the ruined city, a ritual honouring the Moon God.
By the time we climbed the hill, the full moon hung high in the cloudless Indian sky. Tens of thousands of pilgrims dressed all in white stretched down the slopes, spilling out over the grassy plain where three rivers met, their banks lined with the crowds, the dark calm ocean glinting far off on the horizon. Red torchlight from cressets dotted the mass of people, glowing against the silver moonlight.
A soft, plaintive strain of flutes and stringed instruments pulled my gaze back to the shrine. Three men stood on the flat rocky platform just before the curtain strung between two giant banyan trees that hid the altar from view. I recognized the central man at once: he was the same Hindu pilgrim I had spoken to on Lady Verinder’s terrace the night the Moonstone vanished from her bedroom. His two companions that night stood at his sides.
A pilgrim next to me saw me start, and whispered that the three men were Brahmins who had voluntarily renounced their caste to serve the Moon God, tasked with bringing his stolen sacred gem home. That night, their penance was complete. They were to part ways immediately, each taking a separate pilgrimage route across the whole of India, and they would never look on each other’s faces again, never rest, until the day they died.
The music cut off. The three men prostrated themselves on the rock before the curtain, stood, embraced one another, then walked apart into the crowd. The mass of pilgrims parted in dead silence to let them pass, and slowly closed up again behind them, erasing all trace of their path. We did not see them again.
A new, jubilant burst of music blared from behind the curtain. The crowd shuddered and pressed closer together. The curtain was drawn aside, and the shrine stood revealed.
There, carved high on his throne, seated on his sacred antelope mount, four arms stretching out to the four corners of the world, was the dark, awe-inspiring statue of the Moon God. Set perfectly in his carved forehead, glowing bright under the moonlight, was the yellow diamond I last saw glinting from the neckline of Rachel Verinder’s dress in a English drawing room, years before.
After eight long centuries, the Moonstone has returned at last to the sacred city where its story first began. Whatever accident or crime allowed the three pilgrims to slip away with the gem and bring it home to its native land, you may know the full details — I do not. You lost track of it in England, and you will never lay eyes on it again.
The cycles of the world turn, the same stories repeat, and no man living can say what new adventures wait for the Moonstone in the years ahead.
